CHAPTER 1: COCKTAILS 

(Thursday evening, September 1st.)

HERE was a cheerful, bubbling atmosphere in the smoking room of the Magpie Club. Youth was the key-note of the gay little party that had taken possession of it: youth, sufficiently developed to have intelligence and yet care-free enough to squeeze every ounce of spontaneous joy out of an occasion such as this. 

Seward was to be married to-morrow. Bill Seward, the brilliant novelist, playwright and movie writer. 

Seward, the prince of friends, who had shot up into fame like a scintillating meteor in his twenty-fourth year by writing a book which instantly took England and America by storm; and in the next three years had consolidated his position as a fine imaginative writer. 

But Seward wasn't a high-brow. If he worked in the clouds, he came down to earth as soon as he rubbed shoulders with humanity, and the effects of fame trickled away from him like water from a house-top when he mingled with his fellow mortals. 

Tony Parke raised a finger to a passing waiter.

Eight men, hell-bent for mirth on such an occasion of

carnival, needed a prelude of cocktails that were forming a procession. And a moment later Tony glanced at the clock. Dinner here at the club had been staged for "about eight," and they had no trains to catch; but punctuality never had been Seward's strongest point. 

That was why Tony Parke's attention wandered to the clock hands. Besides, Seward was in what he called the "straight for home" with a book he had vowed to finish before he left on his honeymoon, and, at the moment, maybe, time didn't exist in his brain.

A shout of laughter arose. The little group in the smoking room was tuning up. It would be a great night.

Benson, with his back up against the mantel shelf, was telling a funny story, and the faces circled about him were alight. 

Nigel Benson, quick tempered, but with a hundred sides to his character, had the gift of holding attention. 

He was a born actor and would no doubt have developed into a matinée idol had he taken to the stage instead of being a barrister. Unlike the weakling chameleon type who change as they step from one atmosphere to another, Benson generally transmuted the atmosphere about him by sheer force of his powerful personality. 

When he was serious men caught the spirit of his mood: if he was gay they laughed. Never had his gaiety been more infectious than now. Benson, and one or two of the others, had only arrived at the club a few moments before, but everyone had already settled comfortably. 

Someone ought to look Seward up. His flat was about three minutes' walk away. In case-just in case—he'd momentarily tossed all thought of this festive gather-

COCKTAILS 

3 ing to the winds in the throes of writing, it might be as well to drag him out.

With that object in view, Parke uncurled himself from a deep lounge chair, stood listening and idly tapping the ash from his cigarette, then touched the man nearest to him on the elbow. "Eltree, I'm off to dig Seward out. See these bright lads don't die of thirst meanwhile."

Pale, dark haired, with black eyes that carried in their depths an eternal hint of melancholy, Gerrard

Eltree emerged from a sort of dream. "Seward?" came from him quickly. "Oh, yes, of course." His eyes, too, went to the big grandfather clock in the corner. "What I mean is," Parke added, "they're all nonmembers here except you and me. See the little dears get their firewater every five minutes or so, old son." "I will. Leave that to me," replied Eltree, fingering his own already empty glass. He watched the tall 

Tony Parke cross the room-a newspaper man whose alertness of mind was reflected in his step. Tony was the son of Sir John Parke, the eminent K.C. Two years ago he had annoyed the worthy baronet intensely by throwing up a promising career in the city in preference for the fuller and more exciting life of journalism. Now, at twenty-six, Tony Parke was making headway in Fleet Street. He was the "special" man of the Daily Monitor, and latterly had turned his particular attention to crime, partly because it gripped his interest, and partly for the reason that he seemed to have some particular flair in that direction.

Eltree's face was sombre now as Parke disappeared through the door; more pallid than ever in that light, one might have thought, but for two slight patches of colour dawning near the cheek bones. The fruit of these rapid cocktails. He beckoned to a waiter. The young gents were out for a binge, reflected that fleetfooted servitor as he vanished. And were already well off the mark. High steppers, every one of them. But the right sort. Pals of Mr. Seward. He was to be married to-morrow. No wonder the little crowd were getting away from their apron strings on a night like this.

CHAPTER II:  

A (8.22 p. m., Thursday, September 1st.) 

T the door of Seward's flat Tony Parke hummed a revue air as he fished in a pocket for his bunch of keys. He was in a particularly cheerful mood; Tony generally was light hearted, though, to Sir John Parke's surprise, he took his newspaper work intensely seriously. Sir John, perhaps with one eye on the wild oats that he sowed in his own younger days, had at first feared his son might only philander with Bohemianism in Fleet Street. And now 

Anthony's father had begun to chuckle to himself at times. The lad had brains. Gad, he couldn't help that, naturally, considering the stock that he sprang from, but he was using them. He was carving out a career for himself. 

Tony's grey eyes were dancing: keen intelligent eyes that were shrewd without harshness. He was effervescing with the sheer animal spirits of the healthy young male who is in perfect physical trim and knows no care.

A key slipped into the Yale lock, not quite freely perhaps, but he pushed it home with a jerk on the note he was humming, then stepped on to the thick pile carpet in Seward's hall. Automatically he pushed 5.

the outer door until the latch clicked behind him, and strode over the big white bearskin to the half open door of the author's library. And then, as he entered the room, the note he was humming broke suddenly off. "Oh, my God!"

In the fading daylight his glance had fallen first on Seward, standing by the old oak desk in the centre of the library. His hair was dishevelled. One hand was resting on the desk for support. The other was pressed to his forehead. Mute horror burned in his eyes as they were raised for a moment to Parke, then fell again to that which lay still and sprawling-significantly still -on the floor. A man, face downward, hands convulsively clutched, with the handle of a kris—a Malay knife with an ugly blade of waving steel sharpened at both edges-protruding from his back. 

Apart from that thing which seemed to be mesmerising Bill Seward, other objects in the room drew Parke's eyes. The drawers in the desk had been pulled out and their contents scattered, a poker lying amongst them. A tall Sèvres vase that usually stood there with flowers in it, and a flat square-faced clock, had been swept to the floor. The vase lay scattered in a hundred pieces. The door of a cabinet in the corner near the fireplace stood open and its contents had been ruthlessly jumbled. 

In the flat overhead someone was playing "Ain't she sweet?" on the piano, with halting repetition and a heavy, unpractised touch. There were false notes which hammered their way down into this room of discord.

"What-what's happened?" came brokenly from

Parke, usually emotionless and cold in a crisis. "I don't know-yet." There was something dead in 

Bill Seward as he stood there, listless. That indefinable magnetic quality about him, which in some way he managed to impart to everything he wrote, had for the moment gone. "When did it happen?" "I don't know.” 

Parke's tongue slid along his lips, which had gone dry. Grotesquely the fact became apparent to him that 

Seward had not even changed for dinner yet. And, waiting across there at the club were—. "Steady, old lad." He switched the electric light on, took a step forward and touched his friend's arm. "Think! You must know!" 

Seward looked up into the eyes of his friend. Eyes that were always less alert, more pregnant with imagination than those of his friend; but they held depths which for the moment were strangely stirred. About his sensitive mouth was a queer twisted smile which began and ended there. The strong face was drawn and haggard. "I see," he said slowly, in a monotone. "So you think that already! Even you!" 

Parke started. "Good Lord! No, I don't mean-. Of course I don't. 

Pull yourself together, man." "Exactly what do you mean, then?" "Why, it hadn't entered my head, until you sug-

gested the thing, that there was any possibility ofof-" 

Bill Seward jerked a finger forward. "Of my having done that." "Yes, but, for God's sake, Seward, you know something. Here's a man, obviously murdered, in your own library-" "You know as much about it as I do," the other cut in. "You mean you came in here and found-everything -just like this?” 

Seward nodded. "Exactly," he said coldly. "When?" 

"A minute or so ago." 

Parke leaned forward and crushed his cigarette into a tray on the desk, then looked carefully at his watch. "Let's get this straight, Seward," he said tersely. "It's-it's liable to be devilishly important. My watch says 8.25. I came in at, shall we say, 8.23. Exactly how long had you been in this room when I arrived?”

Bill Seward passed a hand over his forehead. "Oh, I don't know. One doesn't think meticulously of time in the presence of a big thing like this! A minute and a half, perhaps. Not more than two, anyway. I walked out of my study into here and saw this. 

Bent over it, I think. Yes, I bent over it, then darted out into the hall, and saw the front door was closed. 

I had just returned here when you blew in. That's all." "Then we'll fix your time for entering this room at

8.21," said Parke, with decisiveness. "And this fellow? 

Who is he?" 

Again Bill Seward shook his head. "Haven't the foggiest."

Parke knelt down and half turned the dead man's head round—a sight to turn away from quickly. "Never set eyes on him in my life as far as I know," he said, getting to his feet and glancing at the telephone. "We must tell the police at once, but we'd better search the flat first." "Yes, that's true," agreed Seward. "But you don't for a moment expect to find anyone in the place, do you?"

Together they went through every room minutely, then returned to the library. "This has given you a jolt. I'll ring the police up for you if you like," said Parke, picking up the telephone.

Seward abstractedly selected a cigarette from a box on the desk, tapped it on the back of his hand and put it between his lips. "Have you got your facts right?" he asked. "Remember, you'll have to stick to the same story, once you tell it." “Of course,” said Parke. "It's simple enough." 

Seward flicked a lighter with his thumb, and a flame sprang into being. He was sitting on the edge of the desk, apparently absorbed in the flame. "Well, ring 'em up and get it over," he suggested. "But I'm afraid this thing isn't quite so simple as you seem to think.”

"I don't understand. The mystery of how it happened has got to be solved. That's for the police to tackle. But the facts themselves, so far, are clear. A man found murdered in your flat. The murderer has got away. There can be no question about that." 

Bill Seward spun round and faced Parke; and for the first time, as he spoke, the dead thing in him had come back to life. "Damn it, man, can't you see the position? In order to commit murder here, someone had to get into the flat." 

There was tense silence. Even "Ain't She Sweet?" overhead had ceased. Something tingling was creeping into the atmosphere of Bill Seward's library. "Well?" sharply from Parke after that weighted pause. "It isn't well at all," replied Seward. "And that's what bothers me. Besides the maid, only two living souls have a latch-key which will open that front door. 

I have one-the other is yours. How this poor devil got killed I don't know. Logically the thing's impossible.

A man can't stab himself in the back. Besides even if he could, how did he get in?" "Well, the police will soon settle that question." "All right. Ring them up quick, and let the band start playing. It'll get on my nerves if we don't do something soon. But remember we calculated, as nearly as we could, that I came into this room at 8.21 tonight and you at 8.23. They may accept our word on the subject. Most sincerely I trust they do, but if they 'don't we're up against it. Write those times down, be-

cause we mustn't make a single slip, and my hand's none too steady. Now call in the sleuth hounds." 

Parke's brow was puckered as he looked at his companion. This had put a new angle into the situation. He took off the receiver and raised it to his ear. 

Seward again struck a light and applied the flame to his cigarette. The amateur musician overhead had begun pounding again. 

Ain't she sweet? 

See her coming down the street! 

Now I ask you very confidentially, Ain't she sweet? 

Into this Parke's voice cut metallically. "Give me Scotland Yard, at once, please." And then, a few moments later, "Is that Scotland Yard? . . .

Send someone up here immediately, will you, and the police surgeon too, please. Flat number thirteen, Regent House, Grasmere Street, Mayfair. Got that? 

Yes. A man has been murdered." 

He turned to his friend. "Now listen, Seward." His back was toward the still object on the floor. There was a note of dawning cheerfulness in his voice. "You know perfectly well that neither of us can be blamed in the slightest degree for this rotten business. For one moment, just now, the way you put it made me feel rather like a trapped rat. Actually it gave me a bit of a shock. For thirty seconds or so I lost the rational viewpoint. That's all. There isn't the slightest danger of you or of me being suspected."

Bill Seward blew a cloud of smoke ceilingward and watched its course. "I'm glad you think so, old lad. But it's a delusion; and we might as well face the fact. You wait and see what these police johnnies with their unimaginative legal minds say when we tell them the truth." "The truth! About what?" 

Seward hesitated for a moment, then: "I say, Parke, if I say something I hope you won't misunderstand it. When I spoke of 'the truth' I was referring to those latch-keys, of course. Now don't get hot under the collar if I point out that there isn't the remotest reason why we should both be dragged into this. It's utterly useless, and you'd find it darned annoying, to say the least. Hold your horses don't get shirty!" "I assure you I'm not in the least shirty, but-" "I did try," Seward interrupted, "to make it clear that I only wanted to save you considerable irritation, and I do know you're not the type to trample your way ruthlessly over the bodies of women and children to save your own skin when the ship was sinking. 

You're even the valiant type of ass who would stay on board until it was too late, and derive immense satisfaction from drowning your silly self with the asinine reflection that the Parkes always did a thing like that instinctively. Only in this case it isn't a case of saving our skins." "That's precisely what I have already indicated." "It never could get as far as that, of course," Seward went on, "but until they get on the right track

of the murderer there's going to be hell to pay. I've thought it all out. It's my job to think things out. 

Maybe you haven't yet pictured the topography of this flat and the whole infernal situation as I have." "Why not?" "Tony, you obtuse lunatic, I don't believe you realise that for a while those latch-keys are going to be both the hors d'œuvres and the pièce de resistance of this mess. Anyone could get out of the flat, but it's the fellow who could get in that their fingers will be aching for. Of course if you insist on being heroic-" He gestured slightly with one hand. 

While Tony Parke had heard the other out patiently, his eyes had glittered for one second only. His cleanshaven face was immobile now. The tinge of humour that usually lurked about the corners of his mouth had momentarily vanished. "You're a stout fellow, Seward," he said, "And I get your point, naturally. But here's a splendid instance of the danger of not following rigidly in the foot-steps of George Washington on an occasion of this sort. To wit: I withhold the fact that I have a key. Next day your maid, or any one of half a dozen people who know I habitually use a key, mentions this supremely interesting fact. My lad, do you know what would happen? They would have me down at Vine Street in ten minutes and be taking my finger-prints to see if

I'd ever done any other dirty work besides a murder at these particular cross-roads. No, Seward, it's a generous thought, but it wouldn't fit in."

"Maybe you're right. I don't seem to have all my wits about me-sort of fussed up." "One thing's dead sure, Seward. We've got to hang on to facts with our eyebrows. Besides, I've a cast iron alibi." 

Seward brightened. "That's something, anyway. I'm glad. What is it?" "There are seven perfectly good witnesses, not to speak of waiters, who would leap at the chance of declaring I've just come over from the club. In fact 

I noticed the time particularly when I left there.” 

Bill Seward nodded before answering. "But-forgive me, I'm only trying to look for possible flaws-did any of the others also take particular notice of the time?" 

Parke bit his lip. "Wait a moment, I've remembered something else.

Yes, Eltree looked at the clock when I told him I was coming to dig you out. He would remember the time.

But it was only about three minutes past eight then.' 

Seward frowned quickly. 

"Some mistake there. According to our reckoning you didn't enter this room until 8:23. That is to say, twenty minutes later. And it's only a few minutes' walk." "Precisely," replied Parke quietly. "As a matter of fact I had forgotten that on my way here-this business had driven it out of my head-I ran up against a man I hadn't seen for years and we stood chatting a while."

"Well, that's an alibi," said Seward. 

Parke's eyes narrowed reflectively. "Seward, old son, aren't you taking rather too serious a view of this thing? You don't surely believe that the police would imagine either you or I-” "Tony, God knows I don't want to put the wind up you, but can't you see, won't you try to get it into your head, that the thing is serious. Murder is one thing when you read about it, with all the frills, in the newspaper. You're detached, then. You're aloof.

You've paid a penny for the privilege of being entertained or thrilled, or whatever you care to call it. Even when you personally write about a crime for your paper it's the same thing in effect. It doesn't actually touch you." "I suppose it doesn't." "Of course not. One murder is as good, or as bad, as another in your eyes, so long as it makes a first-class news story. But when it occurs on your own doorstep, or, a thousand times worse, in a flat where you have one of the only three existing keys, it's different. Very different." "Where is Findon?" Tony queried with sudden interest. "I imagine," Seward replied, "that we can dismiss from our minds any connection between this business and Mary Findon. It's her afternoon and evening out. 

She's cloistered somewhere in the sanctuary of her own kind in Peckham or wherever she lives, or having her hair curled, or enjoying love's young dream in a cinema.

As a matter of fact she's rather a nice girl-not a bit the sort to stab people in the back during her day's routine.” "No," agreed Tony, "we're safe in ruling her out." "And in any case," said Seward, "she'll easily prove she was miles away. That leaves just the two keys. 

This thing will all be cleared up, without a doubt, but you can take it as a stone cold truth from me that if either you or I got it into our heads to take a trip to Paris or China before the matter is more explicable than it remains at present, we shouldn't get far." 

Parke fingered the tip of his ear pensively. "Maybe it's a trifle awkward," he said, "but the fact is I haven't the vaguest recollection of that fellow's name the one I chatted with on the way here. It was a five or ten minute talk with one of those casual acquaintances we all have." "But if necessary could you put your hand on him again to prove you weren't in here during that interval?" "I couldn't possibly." 

Seward snapped his fingers. "And thereby," he observed, "bung goes your beautiful alibi. The clouds are closing in upon us."

Parke's eyes went to a corner of the room near the fireplace, and the ghost of a smile dawned on his face. "Seward, I tell you we're making mountains out of molehills. Look at that! Obviously there's been a burglar here. That cabinet's half empty." "That reminds me," observed Seward, glancing round thoughtfully, "I don't seem to have my gold

cigarette case. No, now I come to think of it, I left it in here." He surveyed the litter of papers and the top of the desk. "It's gone!" "Well, that proves burglars. The clouds are rapidly dispersing." 

Bill Seward quizzically surveyed his friend. "Good! Fine!" he said. "You put that up to your friends the police johnnies, in case they don't think of it themselves. In fact you might make it two burglars, while you're about it, for the sake of verisimilitude.

They had a quarrel over the plunder and one of them (for the sake of a gold cigarette case and two or three bits of silver things, mind you) snatched my Malay kris from the wall-my kris observe-stabbed his confrère to the heart, and cleared out. And when you're telling these sleuths the story, watch the cold and fishy eyes they will turn on you. Or on me. We don't know yet which way the cat will jump. You see, Tony, I don't profess to be a highly skilled criminal, but if I had actually murdered this poor fellow it wouldn't have been very difficult to make the place look as though there had been a burglary. Would it?” 

The bell of the front door tinkled. "Here they come," said Parke. "Now for it." 

Together they answered the ring.

CHAPTER III:  

T (8.50 p.m., Thursday, September 1st.)

HERE were three men on the threshold. "You 'phoned us at Scotland Yard?" queried the foremost. There was nothing brusque about his manner. Rather he suggested a family practitioner. "I'm glad you've come," said Bill, stepping aside. "You're the police surgeon?" "No," was the answer. "I'm Detective Inspector

Neville. This," he indicated one of his companions who was pronouncedly of the army officer type, "is 

Dr. Mappin. Come in, Sergeant," he added to the third, at the same time nodding in friendly fashion to Anthony Parke, whom he had met previously on more than one occasion in connection with Scotland Yard investigations. "Now, sir, there's been a murder, you said." "There has, unfortunately. My name is William 

Cayley Seward. I live here. Will you come this way, please?"

He led them across the hall to the library. None of them spoke again until they stood before the still object on the floor. Then a subtle difference crept into the personality of Inspector Neville. This was his 18

métier: he was on familiar ground. It seemed that he barely glanced at the body, but his steady survey of the room that followed was suggestive of a film camera recording every detail. The doctor was already on his knees examining the victim. Placing two gloved fingers under the ridge on the kris where the stout bone handle joined the blade, he drew the weapon out, not having touched the handle itself, and placed it on the desk. 

The Inspector watched this operation critically. "Has either of you gentlemen any idea how this happened?" he asked, looking rapidly in turn at Seward and Parke. "No. Neither of us," replied Seward. "Nothing has been disturbed since you came in here, I hope?" "Nothing whatever." "What can you tell me about the affair?" "Really I know very little."

The police surgeon straightened his back and looked up from the body, puzzled. "Extraordinary!" he exclaimed. "Most extraordinary! Look here, Inspector." He pointed to the dead man's face. It was livid and the expression was one for doctors or the police alone to dwell upon. "He's been both stabbed and strangled. 'Pon my word, most extraordinary! Not been dead long, either. About an hour or so." 

The Inspector glanced at his watch. "Shall we say near about eight o'clock, then?" "Between a quarter to eight and a quarter past," replied the medical officer.

"Do you know the deceased?" The question came like a bullet at Seward. Under those bushy brows of the Scotland Yard man two eyes were doing their utmost to bore into the heart and soul of the author. "No," answered Seward quietly. "I have never seen him before in my life." "And you?" This time the question was hurled at Parke. 

"I haven't the least idea who he is.' "You've not even seen him before?" "I'm quite certain I haven't." "Sergeant, sit down over there, with your note-book. 

And, Doctor, providing you can spare the time, I'd be grateful if you'd stay a little longer. Was that knife wound enough to cause death?" "Death, yes," agreed the medical officer, "but I should say not instantaneously. It just missed the shoulder blade and must have pierced the lung. If it had been the left side he would have died quickly, because the hæmorrhage would have affected the heart. 

But this was the right lung. A slower death." "An important point, Doctor. You probably see what 

I'm after. How long do you think he would have survived without the strangulation?" "He might have had a little-I say a little-strength for a few minutes, and actually lived long enough to make some sort of statement. It is difficult to be more exact before the autopsy." "Umph!" The Inspector's eyes wandered in their inquisitive way to various objects in the room.

"Wounded as the man was, he couldn't have put up much of a fight?" "I doubt whether he would have the stamina to stand erect. Not much more, anyway." "An odd case, whichever way you look at it, Doctor. 

Interesting, though! Very interesting! Several things about it seem obvious-and then you see they aren't quite obvious. The finger-prints may help a great deal. 

One thing's apparently clear. The man who did this was very definite about his intention at the moment. 

I can't see why yet, but he meant to kill, and nothing short of that. Now, your statements, please, gentlemen. 

I'll take you first, Mr. Seward."

Bill Seward faithfully began to relate the facts as he knew them from the moment he had first seen the deceased. "By the way," he observed parenthetically, "there's a dinner party waiting for me-for both of us—at my club. If it won't interrupt you, I'll telephone from here to explain that there's been an accident." "By all means," agreed the Inspector, "after I've finished taking your statement." "You ring them up, then, Tony. Better ask for Eltree. Tell him to explain how frightfully sorry I am. 

You know how to put it."

Tony put in a call to the Magpie Club and for a few moments he spoke in a low, restrained voice. "No, no, Eltree," he went on, raising his tone a shade. "Not Seward. He's all right, thank goodness.

Some man we don't know anything about yet. A bur-

glar, evidently. But we're absolutely tied up here for the present. Make the other fellows understand that."

As he hung up the receiver the Inspector beckoned to him. "Now you, Mr. Parke. You're not in this flat, I take it, just in the capacity of a newspaper representative?" "Dear me, no. Mr. Seward and I are old friends.

Very old friends. In fact we were at Repton together." 

Neville eyed the man before him soothingly. "And you let yourself in here? Where did you get the key?" "From my pocket. I always carry it, with the rest on this bunch." "Exactly. And will you explain how you come to have the key of another man's flat? In itself that, surely, is a little unusual." "It may be, but you must understand that we're very intimate friends. I have had the key for a long time. You see, when Mr. Seward is in what he calls his 'sound-proof chamber' and the maid is out, he couldn't hear the front door bell ring. Even if it were urgent one couldn't get him at all. Several times that proved awkward when I called. He could hear the jangling of the telephone bell slightly, but if he were deeply immersed in his work he would ignore that. He wouldn't want to hear it. So we found it more convenient for me to carry a key.” "Very well." The Inspector was standing with his back to the flood of light. His eyes-those kindly blue eyes which together with his bedside manner had led

a host of criminals "up the garden" as they would have expressed it-almost had a beneficent expression. 

Abruptly he turned to Detective Sergeant Trim. "Get the Yard on the telephone. Tell Scarlett to run up at once with his camera for fingerprints. I'm ready for the rest of your statement, Mr. Parke."

Having heard a second story, the Inspector spent a full minute gazing frowningly round the room again. "That doesn't carry us much further," he said. "Doctor, I'm much obliged to you for staying. As far as I'm concerned I don't think I need detain you any longer." "Good night, Inspector," replied the police surgeon. “A puzzle, eh? I'd be interested to-morrow to hear what the explanation is."

A velvety veil covered the touch of annoyance in 

Inspector Neville's manner. The Criminal Investiga tion Department had not yet been able to utilize miracles in achieving results. "To-morrow?" His eyes followed the doctor. "Yes,

I hope to know all about this by then." Which was indeed true. One may hope for many things. "Now let me get one or two facts straight," he added, addressing Bill Seward. "You say you were writing in your study from five o'clock this afternoon until 8:21." "That is so. I never stopped for a moment. I was finishing the last chapter of a book, the MS of which I wished to send to the publisher before I leave on my honeymoon." "Were you writing with a pen?" The seemingly irrelevant question came smoothly.

"No. I always use a typewriter." "Then you would be less likely to hear any sounds in this room because of the machine clicking?” "It wouldn't make much difference," replied Seward. "The one thing an author likes to make sure of before he can do good work is silence. The windows in my study were closed because some children were playing in one of the gardens at the back, and this door which leads into the study," he pointed across the room, "is padded with baize to make it as soundproof as possible. Also there is a heavy velvet curtain which was pulled across, inside.” 

The officer pushed the door open and glanced at these fittings. "No," he agreed, "I don't suppose you could hear much in there." "With the curtain across," the author explained, "I can barely hear the telephone bell when I am working. 

That, as a matter of fact, was my chief object in taking those precautions. Also one doesn't want to be disturbed by other things, such as a maid using a carpet sweeper. Someone was playing a piano atrociously in the flat above, as they are doing now, and the sound bothered me, but that is one of the penalties of living in a flat." "You were sitting over there by the typewriter, I take it?"

The chair was standing a little on one side just as Seward had pushed it away on rising from his work. 

Several sheets of typescript lay on the table by the

side of the machine, together with an ash tray in which a mass of cigarette ends was piled. "I sat there all the time," said Seward. "I never left the chair. In that stage of writing a book, you must understand-the last few pages especially-one is intensely engrossed, and almost loses the idea of such things as time and one's actual environment. I was even considerably late for dinner when I wrote the last word of the book and sort of blundered into here." "Blundered?” "Well, yes. I was a trifle dazed after such a prolonged effort of concentration in a stuffy room. The window in front of me was still closed, you see. Moreover, it was getting dark, and I had been writing in there with a strong light."

The Inspector drummed his fingers on the back of a chair. "How did the man-or the men-get into the place?" he asked. "That's beyond me," Seward answered. "Come and look round. Every window in the other rooms was fastened. I particularly told the maid to see to that before she went out. As it was her day off, I knew the place would be empty all night till she comes back at about ten."

The C. I. D. man's eyebrows were raised fractionally. "We'll see," came from him as, with one last close scrutiny of the study, he turned back into the library. "It was getting dusk then, eh,—about eight o'clock?

We'll examine the windows facing on to Grasmere 

Street first." 

Detective Inspector Neville spent the next quarter of an hour making a slow minute survey of the premises. It was a modern flat, on the third floor, and it was exquisitely furnished, for Bill Seward had above all things an eye for beauty. Two doors led from the vestibule into the only two rooms which faced on to the quiet Grasmere Street. One Bill Seward used as his own bedroom. The other was always in readiness for a possible guest. Apart from the bathroom, lavatory and kitchen, there were only three other roomsthe dining room, the library and the study with a padded door behind which Seward habitually sought silence and seclusion for his work. 

Systematically, and without a word to those accompanying him, the Inspector went through each room and examined the windows. The catch had been securely fastened in each case, and had not been tampered with in any way. Moreover, even if one of the windows had been open, ingress there would have been extremely difficult if not impossible without the aid of a long ladder from the street or a rope from some window above. But the windows were all securely fastened. "H'm-h'm!" exclaimed the Inspector at length, with a touch of petulance. "I don't see how anything could have got into the place except by your front door. Maybe there's a loophole somewhere, but we haven't found it yet. Are you sure the front door latch was not fastened back, Mr. Seward?".

Seward stood with both hands in his pockets, facing the C. I. D. official. "It so happens," he said, "that I am absolutely sure of it. There can be no question on that point. None whatever. One has many friends who are liable to drop in at any old time, and this evening I was determined not to be interrupted. And so before I shut myself up in the study I made sure nobody could stroll in without a key." "And afterwards you didn't touch the latch? You didn't go out for a breath of fresh air or post a letter or anything?" "Definitely, I saw that the latch was secure and went straight to my study, and never left it for any purpose until 8:21, when I went into the library and found this man lying dead there. I hope that is quite clear." "Clear?" The Inspector cupped his chin between fingers and thumb of one hand and rasped the day's growth of beard. "Your statement is a marvel of clarity," he answered testily. "One might almost wish it were less definite, and then perhaps we could begin to get one degree nearer the daylight. Possibly someone was hiding in the flat before you made sure the latch was secure?"

Seward leaned back and cast his eyes at the ceiling, thinking deeply for a space before replying. "Honestly, I don't see how that could have been," he said at last. “You see how small the place is. There's nowhere to hide, if you come to think of it. Certainly not under one of the beds. They've both got deep box

mattresses and it would be a tight squeeze even for a child to crawl underneath." "I observed that," remarked the detective. "The maid," Seward went on, "cleaned every room out thoroughly this morning, in view of the fact that the flat is going to be shut up for a while. Besides, I haven't been out all the afternoon. And the door has been latched, as usual, throughout the day."

The Inspector ran his fingers through his hair. "Well, that looks watertight," he said, "but I can't say that it helps." There came a ring on the front door bell. "Hello, what's that? Probably our camera man," he added, glancing at his watch. "Ah, yes, Scarlett, we want you here. Decidedly we want you. Fingerprints, my lad. And take those of the dead man too, while you're about it. Then, Sergeant, you can call the ambulance, and send the body down to the mortuary. 

In a way I'd have liked to leave everything exactly as it is for a while, but I don't see how that can be necessary, and I expect you'll be relieved to have the deceased removed, Mr. Seward.” "I certainly shall be relieved," agreed Seward with an ill-concealed shudder. "Very well. Scarlett, your most important job is to get the finger-prints on the knife. There it is lying on the desk." "Finger-prints on the knife!" The words came from Bill Seward in a voice that caused the Inspector to twist round. Seward's hand was stretched out to the

box of cigarettes on the desk. "I'm afraid there's rather a complication there," he said, flicking his lighter into flame. "A complication? In what way?” "When I blundered into this room and saw a dead man lying there," he said without wavering, "the first thing I did was to stoop over him, and I took hold of the kris to pull it out. A perfectly instinctive act, as you must realize." "And why didn't you pull it out?" "The man was so obviously dead. It flashed into my mind that in a situation such as this one should leave everything exactly as it was."

Never had the C. I. D. official's bearing been more beneficent. It was the velvet veil again. His eyes were even kindly. "A complication indeed! This case doesn't seem to consist of much but complications so far. See what the dead man has in his pockets, Sergeant. We must get him identified as soon as possible."

It was a silent little group that stood watching while the Sergeant carried out his grim duty. He placed the things one by one at his side on the floor as each pocket was emptied-a cheap watch without a chain, seven shillings and fourpence in coins and a ten shilling note, a penknife, a small bent piece of strong wire, and finally one or two old letters. "That's all, sir," said the Sergeant, rising and handing the various objects to Inspector Neville. "Wait a bit, Sergeant," came from the Inspector.

"Have another look. See if there's a key somewhere. 

That's important. Run through all those pockets again in case you've missed it."

The man obeyed, but shook his head. "There's nothing else, sir," he declared with certainty.

The Inspector's attention went to the papers. "Here's something, anyway," he said, picking out an envelope. It was addressed to:- 

Mr. John Anderson, 17 Jeffry Place, Brixton, S.W. "Good God!" broke from Bill Seward as his eyes caught the writing. "This is indeed the ultimate possible complication so far as I am concerned." 

Neville was caressing him with a glance that gave no real indication of all which lay behind those benign blue eyes. "So you do know him, after all, eh?” "Know him? Lord, no! The clouds are indeed closing in. Inspector Neville, before you open that letter

I should like to draw your particular attention to the fact that ever since you entered this flat I have made no attempt to evade facts. I have no experience such as yours, of crime, but it is increasingly apparent that the situation is becoming extremely uncomfortable for me. That is why I want you to remember I have not attempted to mislead you in any degree. I mean by saying the door was unlatched, or that I had found

a window open and since closed it. Now, look at that letter please. It is addressed in my writing."

The Inspector whipped a sheet of notepaper from the envelope. On it was a brief note, which he read aloud. 

DEAR SIR, Flat 13, Regent House, Mayfair, W. 

27th August, 1927.

If your letter is not a jest-the humour of which unfortunately escapes me-it was written under a misapprehension, due to mistaken identity; but

I warn you that the underlying hint in it is strongly suggestive of an attempt to extort blackmail, and if I hear from you again in any way I shall place the matter in the hands of the police. 

Yours adamantly,

W. CAYLEY SEWARD. 

The Inspector's voice ceased, and he stood stroking his chin thoughtfully. "It is, indeed, as you suggest, almost the ultimate possible complication so far as you are concerned, Mr. 

Seward. Just now you mentioned that you were going away on your honeymoon to-morrow." "I was. But that of course is out of the question." "And why is it out of the question now?" came from the Inspector silkily.

"Heavens, officer, what do you think I'm made of? 

What do you think the lady I am engaged to can be made of? Damn it, man, I can feel the suspicion that you're all conscious of. And in a way I don't blame you. If I were in your shoes at this stage I should want to know a great deal more than we do at present before 

I ceased to suspect William Cayley Seward. This letter referring to blackmail! My finger prints on the kris! (By the way, the kris has evidently been snatched off the wall where it always hung there near the door. I bought it in Malay when I was there two years ago.) And then the astounding fact that I was absolutely locked up in the place with the poor wretch who is dead! Would anybody wish to go to the altar with his bride with such an accumulation of evidence hanging over his head?" "For the moment I certainly should put out of my head any idea of leaving London," said the Inspector suavely. "As for suspicion, it is my job to look upon everybody as the potential criminal. In this note to—" he glanced at the envelope again, "-to John Anderson, you speak of a letter received from him. Is that communication now in your possession?" "It is in my study, under the small bronze monkey paper-weight near the typewriter." With a movement of the head the Inspector sent the Sergeant to fetch it. "I kept the thing in case the fellow wrote again.

Had he done so I meant to hand it over to the authorities."

The Sergeant quickly returned to the library with a sheet of paper. The letter ran:

17 Jeffry Place, Brixton, S.W. 

26th August, 1927. 

DEAR "MR. SEWARD," 

Permit me to raise my hat! 

You always fall on your feet, don't you, Bunty! 

I didn't want to spoil your new game, whatever it is, especially as you seem to be playing it so successfully, but after I passed you and the lady in the street I followed you home to make sure of your address-and your name at the moment.

I ceased to be a guest of the government six weeks ago, and am distinctly embarrassed financially. Slim, also, is out-left his country residence on Saturday. Did nearly four years this time, and it doesn't seem to have cramped his style a bit. As you may imagine, though, he has a rod in pickle for both of us. But don't worry, Bunty. I will keep my trap shut. (How one does pick up slang in those abominable institutions!) 

Very likely he will not find you.

May I suggest that a few crumbs from the rich man's table, in negotiable form, would be especially welcome just now? I will call in person for it, if you wish, but naturally understand that you may prefer me to remain in the background. 

Yours ever,

JOHN ANDERSON.

"And why, may I ask," said the Inspector, "did you not hand this interesting document over to the police at once?" 

Bill Seward was silent for a few moments. "Of course," he explained at last, "it is a banality to say now that I most sincerely wish I had done so. 

But who could have foreseen what was going to happen? For one thing, we all shrink intuitively from being mixed up in police court proceedings, especially in a case such as that would have been. A little mud always seems to stick, you know. I really gave the matter very careful thought-read the letter through half a dozen times. Anderson, if that is his real name, very evidently mistook me for some crook whom he had known, perhaps years ago. If one chooses, one may read into it more than a subtle hint of blackmail, but on the other hand it contains no direct threat. 

On the contrary, the man is most conciliatorypromises, you will observe to keep his 'trap' shut, and gives me a friendly warning that one Slim ‘has it in' for me." "And who is Slim?" The question was apparently put casually. "How can I know?" Seward answered. "I never heard of either of them until that letter reached me. 

A wiser and more practical man than myself would probably have taken the precaution of mentioning the matter to the police at once. The man was clearly under the impression that my real name was 'Bunty,' and if he thought so I can see the bare possibility of it being regarded as a very natural begging letter."

"You can?" observed the Inspector, in non-committal tones, fingering his chin and glancing through the letter once more. "By the way," said Seward, "if you will excuse me, I really feel I ought not to delay any longer in speaking on the telephone to my fiancée. The wedding was fixed for two o'clock to-morrow afternoon. You see my position, Inspector?" he added, with the delicate touch of a question in the movement of his head. "Er-yes, telephone to the lady, by all means," said Neville.

Seward picked up the receiver and gave a number. 

It seemed a long time before he spoke again. ... "Ask Miss Merlyn to come to the telephone at once, please. The matter is urgent. Say that Mr. Seward wishes to speak to her." Then, after a brief pause, "That you, Stella? Having a busy time, what? Final preparations. No, listen, dear! I-I hardly know how to tell you, but something rather beastly has happened. I'd have come straight there to explain, but things are in a distinct mess. . . . No, not a fire. It's —it's difficult to explain over the wire. . . . Yes, I'm all right, but there's a man dead here in the flat, and we have some police johnnies in. No . . . forgive me, but please listen! About the wedding. I want you to believe me implicitly when I say it wouldn't be feasible in the circumstances to go off on our honeymoon. It simply couldn't be done. I'm appalled at having to give you such short notice. But if you can manage it somehow or other-the wedding will have to be postponed for a few days. . . . Yes, postponed." Seward's

hand was trembling slightly, and his voice had lost some of its timbre. For ten seconds he was rigid, listening eagerly for some reply that did not come. At last, "Stella, I'm waiting-everybody here is listening—I'm waiting for your answer." A pause. Then, "Stella, you're a brick! A few days only, I promise you. I'll ring you up first thing in the morning or come and see you if possible. Now, don't lose any sleep over this. 

Good night. ... Good night, dearest. . . . Yes, always. More than ever, in fact. Good-bye."

He dabbed the moisture on his forehead with a handkerchief. "And some people decry the modern woman!" he observed in an undertone. "Lord, when it comes to showing fineness-real fineness in a crisis-they've got us whacked!"

He remembered the look in Stella's eyes a few hours since, when he had said good-bye to her for the last time before they were to meet at the altar steps. It was as if some prescience of all this had been upon her. She had clung to him tenderly, the glow in her face tinged with vague sadness. "What is it, dearest?" he had asked, his lips still close to hers. "There won't be any more partings after to-morrow." "Somehow I don't want you to go, Bill," she had answered, "and yet I suppose it is only silliness. Tomorrow we shall see the white cliffs of Dover growing fainter and fainter behind us. And yet, my beloved, I feel to-night that, as the French say, to part, it is to die a little,"

CHAPTER IV:  

F (10.05 p.m., Thursday, September 1st.)

Or some time the photographer had been painstakingly engaged obtaining records of fingerprints, with flash lights, in various parts of the room, on the handle of the kris, the polished surface of the desk, the poker and furniture; first sprinkling a fine powder over a small area, and then blowing it aside so that the residue stood out distinct where any impression had been made by a human hand. "In the circumstances, gentlemen," Inspector Neville said, "you will understand that I want a record of your finger-prints, too." "Obviously," agreed Seward.

Producing his portable apparatus, Scarlett took a print from the hands of both men. "Now, get back to headquarters quick, Scarlett," said the Inspector. "Ring me up here as soon as you can, and let me know if we've had anything against the dead man. 'Anderson' may not be his name in our records. And-er-" he paused just long enough to take out a small enamel snuff box and sniff of its contents, "and anything important about the other prints, of course."

As the front door closed on Scarlett, Neville pulled 37

out his watch, regarded it for a moment, drummed his fingers on its face, then returned it to his pocket. "Is your maid generally home pretty punctually, Mr. Seward?" "Ten o'clock or thereabouts, as a rule, I think," was the reply. "One doesn't like them coming in at all hours, but until now this has been a bachelor establishment, and I haven't been especially fussy about such things. She's never very late, though." "Well, as she was one of the three people who had access to this flat, I want to see her. What's her full name?" "Mary Findon." "Write that down, Sergeant. Mary Findon. And her home address?" "Goodness knows! In Peckham somewhere. Wait a minute. Oh, yes, Riviera Terrace: the name struck me as quaint for Peckham. But I haven't the least notion what number." "How long has she been employed here?" "About two months." "You got her from an agency?" "Yes, Loughs Ltd., in Bond Street. It's a well-known firm." "Her references were satisfactory?" "Well, she herself is quite satisfactory. She was rather unfortunate about references. It seems she had only had one situation in the previous three years, and her mistress died. After that she spent a few weeks at home before coming to me. Her mother brought her here, and spoke highly of the girl."

"She naturally would," commented the Inspector. "That dead employer is an old gag. Still, she may be all right. What is your experience of her character?" "I know nothing against her whatever. She keeps the place very clean, buys anything I need and doesn't bother me. That's all I could ask of her." "Is she honest?" "Really, I haven't counted the spoons and things, but I should say she is, quite." "Have you noticed if she has any men, or a man, hanging around after her?" "No. At least-" he broke off momentarily-"now you speak of it she has a young man. They generally do at that age, don't they?" "How old is Mary Findon?" "About twenty-two. Rather pretty. In fact decidedly pretty for her type." "You've seen this young man of hers?" "Once or twice. Apparently he had walked back here with her. I'm afraid I couldn't give much of a description of him, though, because I didn't pay any particular attention to the man. Findon could tell you about him." 

With a discordant crash, the air, beaten upon a piano, came through the floor above and vibrated in the library: "Ain't she sweet! 

See her coming down the street-"

The Inspector glanced at his watch again.

"Quarter past ten already. Of course you don't know where this girl is?" "She generally goes to see her mother." "Good. If she isn't here soon we'll get on to her trail. I should like a photograph of her if there is one about." 

Seward looked blank. "Well, no. I'm afraid I haven't-" "In her room," suggested the C. I. D. man. "Where does she sleep?" "In the domestic quarters two floors above." "How often does she go out in the evenings?" "When she likes, practically, unless I happen to have any friends in." "And does she go straight up to her bedroom when she returns?" "No. Before she goes out she leaves her bedroom key hanging on a nail in the kitchen here. Then when she comes back she enquires if there's anything I need, makes herself a cup of tea, and goes up to her room."

With a movement of the head Neville signalled the 

Sergeant to accompany Seward, who went upstairs and found what he sought on the woman's dressing table. "This is she," he explained to the detective on returning to the library. "The other woman in the picture must be her mother, though I don't recall the face." "Faces are a weak point in your memory, Mr. Seward, aren't they?" said the Inspector. "You don't

recall that of the girl's young man either, though you've seen him more than once." "I remember them when they interest me in the slightest degree," retorted Seward in a tone which Tony Parke alone recognised as the herald of irritation, and he frowned a quick warning, only to become irritated in turn when the Inspector, with one of his lightning glances, which flickered away again so quickly that it appeared to have missed a chance, caught him in the act. It was against Tony that the next attack was directed. "I should like to go rather more closely into the details of your movements this evening, Mr. Parke. 

You say that it was exactly 8:23 when you let yourself into this flat?" "As near as I can fix it." "And the murder was committed between a quarter to eight and a quarter past eight. Where were you at eight o'clock?” "In the Magpie Club, three minutes' walk away from here, with several friends." "They can vouch for that?" "I'm sure of it. We were having cocktails together in the smoking room before dinner, which dinner, by the way, I never had at all." "What time did you enter the club?" "Long before that hour. Just after seven. I was never alone there for a minute until I came over here. 

There are plenty of people who can corroborate that statement."

"We'll look into that later. Now, what time did you leave the club?" "At about three minutes past eight. I happened to look at the clock as I came out." "And you came straight here?" "Yes, but unfortunately on the way I stopped on the footpath and chatted with a casual acquaintance for five or ten minutes." "His statement might be useful, as corroboration." "Yes, but it so happens that I haven't the remotest idea who he is, except that he is an American whom I knew slightly when I was in New York. I hadn't seen him for years previously. He would have gone straight past me, but I happen to have an extraordinary memory for faces, and I stopped as soon as we looked at one another. His name has gone clean out of my head.

I tried to recall it while we were talking, and I fancied he was in the same difficulty, but neither of us happened to bring the matter up. You can understand that, Inspector. Our acquaintance was so casual. He mentioned that he was leaving England immediately." 

The detective's eyes were half closed as they rested on Tony Parke. "That might prove awkward." 

Parke inclined his head slightly. "I can only give you the facts as I know them." "Very well! Did anybody see you standing there talking?" "Not a soul so far as I am aware. I shook hands with him after a while and came straight here. The rest you know."

"From where you were standing during this conversation could you have seen anyone going out of 

Regent House?” "No. We were in Sherwell Road, within twenty yards of the entrance of the Magpie Club." 

Neville paced to and fro in the library, groping mentally among a mass of confusing facts which he was only, as yet, engaged in the process of tabulating. 

More than once his fingers wandered to the little box in his pocket and he took snuff with an air of relish. 

The telephone rang sharply. With a gesture Seward invited the Inspector to answer it. "Your business probably," he said.

Neville grunted and picked up the receiver, listened, and grunted again. "All right!" his voice rasped. "And what about the dead man?”

Three pairs of eyes in the room watched the C. I. D. officer as his ear drank in the news. "Fine!" he said at last over the wire. "I shan't want you again to-night." 

Turning to Seward he again pulled out his watch. "Twenty-five minutes to eleven," he said crisply. "That girl Mary Findon is very late. I take it that in the ordinary way she would have been home by now?" "It's quite unusual, I believe, for her to be as late as this," Seward agreed. "Good enough," said Neville. "We'll be after her!" and again he picked up the telephone receiver, giving the Scotland Yard number. "That the Yard? I want to speak to Macintosh. . . .

Macintosh? Inspector Neville. Take this down. Mary 

Findon, whose parents live in Riviera Terrace, Peckham. A maid servant, supposed to have gone home today, to see her parents. Put someone on the job at once, and ring me up here, 10437 Grosvenor, the moment you get word. If our man finds her and she's hysterical, or seems to be considerably upset, bring her in at all costs. And, most important, see if she can produce the latch-key of flat 13, Regent House, Grasmere Street,

Mayfair. If she hasn't got it, hold on to her tight, and find out who has it. If you can dig up anything about her movements to-day, that information may be vital. 

Is that all clear? .. Right! Now get a move on!" Neville put the receiver back with an amiable expression and again glanced at his watch. "Maybe we've touched something tangible at last," he said. "And now, Mr. Seward, I'll trouble you to give me a detailed list of everything that has been taken from the flat this evening." "To begin with," said Seward, "there's my cigarette case. It is gold, but apart from that it has a great sentimental value. It was given to me by my fiancée." "Anything engraved on it?” "No. It's square and perfectly plain, but there is a curious dent in it near the fastener. I dropped it yesterday and was going to have that dent taken out." He was over by the cabinet now. "And several silver things, some of them rather old and valuable, have been snatched from here," he went on. "Hurriedly, too, I should say. See, the other things are all in a heap." "H'm-h'm! Looks as if the thief had just grabbed

enough to fill his pockets-or handbag. Can you enumerate the missing articles?" "I think so. Let me see, there was- 

One by one the Sergeant entered in his note-book a description of the various things. "May one ask when you will have news about the finger-prints, Inspector?" Tony Parke asked when that was finished. "We've traced the deceased already," was the answer. "Already! You know something about him, then?" "He's an old offender. Been through our hands several times. Did eighteen months for housebreaking fifteen years ago. Was sentenced at Durham for theft after that, served several years for various other offences-I forget what-and was released not long ago on ticket of leave after doing three years hard labour for burglary. He was from Sydney really, name of 

Peters-a sort of swell mobsman usually known as Australian Pete-but had various aliases." After the 

Inspector's voice ceased, that of Bill Seward broke the silence. "Has there ever been any charge of blackmail against him, Inspector?" 

Neville shook his head. "We have no record of it." 

Again the front door bell rang. “Mary of Peckham, eh?" queried Neville, with a touch of eager interest; but when it proved to be the ambulance he exchanged glances with the Sergeant. A few minutes later all that remained of Australian Pete had gone on its way to the mortuary.

Inspector Neville then stooped over the desk, of which he made a minute examination. "Three of these drawers were locked?" He turned questioningly to Seward. "Yes, the three top ones on the left side. I never bothered to fasten the others." "Australian Pete forgot to bring his jemmy," the

C. I. D. official commented, "or else he didn't anticipate such a chance to use one. Strange how the artist in a man comes out, even among burglars!" He was fitting the poker into the places where it had been used as a lever. "It took a real craftsman to force strong drawers like those with a plain poker of this size. Anyone less expert would have taken most of the day and half broken the desk to pieces. Anything missing from here, Mr. Seward?" "Nothing worth taking," replied Seward. "Only old manuscripts, some of them incomplete. They were only locked up to make sure nobody would interfere with them. Rather a nuisance when anyone starts shuffling up loose sheets of manuscript. By the way, Inspector, about finger-prints. Was anything else of importance discovered?" "Prints like those of a woman,” replied Neville. “In all probability they're the maid's." 

Seward looked straight at Neville as he put his next question. Outwardly he was little more than just politely interested. "And the finger-prints on the kris? You found others, besides mine?"

Neville's survey of Seward, as he held him on a ra-

zor edge by not replying for some moments, was less casual. In those seconds a touch of glitter crept into the detective's eyes. Was it the barest soupçon of conscious triumph? Was it just a gigantic cat playing with a mouse? Was it sheer keenness, the outward sign of which the man could control up to a point and not beyond? All these alternatives flashed into Bill Seward's mind. In some indefinable way as he stood there, a lengthening ash on the end of his cigarette, he grew conscious of the impression that, though a net had closed in upon him, the detective was baffled as yet by torn gashes in the mesh. "That," said Neville at last, "is a matter which I cannot discuss yet. In law, everyone is innocent until he is proved guilty. But from my point of view everyone connected with a case of this kind is guilty until I find I don't want to put my hands-' 

As the telephone bell jarred he broke off and applied his ear to the receiver, at the same time glancing at his watch. “All right, Macintosh," came from him incisively in the still room a while later. “I half expected as much. 

Keep someone on the job near the Riviera Terrace house all night, and let another man relieve him at eight to-morrow morning in case she hasn't turned up by then. I want her, Macintosh. I want her badly.

Maybe she's the missing bit in this jig-saw puzzle I have on my hands.” "It is now eleven-fifteen," he said turning to Seward.

His look gave Bill the feeling of being a mouseallowed a little more rope by the cat. "With your help

I'll have a complete description of that girl ready for circulation." "But Inspector, she's only about five feet four and not by any means an Amazon! You surely don't suspect her of stabbing and strangling a husky fellow like Peters!"

The Inspector's expression was one of faint amusement. "Write it down, Sergeant. Mary Findon, servant, aged 22, employed until the 1st September at Flat 13, Regent House, Grasmere Street, Mayfair. Parents' home, 72, Riviera Terrace, Peckham. Height about five feet four. Now, sir, will you give me fuller details, please?" "Eyes hazel," the Sergeant's pencil travelled over the paper, "brown hair, shingled, even teeth, and slight white scar over the left eyebrow. Smartly dressed." "What was she wearing when she left this flatwhen you last saw her, I mean?" asked Neville. "I'm sorry, Inspector, to be so dreadfully unhelpful, but I really didn't notice. In fact I believe I was in my bedroom when she went out. I only know that, for a maid, she always did appear rather smartly dressed." "Had she any peculiarity?" asked the C. I. D. officer. 

Seward searched his memory. "Well, she had a trick of tilting her head a little on one side attractively when she smiled at you with those lovely eyes of hers," said the novelist, "but I suppose you'd hardly want that sort of thing in an official description."

"I mean did she squint, had she fallen arches, was she knock kneed, or anything like that?" "No, Inspector. I'm afraid my artistic soul would find it so depressing to have a squinting girl with fallen arches constantly in attendance, that I should either have to change her or my job." 

Barely listening to that, apparently, the detective placed a chair in front of the desk, and sat down, as the murdered man might have been sitting when the kris entered his back. As Peters might have thrown his arms out in the sharp agony of the attack, Neville thrust out his, with a wide sweeping motion that would have sent the Sèvres vase, the clock and other things clattering to the floor. He picked up the little square faced clock, still ticking industriously after its adventure, just as it ticked through those terrible moments when a human throat was being inexorably squeezed and squeezed until a soul went to its Maker. He compared its hands with his own watch. "Pretty good time keeper, that," he commented. "Within a minute of being correct." "Yes, it's a good clock," agreed Bill Seward politely.

The Inspector sniffed, contemptuous of the golden trifle. "I'd have been thankful if you'd paid a hundredth part of the price for it," he said. 

Seward looked at him questioningly. "And why?" "Because the darned thing would very likely have stopped when it fell, and in that case we might have

had a clearer notion just when Australian Pete did get that knife in his back."

From his seat at the desk he stared round the room. 

The door leading from the study was almost in front of him. That from the vestibule was directly behind. "Sergeant," he said meditatively, "go into the study, close the door after you, and then come out again, opening the door as softly as possible. Don't thump your feet like that, either. I don't want to hear you, but the door." "Right, sir," said Sergeant Trim, disappearing into the other room. As he re-opened the door the handle made a faint snapping noise and the creaking of hinges could be heard distinctly. "That will do," said the detective. "Mr. Seward, lend me the front door key a moment, please." Seward slipped it from the ring in his pocket and laid it on the desk. "Sergeant, take your boots off and do exactly as I say. Go out of the front door and close it after you. Then come back here as quietly as you can."

While the Sergeant was gone Neville sat still, his back exposed to the door, ears straining. There came the curious unmistakable sound of a key being inserted in a lock, and the slight click of the latch as the door was closed-neither of which, the detective reflected, would have been distinguishable but for the total absence of other sounds, such as the pounding of a typewriter, or that confounded piano overhead. 

On the thick pile carpets the movements of the Sergeant might have been those of a shadow until he stood behind his chief.

"That'll do fine," said Neville. "You can put your boots on again now."

The Sergeant sat down heavily. "That latch-key of yours stuck in the door, sir," he said to Seward as he pulled one regulation boot on. "I couldn't get the thing out again. It's there still." "Yes, that does happen occasionally now," replied 

Seward. "Something went wrong with the lock a day or two ago. I meant to have it repaired, but I've been too much occupied with other things." "Faulty latch, eh?" said the Inspector. "I'd better have a look at that. Now how on earth could a key sticking possibly have any bearing-" He broke off, striding to the outer door. "It'll probably come out again with a little wiggling," suggested Parke; and Seward gave the imprisoned key a slight to and fro motion. "There!" "H'm!" came from the detective, his brows contracted, as he regarded the key and the lock abstractedly. He closed and unfastened the door several times. 

In each case the latch worked without difficulty. For the present he was willing to connect anything peculiar concerning flat number thirteen with the body of a man now lying at the mortuary; but in this instance he saw no obvious, nor even subtle link between the two.

The clang of midnight boomed from the church tower near. "And so Mary Findon does not come back!" muttered Inspector Neville, returning to the library and

picking up his hat. "Where do you intend sleeping tonight, Mr. Seward? Here?" "I should hate to sleep here," replied Seward, with a quick sense of intense relief. It was the first moment for some hours that he felt fairly certain he would not be asked to go to the nearest police station. "You'd better let me put you up, Seward," said 

Parke. "There's a spare room in my flat-Leyham 

Mansions, Manning Square, Inspector." "Very well," Neville turned to go. "Good night, gentlemen. We-we'll leave all the doors wide open for the present.' 

"Leave-these-doors-open?" queried Parke, puzzled. "Dear me, no," observed the Inspector with a queer little smile. “I was thinking of quite a different kind of door. Trap doors, shall we call them?" Then he was gone. "Let's get out of here quickly, old lad,” said Parke to his friend. "It has an atmosphere that I don't like. 

Stifling, somehow. Just pick up what you need for the night and we'll walk across."

Three minutes later as they turned out into the night air Seward touched his friend's arm. "Regardez bien!" he said. Leaning against the railings and smoking, was a man in a soft felt hat, with a large drooping moustache, one hand in his pocket, the other holding the bowl of his pipe. He glanced at them casually as they passed, then detached himself from the railings.

After turning two corners at a leisurely pace Tony Parke glanced over his shoulder. The man in the soft felt hat was keeping pace with them. As Tony and his friend turned into the entrance of Leyham Mansions the shadow again leaned up against the railings, pulled out his pouch and knocked the ashes from his pipe. 

Parke chuckled drily. "I'd offer him a few cigars just to show there's no ill-feeling," he said, "only he might suspect they were explosive or doped." "Don't you believe it," replied Seward, and turned.

Without hesitation he walked back to the detective. "You're from Scotland Yard, aren't you, officer?

Well," he went on without waiting for a reply, pulling out a well-filled cigar case and proffering it, "my name is Seward and we're just going to bed in the second floor flat here. You'll have a dull time waiting-take two or three." "Thank you, sir," said the detective taking four, slipping three into his pocket, biting the end off the other, and striking a match. Then, at the last moment, removing the cigar from between his teeth, he regarded it stolidly, pushed that also into his pocket with the others, and replaced the pipe in his mouth. "It's a suspicious world!" commented the novelist, rejoining his friend. "By the way, old dear, don't mind me. As a crime specialist, you're pretty lax, aren't you?" "You mean the Monitor?" "Well," commented Seward, "here's a chance positively to trim your cap with feathers, isn't it?"

"I've been too fascinated during the last hour or two with watching the noose dangling about our necks to think about my job. Besides, it's different. I mean when the thing becomes personal-when one ceases to be aloof-" "Parke, don't be an ass," snapped Seward, as they sank into chairs in the flat. "Do you imagine our names won't be on everybody's tongue when the evening papers come out to-morrow? There's plenty of time yet for the last edition of the Monitor, and one doesn't blunder upon a murder scoop every day." He walked over to the sideboard. "And now I think a spot-just a spot-is indicated, old lad. Say when."

Parke was already calling the Monitor's editorial number over the wire.

CHAPTER V:  

S (10 a. m., Friday, September 2nd.)

TELLA put down the newspaper again. Three times within half an hour she had read every word printed at the top of the Daily Monitor's front page. Bill had already spoken to her over the telephone and should arrive any minute. 

She sat still, looking out of the window. All the telegrams had gone, dozens of them, handed in by herself as soon as the post office opened that morning. 

Owing to unforeseen circumstances the wedding has been postponed for a few days. 

Stella Merlyn.

There was something incredibly brutal in fate forcing any girl to broadcast such a message among her friends on the very day when she was to have stood before the altar. It had brought tears to her brown eyes-steady, characterful eyes-but she had held her head high. "There's a man dead here in the flat, and we have some police johnnies in." Those words had been ringing in her ears all through a sleepless night. What could they signify? How could it affect Bill so vitally 55

that he had to ask her to postpone the wedding? From midnight to dawn, it seemed, Stella had asked herself these questions, and tortured herself with nameless fears. 'And then, this morning, the boy's dear voice again. "I'm all right, Stella. Try not to worry, darling. I'd have come round after it was over last night, but there were detectives all over the place, and I didn't want to draw their attention to you after midnight. There's some extraordinary mystery about it, but they'll get to the bottom of things to-day or to-morrow at the latest. I'm coming to see you almost immediately. But meanwhile have a look at the Monitor. It gives the bare facts as far as we knew them up to about midnight when Tony Parke rang up his paper. Nothing further has happened, but I'll tell you all I can when 

I see you." 

Of a sudden the girl tensed, her eyes on the queer figure of a man kneeling before the fire, carefully building up the coals. According to the Monitor the murdered man died at about eight o'clock. "Lo Chang!"

The man sat back on his feet and turned an Oriental face. He was a souvenir of the Merlyns' years in the Far East. Stella remembered Lo Chang when she was a little girl at Shanghai. There he had been one of the usual retinue. Here he took an immense pride in keeping the house clean. For a Chinaman he was rather tall. One could not easily have placed his age. 

When the Merlyns left the Orient for England Lo Chang had begged to accompany them, not from any

desire for travel but because no dog could ever have been more attached to human beings than was Lo 

Chang to "Meester Merlyn" and "Mees." "I gave you a note to take round to Mr. Seward last night. What time did you deliver it?" 

The slanting eyes were fixed on the girl. Years ago she had abandoned the task of trying to read anything from them. Generally they were abysmally inscrutable. If they seemed to smile it was possible that he was hiding some slight hurt. One never knew. 

He got up and after a pause slipped a hand into a pocket in the depths of his tunic, then produced an envelope. "Lo Chang velly solly. Him forget." He was standing with a dignified air of penitence, his tall spare figure slightly bowed. "You-you never delivered it! You-you forgot?"

Stella was puzzled. It was a tradition in the family that Lo Chang was the most faithful of all creatures and never forgot anything. "But you went out, with it, Lo Chang. Some time after half past seven. I saw you go."

His arms folded into his voluminous sleeves; he bent his head a shade. "Lo Chang met some fliends, fellow countlymen, and muchee talk. Him forget letter for Meester Seward. Him velly solly."

The fingers of Stella's hand tightened on the arm of her chair. From out of nowhere a fleeting idea had flashed into her mind. It was born of nothing except an inescapable coincidence: Lo Chang was sent out

with a note and he should have arrived at Bill's flat just about the same time that a man was murdered there. "Lo Chang forget. Him velly solly." The reiterated apology drifted to Stella's ears, and, as suddenly as the fleeting idea had come to her, it was almost dismissed —almost. Her nerves were raw and torn. This ripping to bits of her marriage plans, with the underlying current of tragedy at work, had come with devastating swiftness, and a sleepless night had made things worse.

But Lo Chang was permitted to see nothing of that tumult within her. The breed of the girl was in the furnace, and it was standing the test. Like her lost mother, Stella was proud. That carriage of the head, the delicate curve of the nostrils, the small finely chiselled ears, had not come down to Stella Merlyn as meaningless heritages.

This son of China, she remembered, led a peculiarly lonely life, voluntarily exiled from his people by his own blind loyalty to her and her father. She pictured the joy that would have been hers had she, immured in some alien land, run into friends whom she had never anticipated meeting. What more natural in the excitement than to forget to deliver a note? For Chinamen did get excited, in spite of their outward impassivity. That mask they all habitually wore was the result of generation after generation of them, throughout the centuries, learning to keep what Bill called "a poker face." "It doesn't matter, Lo Chang. Leave it there on the table, please."

With bowed back, Lo Chang padded softly from the room, closing the door behind him silently as ever, Stella noticed. Chang was very quiet in all that he did. It was "creepy," according to some of her friends.

But she had long grown used to him and his ways. 

This morning, though, perhaps because she was so nervy, there did seem something just the least uncanny about the Chinaman. It had never struck her in quite the same way before, but he moved about with gentleness that was feline, passed across a room as if his footfalls had no weight, and appeared at one's side like a wraith. And yet one felt that there was strength behind all that gentleness. Lo Chang was very muscular. She had seen him lift heavy things, such as trunks, with effortless ease. 

The girl's eyes went to the door through which he appeared to have slid, as though in this mood of hers she half expected him to materialize in the room again. 

And she started-for the door opened. But it was Bill 

Seward who came to her.

He took her into his arms, and then held her away from him. "Stella-Stella, my dearest, what can I say to you? 

Are you ever going to forgive me? I wouldn't have had this happen for all the money in the world. But you've been wonderful about it-perfectly wonderful!" "I want to know everything, Bill," she interrupted. "Everything, mind!" All her nerviness had gone now.

She was braced, steady. Something terrible had touched the life of the man she loved, and he was by her side.

He sat on the arm of her chair, and took her hand in both his. "It's queer," he began. "There's little more to tell you, and yet and yet there is so much. The thing is bewildering. The more one thinks about it and tries to unravel the mystery of it all, the more one's thoughts become tied in knots. The impossible, the utterly impossible, has happened; and yet that doesn't make sense. Everybody knows that the impossible cannot happen. Only one ray of light straggles through the tangle. Mary Findon went away after luncheon yesterday: it was her afternoon and evening out. She, naturally, had her latch-key. I had one, and Tony had his. Those three Yale keys are the only keys to the door of number thirteen. Stella, tell me frankly and honestly, as a woman whom I count a pretty good judge of character, what do you think of Findon?" 

Stella reflected for a while. "I have only seen her occasionally, but I think she's straight, Bill. In love with you of course- "Stella!" 

"Oh, discreetly in love. Not in the least likely to make an emotional scene, but she worships you as a sort of glowing star in another firmament.
