A brilliant writer of books which she thinks have no equal. Probably goes to your movies and weeps over them and gets immense satisfaction thereby. But I rather like her for it. It shows a proper spirit of appreciation for my Bill. Everybody loves you, dear; and I want them to, providing, that is, they display the same discretion in the matter as Mary Findon."

The man looked at her in wonder. "But, good Lord, Stella, I had no idea-" "Of course. She didn't mean you to. You're not within her range, that's all."

Bill frowned in perplexity. 

"In a way that makes it more complicated," he said. "Less understandable than ever, I mean. Stella, the girl hasn't come back yet."

There was a brief silence, broken only by the rumble of distant traffic. "You surely-surely you don't suspect her of committing the murder, dear? She is the last person on earth-" "No, no," the man broke in. "I have weighed the possibility up, naturally, but the idea is ludicrous. 

True, a woman might use a knife where a man would use a bludgeon. But the rest-no, Findon strangling a dying man is utterly unthinkable. I'll say that much for her, and don't intend to change my view. But the police attach no end of importance to the fact that she has vanished." "In what way, Bill?"

His shoulders were shrugged slightly. "She had a young man. One can't tell. Possibly- 

I only say possibly he was less interested really in 

Findon than in some ulterior purpose. It sounds mad. 

Why should some man cultivate a servant in order to gain entrance to her employer's flat and there kill a person who had no right there whatever? I give it up. At least, I'd be glad if I could, but for the present 

I'm not in a position to. Stella, darling, for a time

last night I was driven nearly crazy by the thought of how you would feel if-" he jibbed at the fence and then faced it another way. The girl's hand was cold, but the truth was inevitable. "The fact is, dearest, things looked so black for a while last night, that if Findon had returned at the ordinary time and given a rational explanation of her movements, one couldn't have blamed the Scotland Yard johnny if he'd invited me rather firmly to go back with him to headquarters.' 

Stella stared. "You!" 

He nodded. 

"I don't say they would have actually arrested me then and there. That would have involved definitely bringing a charge of murder against one William Cayley Seward, and there were two or three peculiar things about the case that pointed in other directions. The burglary, for instance, though I could see as well as they how easily that part might only have been a fake. 

No, I think in any event they would only have ‘detained' me, as they officially call it. Tony Parke, too, probably. He was in it, up to the neck. A good egg, Tony!" "He stood by you, Bill, dear. I'm sure of that." "Like a brick. I gave him one chance to keep out of the mess and he came near to exhibiting physical violence. Not seen him so cross for years. Don't look horrified about it all, my child. It's only an absurd situation which will be smoothed out in due course. 

You see, don't you, that I had to postpone the wedding, that's all. Only, we're not out of the wood yet.

Those Scotland Yard people have got to do their duty, and the more thorough they are about it the more pleased I shall be. Now," he drew her from the chair to the window, from which he glanced into the street, "you mustn't be alarmed, because this kind of thing leaves everybody connected with it more or less under suspicion for a time. Do you see that apparently harmless soul standing on the kerb across the road, who's just bought an early paper and is probably trying to pick a winner for the three-thirty this afternoon? Well, that's perhaps exactly what he is doing; but it isn't his main object in life at present- 

The girl's fingers were holding on to her fiancé's arm. The remnants of colour were draining from her cheeks. "Billy," she cut in, "you don't mean he is aa-" "Yes, precisely. A real flesh and blood sleuth!"

She winced, and for a few seconds her self-control hung in the balance. But she held on to Bill more tightly, and only her lip twitched. "My dear! My dear!" came from her, "I am terribly sorry. But you know that, surely!"

It was her suffering that galled the man. "Of course. And—and Stella, I'd like you to know how proud I am of you. But don't let this sort of thing," he indicated the detective with a jerk of the head, "make you uneasy. They have to do it, and, from what I know of Scotland Yard, it is nothing if not thorough."

A memory darted swiftly into the girl's brain. She

moved from the window, across the room. Bill was saying something, half jestingly, as though to relieve her tension, but it fell on deaf ears. She was standing on the hearthrug, looking into the fire. To her the flickering flames there did not exist. Then her eyes trailed toward the door. “Bill,” came from her in a low voice, “have you any suspicion, any shadow of a suspicion, who might have committed that murder?" "Not the remotest." "And the police? Do you think they have?"

It was a delicate point to answer truthfully. He hesitated for a few seconds. "I suppose they have suspicion," he replied. "They're bound to suspect until they have proofsuntil they narrow the field down to one person and make an arrest." "And how many are there in that field now? Let us look at this thing fearlessly, dearest."

Bill lighted a cigarette. Here was another fence at which he would gladly have jibbed for her sake, but he was learning anew the measure of Stella Merlyn, learning to reverence a side of her which hitherto he had only been able to guess at. "Shall we say three?" he told her. "Possibly four." "The three consist of Findon, Tony Parke and yourself?" "For the present the police have no option but to suspect us." "And the fourth?" "I don't know. So far a purely imaginary person,

or it might even be two others. It's difficult to say what really is in the minds of these crime experts. The 

Scotland Yard wallah who turned us inside out last night isn't exactly what I should call a communicative individual. A most disingenuous person. But at the same time I should say he's a corker. Once or twice last night, when I was half crazy with the worry of it all, knowing it meant chaos on the day we were to be married, I got a little impatient, which to say the least was not tactful; but he understood me like a mother. Just took another pinch of snuff and went on with his job. He's keen, though; not the type of man 

I'd pick to have on my trail if my conscience were uneasy." 

All this time Stella had been weighing something up in her mind. A distasteful business, and yet― 

She walked across to the table and there picked up a letter. "Bill, I sent Lo Chang across to you last evening with this note."

He looked at her enquiringly, imagination kindled by her manner. "Nothing serious, I hope, dearest?" he said quickly. "Not in the letter. It was just a message of love that was in my heart for you.' 

"You angel!" His fingers were beginning to tear at the envelope. "May I?" 

The girl's hands closed over his. "Not just now, dearest, though I still mean every word that is written there. Bill-how can I put it? 

Don't you see, dear? I sent him across to your flat. He

left here just after half past seven. But he never delivered it."

Neither spoke for a space. The tension in the room was increasing. "Half a minute, Stella! I don't exactly grasp this. 

Not quite myself these crowded days. Why didn't he bring it?" "I don't know, Billy. You remember he has a most extraordinary memory, but he says he forgot it because he encountered some Chinese friends." "Such a thing is possible. Even the best of us make slips at times." "I keep telling myself that," the girl went on, "and perhaps I am horribly disloyal even to raise a doubt about Lo Chang. I think he is the most faithful creature that ever lived. You know, dear, I'm not exaggerating in the least when I say he would lay down his life for me." "God bless him for that!" "And perhaps he would almost do it for you, now, Bill, because he knows that I love you.' 

Queer thoughts were flooding the man's mind; wild ideas that leaped on beyond the half expressed fears of Stella. Everything in the room seemed oddly still again. Then unconsciously Seward gave a low almost noiseless whistle. "Old Poker Face!" The words came from him in a whisper. "Bill," the girl put a hand on to each of his shoulders, and held a distressed face up to his, "tell me, is it wrong for me to have said that? It is you I am think-

ing of, dear heart. I can't help that. Perhaps even you don't understand. Lo Chang is not just like a servant. 

He seems to have given himself up body and soul to dad and me. You remember I told you that father went to a lot of trouble about his only son years ago, in Shanghai. Lo Chang worshipped the boy, who became ill when he was about sixteen. The Chinese doctors did what they could, and some of them are quite clever, but Lo Chang's son began to die. He would have died, too, only dad moved heaven and earth to fetch a German specialist who happened to be within reach. And the specialist made the boy live. Lo Chang never forgot that, though his son was killed three years later in a railway accident. He attached himself to us, just as you have seen, ever since. He had no near relatives left, so came over to Europe when we returned." "He always does seem like a sort of big watch dog about the place," agreed Seward. "Doesn't he? And suppose suppose when he got to your flat last night he found someone trying tooh, Bill, I don't know what to think!"

Seward was sitting on the arm of a chair, his eyes narrowed in intense thought. "My God! Old Poker Face! I wonder!" he breathed softly, even as the door opened and the Chinaman drifted across the carpet, in his strange self-effacing way, bearing a telegram-one of the many answers to Stella's messages sent early that morning. He handed it to her, turned and was half way back to the door when Seward stirred and spoke. "Lo Chang!" The Chinaman halted, came a step

nearer, and stood in an attitude of polite deference. 

Then, and not until then Seward felt himself to be at a perplexing cross-roads. Remarks of Stella's, half forgotten, and uttered at the time in a spirit that might have been jesting, were coming back to him. "Lo Chang is adopting you also, now. He already regards you as one of the family." The beady slanting eyes were so utterly unreadable. The Occidental mind was beaten, in a way, before it began a groping attack there; and Seward had little experience of the Chinese. "Miss Merlyn gave you a note to bring to me last night."

There was dignity in the Chinaman's bearing. "Him forget. Lo Chang alle same velly solly. Muchee talk to fliends." "Lo Chang, a man was murdered at my flat a little while after half past seven last night."

All Bill Seward's being was concentrated not on the 

Chinaman's answer, but on the Chinaman. He bowed a shade lower, dignified as ever. This was no news to him. "Lo Chang velly solly." Seward felt as if he were up against an impenetrable wall; a wall not of bricks nor solid granite, but some substance that was beyond his comprehension. "The police are making searching enquiries. You understand, Lo Chang?"

In that instant Seward fancied he detected a momentary gleam in the Chinaman's eyes.

"Lo Chang savvy." His somewhat high pitched birdlike voice, with its curious intonation, had not varied. "You see," came from Stella from across the room, "we are all terribly distressed about it. And if anyone saw you near Mr. Seward's flat about that time last night-" The servant's face crinkled into a smile-a Chinese smile. "Lo Chang savvy. Him no go near flat. Plenty fliends to bleak saucer and say him muchee talk long way flom flat." Deferentially he inclined his head and turned. He slid through the doorway. The door closed noiselessly behind him, leaving the girl and the man watching it, wondering. What had Lo Chang seen? 

What had Lo Chang done? The knife! Strong, merciless fingers clutching round a dying man's throat!

Seward repressed a shudder, and a moment later he had leaped up and caught the swaying form of Stella. "Bill! Bill!" she cried. "I'm frightened! It's all too horrible! I don't believe I can bear much more of this! If only we knew! If only we knew!" Then, of a sudden, as though the touch of Seward's arms gave her renewed strength, she was calmer. "I'm all right, dear," she went on a moment later. "I shan't give way like that again. But," extending a finger toward the window, "there's a man out there because the police think you may have done it. We've got to recognize that fact, Bill. It's ghastly! Suppose they took it into their heads to arrest you! To put you in prison and—

and-" she broke off, shaking her head as if to throw the thought aside, "and suppose, all the time, Lo Chang, whatever his motive-" The man's arms went around the girl. He drew her closer, soothingly, and she clung there. "We can't do anything-about old Poker Face, I mean. Fears aren't evidence, dear; and there isn't anything to connect Lo Chang with the crime so far. 

Leave things as they are at present, but we'll talk it over with your dad, and-"

Both arms fell to his side. With face as inscrutable as ever, Lo Chang stood within the doorway. Neither 

Bill nor Stella had seen him enter, nor could they guess how much he had heard. Hardly appearing to have paused, he came on now, bearing a salver. "Telegram, mees!" He remained like a waxen figure while the girl picked up the buff envelop, then turned without raising his eyes. A moment later he was gone. 

The unopened telegram fluttered from Stella's fingers to the floor. "Bill!" she said in a hushed voice, "he heard what we were saying. I'm sure he did."

The man lighted a cigarette with a steady hand. "I shouldn't be surprised," he replied. "In fact I believe I'm getting to the stage where nothing would surprise me much."

It was within half an hour of noon when Bill returned to Tony Parke's flat. In the hall there was a man sitting, a bowler hat balanced on his knees. He

rose as the author appeared, and spoke with an only half hidden touch of authority. "Mr. Seward?" It was more an assertion than a query. 

Bill's pulse beat a shade faster as his eyes took in the by-no-means-new bowler hat; the semi-military cut of the man; his manner, which though not discourteous had the unmistakable weight of the Law behind it. "That is my name. What do you want?" "I'm Detective Macintosh. You're wanted at Scotland Yard, sir. Inspector Neville sent me up specially." "I see," came from Seward steadily. "And may I ask exactly what I am to understand by the term 'wanted at Scotland Yard'?" "The Inspector didn't give me any particulars, sir. 

Just told me to lose no time." 

Seward turned on his heel. What was the meaning of this new twist in the game? What card was Neville pulling out of his sleeve? Bill wondered vaguely whether he would be at liberty-would he?-to get back as arranged to have tea with Stella. "Come along, then," he said. "We'll get a taxi at the corner."

CHAPTER VI: SLIM JOE 

(10.30 a. m., Friday, September 2nd.)

HERE is something depressing about Cluney Street, Bloomsbury. It is even losing that air of having seen better days which clings to other Bloomsbury streets. One gathers that those who dwell there have abandoned a good deal of hope. Life has ceased to pulsate electrically so far as they are concerned. Their clothes are respectable and shiny. 

They have little to look forward to except the next dismal meal at the tables of these arid boarding houses. 

Romance is a thing of their forgotten youth. But it is cheap to live in Cluney Street. 

There was a slight drizzle as Detective Sergeant 

Trim walked briskly along, glancing carefully at the numbers, but the weather and the spiritual qualities of the district left him unmoved. His expression conveyed a distinct degree of satisfaction. He had a full face, a reddish moustache, a slight tendency to embonpoint, and generally gave the impression of a contented farmer dressed up for London in his best blue serge. On the whole, this morning, the Sergeant was rather proud of what he had achieved in a remarkably short time. It might have taken days, even weeks, to attain this end. But the records at the Yard were a big help. 72

SLIM JOE 

73 True, luck had been with him, and Neville had dug a spur deep into his side, putting him on his mettle. 

This flat murder seemed to have floored the Inspector. 

Trim could see that. He knew. Hadn't worked with old Neville all these years without learning something about the man. To-day the Inspector was like a pea in a hot frying pan. Other people might not spot that, perhaps, but they didn't have the same opportunities of judging. He got wound up, Trim reflected, entwining metaphors, like a bear with a sore head, if there was any little hitch in carrying out his orders. 

That was a sure sign with Neville. As a matter of fact he seemed to have a bee in his bonnet over this case. 

It had looked a bit puzzling at first, but not for long. 

It was too soon to put the bracelets on anyone yet, but the end was in sight. Seward and the servant girl.

There couldn't be much doubt about that. 

She let the deceased in, maybe quite innocently, the Sergeant ruminated, and as soon as Seward saw him there was a hell of a row. Naturally! You could only expect that when a blackmailer arrived on the scene. 

Then they had a fight, and fur and feathers must have been flying while it lasted. No Marquis of Queensbury rules about that little scrap. Seward got the chap by the throat and was squeezing the life out of him, when the girl lost her head and butted in. Thought her boss was going to get the worst of it, and felt scared Peters would do for her too. So she grabbed the Malay knife off the wall and gave him a dig to clinch matters. Probably hadn't the remotest idea of actually killing Peters, and nearly threw a fit when she realised what she'd

done. Then bolted, in a panic. The Sergeant could see her doing it. Maybe, even, Seward told her to clear out and say nothing, because he knew she'd only done it to help him. No wonder she hadn't had the pluck to go back again, knowing the police would charge her with killing Peters. Then Seward did a bit of quick thinking. He'd just got the room nicely fixed up to look as though burglars had been in, when his pal arrived.

There didn't seem any possible flaw in this theory, to Sergeant Trim. Still, they had to make very sure of things at the Yard before charging anyone with murder. That was one reason why Neville had dug a spur into Trim's side over the job he was now about to complete. After ten minutes at the dossier, two urgent telephone enquiries, and one more reference to the dossier, here he was. Ah-number thirty-three! As likely as not his bird would be out at this hour, but then again he might not.

A tired-looking slattern answered his ring. "Tell Mr. Joseph Lowther I want to see him." "Mr. Lowther? I don't know if 'e's in. Who is it wants 'im?" "Er-Mr. Trim, say."

The slattern surveyed the Sergeant from the feet upward. They were very particular whom they asked in at 33 Cluney Street. But this customer looked all right: though you never could tell! "Better come in," she admitted grudgingly, and, having deposited him in the mausoleum that was known as the drawing room, faded away.

SLIM JOE 

75 "The gent's shaving," she announced, returning a while later. "But I called through to him and he'll be down in a few minutes."

Trim nodded, straightened his waistcoat and stared blankly at the mausoleum exhibits for a few seconds, then settled down to wait-with his eyes fixed through the open door of the drawing room, into the hall. Presently he got up with a jerk. The man standing there had an air. The aroma of Cluney Street was not his true environment. Perhaps he was a shade too thin for his clothes, but even so he carried that discrepancy off with distinction. His linen was spotless, his shoes clean and of good shape. Maybe his eyes were rather close together, but otherwise they were frank and disarming. He came nearer with the polite interest of a nobleman receiving an unknown caller. There was so little about this middle aged elegant to suggest that he was known in other circles as "Slim Joe," down on his luck for the moment, but sure to find his place in the sun again soon-the cleverest confidence trickster from

Land's End to John O'Groats. 

"Mr. Trim?" His voice was well modulated. "Detective Sergeant Trim, of Scotland Yard." 

Mr. Lowther looked no less politely attentive. "Oh, I see!" One might almost have fancied he could not spot a plain clothes officer a hundred yards away. "And what can I have the—” "You're on ticket-of-leave. They want a word with you down at the Yard, Lowther." The Sergeant's tone was blunt. He had not come there to make pretty speeches.

Mr. Lowther's eyebrows rose in frank surprise. "Really! Of course I will come. With pleasure—at least-" He waved a well shapen hand that had not yet fully recovered from some rough task to which it had been ill-adapted. His smile contained more than 

Trim could have said in many a long sentence. "I am at your service." Taking his hat from a chair where he had placed it in the hall, Mr. Lowther led the way to the outer door. "The 'buses-" he began, suavely. "We go by cab," answered Trim, abruptly, keeping within arm's length.

A few moments later the gears of their taxi grated. "You're pretty busy these days at the Yard?" queried Mr. Lowther with aggravating nonchalance. 

Sergeant Trim cleared his throat and pulled down his waistcoat. Crooks of all varieties passed through his hands, but this was the first time he had encountered Slim Joe. "A bit too busy for some folk," he retorted; and lapsed into silence which his companion did not disturb. Mr. Lowther had plenty to think about. At last the cab pulled up. "Come on, Lowther, this way!" urged the Sergeant, ushering the confidence man through an uninviting doorway. A few moments later he found himself with a kindly mannered, elderly soul, who looked as though butter would not melt in his mouth. He was waved to a chair. "I don't think I have had the pleasure before-" 

Lowther began gracefully.

SLIM JOE 

77 “No, no, indeed," replied Detective Inspector Neville. "Quite a-um, um-quite a misfortune-for me, shall we say?" There was even a twinkle in his eyes. "At least there remain no old sores, so perhaps you won't mind my asking a personal question." "You may be sure." Slim was the apotheosis of courtesy, while Neville's eyes rested upon him without animosity. Did Slim know what trick was to spring out of the box? Did a vision already float before him of a crowded court; a grim implacable judge putting a black cap on; gaunt gallows waiting at dawn in a prison yard? Effrontery was this man's stock in trade. "Where were you between 7.30 p. m. and 8.30 p. m. last night?" asked Neville easily.

Lowther flicked a speck off his coat sleeve. "At last," he said, sparring evasively for time, “I fancy I see the drift of all this. Why not have been more explicit at first?" "So! What morning paper do you generally read?" the Inspector shifted his ground adroitly. "I prefer the Times as a rule." "And have you seen the Times this morning?” "By chance, no. Have I missed anything of special interest there?" "What paper did you read, then?" "I glanced at the Monitor."

He was suave and cool. Neville was leaning forward, his elbows resting on the desk. "Curious, that, wasn't it, Lowther? You haven't by any chance run your eyes through every morning paper you could get hold of?"

"Indeed, no. Why should I?" "Because," said Neville, "it so happens that you couldn't have known what I was getting at unless you had seen the Monitor. That happens to be the only newspaper published this morning containing the news." "Let us be quite clear what you are getting at," said Lowther. "I presume you refer to the murder of Australian Pete. An unpleasant character. Frankly, I am not going to be hypocritical enough to say that his death moves me to tears. As you are aware, it was largely owing to his defection that I was deprived of my liberty for three years and nine months recently. 

By the way, I suppose this is what you are driving at. 

So far I am only guessing." "And a pretty accurate guess. But you couldn't have gone far wrong, and I should never classify you among the fools."

Again that charmingly courteous inclination of the head. "You are too kind, Inspector!" Neville ignored the subtle insolence. "Where were you at the time Peters was murdered?” 

This was hurled like a grenade.

Lowther sat still and composed, the light from the window full on his pale face. Almost an intellectual countenance. His long, slender fingers were resting easily on the arms of the chair. Either his brain controlled both features and hands marvellously, or he was laughing up his sleeve.

SLIM JOE 

79 "Really, Inspector, you know you do ask most awkward questions!" "It is my business to ask awkward questions. And they've got to be answered." "I'm rather afraid in this case there will be some little difficulty," said Slim.

Neville pushed a mass of papers away from him and sat back. "Well, it's your funeral."

The other man's shoulders moved infinitesimally. "In that case it remains-my funeral." "With a charge of murder hanging on to it! Think twice, Lowther. Better to have an alibi that would land you back in Dartmoor than to have your neck stretched." "Quite," agreed the confidence trickster. "Please understand that I see your point very clearly. Startlingly so. You leave me suspended between the devil and the deep sea. And yet-I must remain there." "You may find that inconvenient." "Perhaps. But if you don't quite know what to do, arrest me on a charge of murder, and I'll make a fool of you." "How?" "With the clearest, most cast-iron alibi you were ever up against.” "You'll need it, Slim. We have a letter written by

Australian Pete showing he anticipated trouble from you." "Do you wonder?" asked Lowther, without any sign

of agitation. "But there are less crude ways of punishing an enemy than strangling him.” "It may be. That isn't my business. For the moment I'm concerned with nothing except your movements between 7.30 and 8.30 last night." "Very well." For a space Lowther's eyes strayed to a desultory ray of sunlight filtering through the window. "I will give you some idea. I warn you though, the story will carry no conviction. It is open to you to bring a charge of murder against me if you dare take the chance. Then you will force my hand; but nothing less than that will be sufficient to make me drag others in and produce proofs. To begin with, you don't suppose for an instant that my real name is Lowther?" "It serves so far as our records go." "I won't go so far as to assert that I have a conscience, Inspector. That might place too great a strain upon your credulity. Besides, it wouldn't be true. I have one little weakness, however, which, for want of a better name, we can put down to sentiment. Once upon a time I was the bright particular star of an honoured family. I went to a public school: one of the greatest in England. But in my early twenties I found that my instincts and those of the rest of my family did not blend. The call of adventure in the blood stirred. Atavism, doubtless. I roamed the world, sought trouble, and found it. But by that time I had hidden my identity under another name. You are beginning to appreciate the delicacy of my position?" "Not at all. Please continue."

SLIM JOE 

81 "By this time those of my family who survive have come to regard me as a hopeless nomad, a little eccentric, perhaps, but sinless. Gloriously sinless, Inspector; worthy of the finest traditions in a grand old family, save only that I do not settle down, enter Parliament and devote myself to a life of visible virtue. "Well, here is my alibi. Now and again the sinless 

Prodigal returns from afar and descends into the bosom of his family. In some way he gets a kick out of it. 

It flatters his dormant pride, perhaps, to move for an hour or so in a circle where the lock step is unknown and the deference of servants is no mocking return for money paid, but is a real thing, built upon lifelong knowledge of those they serve. I was many miles from London all yesterday afternoon and evening, Inspector. To be precise, until at least half past nine last night I was with those who have known me since boyhood. After that I was driven behind a fat old pony back to a country station, and returned to that dingy other life, which suffices for the moment, in a 

Cluney Street boarding house."

He paused, meeting the C. I. D. official's eyes squarely. "I will not say," he went on, "that I would die rather than let my friends know their Prodigal is only a ticketof-leave man. But you can take it from me, here and now, definitely and finally, that nothing you can think of short of an actual charge of murder is going to induce me to open my mouth any further and let those friends get even a bare hint of the truth. "One of them, my eldest brother, is a highly placed

dignitary in the Church; a man whose evidence would carry such weight that no jury would dream of convicting in the circumstances. My sister, who upon her marriage became a titled woman, was there all the evening; and her husband, though he has no particular use for me, would make up an all-sufficient trio. That, Inspector, is my alibi."

Neville had listened carefully, and now regarded his vis-à-vis with added interest. Figuratively Slim Joe had made his move and declared "check!" One could call his bluff, but the price might be high. Meanwhile Lowther was known to be such a past master in the art of lying that when he arrived at the pearly gates St.

Peter himself would have to put on his thinking cap. 

On the other hand, the truth was sufficient sometimes even for an arch priest in the Temple of Lies. "It's a good story, Lowther," he said. "And you told it well. Plenty of restraint. No mock heroics. If I didn't know you to be one of the cleverest, most cunning crooks in our collection, I might almost have believed every word. I said I might. And anyway this is only the first round so far as we are concerned. 

You're a shrewd fox, Lowther, and you know as well as I do, that if I can dig up any evidence against you, all the archbishops on earth won't save you from being arrested." "I must thank you for an entertaining interview, Inspector," said Lowther, "but the trend of our conversation has not been exactly elevating, has it?"

The C. I. D. official pressed a button. Anticipating his dismissal Lowther got up.

SLIM JOE 

83 "You have my address, I believe?" It was the most urbane of enquiries. 

Neville helped himself to a pinch of snuff. "I believe we have." The door had been opened promptly and, obeying precise instructions, Trim had ushered Bill Seward in at the signal. Bill paused, framed at the doorway, for a second, his glance touching the two occupants of the room. To the Inspector he gave a quick nod of recognition. His eyes fell on

Slim Joe, hovered there for a space of three heart beats, then turned back to the C. I. D. official. And in that brief time infinite possible contingencies had hung suspended by a slender hair. Even Neville's mask had momentarily fallen away. His head was thrust forward expectantly. He hardly breathed. One quick start of recognition on the part of Seward would have completed a damning chain of circumstantial evidence. 

It would have gone a long way toward discrediting every statement the author had made. 

If he knew Slim Joe, his defence would begin to crumble rapidly. That defence rested mainly on the allegation that Australian Pete and the recently liberated criminal referred to in Peters' letter as Slim, were total strangers. "You wanted to see me, Inspector?" he enquired, outwardly unconcerned.

Slim, having regarded Seward with a blatant, noncommittal scrutiny, during which his lips twitched, moved nearer to the door. And did not look back. He was permitted to pass along corridors to that main doorway which was so much more inviting when one's

steps led outward. In the open air once again, Slim 

Joe permitted himself the luxury of an agreeable sigh. 

A drizzling rain still continued, but there were times when it felt good to be out in the rain. Particularly so to-day. There was every chance that he would pass those portals sooner or later-one never knew how soon!-and not emerge again free as the four winds of heaven. Unconsciously his step quickened as he gained the street. That which lay behind was unpalatable to the thoughts. Few people had pleasant memories of Scotland Yard. This was not the first time 

Slim had been taken there against his wishes, but hitherto no question of murder had been associated with his visits.

Murder was a sinister business. His self-control during the last half hour or so had not been maintained easily, even though Neville might have thought so. 

While his fingers toyed idly with the cord of an eyeglass, his nerves had come very close to getting out of hand. Thanks, probably, to that last term of imprisonment. It had shattered something in him, torn away a part of that supreme self-confidence which had for so long buttressed him in any tight corner.

As soon as he was on the Embankment, well clear of the accursed police atmosphere, he ran one hand along the inside of his collar, to ease it from his throat.

I 

CHAPTER VII: "WANTED!" 

(Saturday morning, September 3rd.) 

T was the second day after the murder. Detective

Inspector Neville swung to and fro in his swivel chair. The slanting rays of the autumn sun were warming the tone of this somewhat austere Scotland Yard room. It might have been the office of a solicitor with untidy habits. In all save his work, Neville was untidy. From sheer weariness after many years, his wife had almost ceased to point out the fact that she admired men who had a place for everything and kept everything in its place. Also he took snuff, which he knew was in keeping with his outward untidiness. Even now, as the swivel chair swung slowly, he fished out the enamel box, snapped up the lid, and proffered it to his military looking friend in the easy chair. "Ah, no, you don't take it, of course, doctor. A dirty practice, but agreeable and stimulating to a sluggish brain. So❞—he sniffed a pinch of the brown powder with relish "so you have no helpful theory about this 

Regent House affair." "It isn't my business to work out theories about such things, Neville,” replied the police surgeon. "But you can't help 'em coming, can you? I've got half a dozen at least, in connection with that murder." 85

"You think the author chap Seward did it?”

The detective half closed his eyes and looked into the purples and golden reds of the sunbeams that filtered across his desk. A crowded desk on which were jumbled notes, written statements and other things, all mosaic bits out of actual lives in the seething panorama which unfolded itself before him endlessly in his many-sided work. Some of the bits he had pieced together in various cases. Many were loose odds and ends that apparently fitted nowhere. Others, set aside, were disconnected links in perplexing problems that he hoped some day would yield their secret, when the everlasting mills of Scotland Yard had gone on grinding long enough.

There was the child's toy which still might prove the key that would solve the problem of a Liverpool shipping magnate's grim death in a London hotel bedroom, though nobody had yet guessed why he should have been clutching a woolly dog with one leg missing when he was murdered. In Liverpool the man had lived a normal life with wife and children, apparently content in his own home. The woolly dog was a hint at some hidden drama on which the curtain had possibly been rung down for ever now.

There was an ebony chess bishop which a London 'bus driver received by post from Paris the night before he was found shot on his own doorstep in Wimbledon. Doubtless, that 'bus driver knew who sent it and understood the connection between its message and his own death; but his lips were now eternally sealed.

There was a violin mute which was found resting on

"WANTED!" 87 the forehead of a member of a night club jazz band who apparently had died of heart disease, but whose dead face was eloquent of terror. "You've been with us a good many years here at the 

Yard, doctor," he said. "Professional red-tape, or something, won't let you admit that you're interested in police work, but you know more about it than many of our fellows whom I could mention. And you know that in a case of this sort, as a rule, if you can only place your finger on the motive, you're not far off putting your hand on the culprit. Now, take the position of the newspaper man, Anthony Parke, first. No apparent reason why he should have done it." "Even so, I wouldn't cross him off quite," replied the doctor. "May I ask why?" "Well, he, at least, is one of the few people who had the opportunity of committing the murder, and his story of that conversation with an American acquaintance whom he cannot produce is, to say the least, not satisfying." "Exactly. And yet you and I know how easily it might have happened. On the other hand, look how different the position is in regard to Seward. Be honest and tell me, can you see a clean-cut motive in that man's heart for murdering Australian Pete?" "Well, yes, if the case weren't so tangled up, I would agree; but there's the servant girl." "And you doubt whether she would have been capable of committing such a crime?" 

The doctor's brows became knitted together.

"Physically she, of course, could. To stab a strong man with a sharp knife is easy: to strangle him is quite another matter excepting, as in this instance, where he had already received a knife in his back, and was dying. But, my hat, if she did it—I mean if she killed that man in the way he was killed-there must indeed have been some terrific motive. And you know of none so far as she is concerned." "I have a theory, but that's all." Neville fingered the photo of the girl that had lain on his desk. “Nothing especially criminal about her face, you see," he mused. "May I ask what your theory is?" "So far it's useless, based on no evidence whatever. 

We have not been able to trace any link between her and the deceased. But," he turned to the medical man with a gentle smile, "suppose we found that Peters had been about with her had tampered with her affections, as it is called." "Ah! A crime of passion! That would account for a good deal. The woman whom passion betrays into crime is very different from the born offender. Lombroso himself admits that women who commit such crimes have no special physiognomical characteristics nor signs of degeneration." "And," replied the detective, "premeditation in some form or another—that is, the intention to do bodily harm-is often nurtured by a woman longer than by men, so that her crime is eventually executed with an ability and a gloating which otherwise would be psychologically impossible." He tossed the photograph

"WANTED!" 89 back on the desk and took another pinch of snuff. “All of which is perfectly true but it doesn't get convictions. I wouldn't worry much about motives if that girl's finger-prints had been found on the handle of the kris." "No sign of anything there, except those of Seward?" "Not a darned sign. And yet if Mary Findon was all dolled up to go out-a smartly dressed bit mind! -she would have gloves on. And you just naturally wouldn't expect her to ask Peters to wait a minute while she took them off before she knifed him. It's only theory, doctor, and it won't hold water, not for one holy minute, but," he spun in his chair and faced the medical man abruptly, "why has she disappeared? Her mother says she waited in all Thursday afternoon and evening for the girl, who never turned up." "Which may and may not be true," observed the doctor. "Yes, don't I know it!" agreed Neville, playing a fretful tattoo on the desk with his finger tips. "Mothers make marvellous actresses when it comes to protecting their own offspring. It's often the strongest instinct in the world-stronger, even, than that of self-preservation. For example, don't forget that Highgate woman who secreted her eighteen-year-old son for six weeks early this year, knowing he had been guilty of a particularly brutal murder." "You've been through Mrs. Findon's house in Riviera Terrace?" "Oh yes, and made sure that nobody's concealed

there. I took no chances on that, you may be sure, but there's no telling what the old woman really knows." "When did Mary go home last?" asked the doctor. "The previous Thursday, and, according to the old woman, she didn't seem quite her natural self then. 

Not as bright as usual: something troubling her a bit.

Mrs. Findon thought she might have had a difference with a young man, but the girl didn't say. Seems to have been rather reticent about her love affairs, since she broke off with a plumber in the spring." "And the plumber?" "Oh, he's married and settled down in Birmingham.

Attended a lodge of Oddfellows there last Thursday night. No question about him." "And Mary Findon's other friends in Peckham? 

You tackled them?" "Yes, but she isn't the sort to make many friends.

One or two girls, that's all, and they haven't seen Mary for some time. Why is that girl afraid to show her nose? Tell me the answer to that, man, and maybe I'll be able to sleep a little better o' nights."

The medical officer stroked his moustache slowly, twisting the ends with a vicious little final tug as was his habit when particularly thoughtful. "To me," he said, "the clear inference is that she may have passed her key on to someone else, probably knowing that person had criminal intent, and she is keeping out of the way now because she is aware that she'll get her wings clipped."

'WANTED!" 91 The detective patted his snuff box impatiently with the tips of his fingers and took a pinch. "There's nothing so far to support such a theory. 

It might be so, but time enough to face that when she puts the yarn up in her own defence." "Very well," said the doctor, his lips curving, "only before she puts that yarn up you must go through the formality of catching her. Now, for the sake of argument, we'll say she let herself into the flat. Remember, that was hours after she went off for the day, and hours before she was due back. If that was so, who let Australian Pete in?" "That's easy-if one presupposes that Mary Findon murdered him. She let him in herself. Went back to the flat unexpectedly, perhaps, and found him ringing the bell. Whether she met him there, though, or elsewhere, is immaterial. There must have been some sort of understanding between them." "Because of the extreme violence with which she killed him, you mean," put in Dr. Mappin. "That-and the fact of the place having been burgled. Expert though he was, Peters couldn't have opened those three drawers with a poker in under ten minutes. That was his work, undoubtedly. There is no trace on the poker of any finger-prints except his. 

And, bear in mind, a law-abiding servant doesn't stand twiddling her thumbs while desks are being pried open." 

The doctor nodded. "Considering the highly abnormal condition in which

she must have been, a pathological phenomenon in fact," he commented, "the wonder is that, after killing the man, she had sufficient mental balance left to pick up her handbag." "We don't know that she carried one." "Well, key then. It's the same thing. Actually she must have picked up her bag containing the key. They all carry a handbag these days. Women don't have pockets." "She must have been a pretty cool customer," remarked the detective. "Pretty cool! Took the other things out of the cabinet, and the cigarette case, to give colour to the idea of burglary. Then some hours afterwards got cold feet, which is more understandable. 

Had no alibi, and maybe feared she had been seen near the place. I'm waiting for someone to come forward and say they saw her enter or leave Regent House round about eight o'clock last Thursday." 

The doctor's lips were again curling. "All very ingenious, Neville," he agreed. "A crime of passion, you call it, eh?" "What else?" 

The doctor shook his head. "Because I doubt very much whether she killed

Australian Pete at all. If she did-if the basis of that tremendous brain storm lay in the primal fact that he was a man and she was a woman-she would no more have been capable of staging an imitation burglary immediately afterwards than you could tuck St. 

Paul's Cathedral under your arm and walk away with it. We're talking about a woman's crime of passion,

'WANTED!" 93 remember, not a man's. When it was over she would have been practically demented. She would even have exulted in what she had done." "I haven't noticed so far that she's proclaimed it from the house-tops." "I don't say she would go to such extremes, but most emphatically she wouldn't immediately-immediately, observe, for there was a very small margin of time before Seward and Parke were on the spot-have calmed down sufficiently to think of such a neat plan of deception." "We don't exactly know that the burglary was a fake." "At any rate, Neville, if you attribute that to her, as a fake, I'm afraid you're up the wrong street. Why 

Mary Findon is afraid to show her face perplexes me unless she let another man in, or gave him the key."

Neville pulled out the snuff box again, almost fretfully this time. He dealt in facts and man-made laws. 

He didn't claim to be a pathologist: couldn't argue with one. Dr. Mappin knew his job, and he was accepted as an authority on the criminal brain. Darn 

Mappin for upsetting one of his pet theories about that cursed affair at flat thirteen. At best it had only been a conjecture, but it had seemed to hang together fairly consistently. The only thing lacking to prove it one way or the other was the presence of the girl herself.

All he wanted was ten minutes in that room at Scotland Yard, alone with her. And if she had pierced

Peters' back with a knife, afterwards wrapping her

wiry fingers around the man's throat until she had choked the remnants of life out of him, he would be aware of it. Oh, yes, he would know! Or, if she had only been a spectator of the tragedy-a contingency that was distinctly possible-he would drag her secret out of her.

There came a tap on the door, and in answer to Neville's call, a quaint figure entered. "Ah, Cripps," the detective waved a welcome, “just the very man I wanted to see. Well, what d'you make of it?"

The visitor carried a small leather handbag, and regarded Neville over the top of his spectacles. He was over sixty and his face was covered with wrinkles. 

The little eyes were extraordinarily bright. He walked with a curious gait, knees always slightly bent like those of a weary cab horse. Personal appearance was clearly a matter of no importance to Mr. Cripps. 

But to Scotland Yard he was a jewel of the first water. He came of the old school of lockmakers, men who learned their craft in the days when a skilled trade was something of an art, and had shied at factory ways. Now when anything in connection with keys or locks bothered them at the Yard, Cripps was sent for. 

He was probably the greatest authority in London in that particular field.

He put his handbag on the table and took from it the Yale lock that had come off the door of flat thirteen in Regent House.

As if it had been the jewels of England, he laid it carefully on a blotting pad.

"WANTED!" 95 "The lock's all right, Mr. Neville," he said, "except that a screw inside is coming loose. It's like this," he drew a small diagram, talking the while. "Now see here, when the screw works loose, the pins inside the lock don't fall true into the ridges of the key, and the key would have a tendency to jam in the lock, same as you told me it did when you tried it in the door. 

But that's all. The lock's in perfect condition otherwise; well oiled and no sign of rust or dirt."

Dr. Mappin looked from the face of the old lock expert to that of Neville and observed there a definite expression of disappointment. Obviously he had hoped for something more helpful from Cripps. "And how far would that affect the working of the lock?" the detective asked. "It wouldn't affect it at all," replied Cripps decisively. "Not in the very slightest degree. The lock's as good as the day it was made-except for the one fact that the key's liable to stick a bit." "All right, Cripps," Neville grumbled. "Go back to

Regent House and put the thing on again as you found it."

Cripps pushed his spectacles up on to his bald head. "And what was it you expected me to find was wrong with the lock, Mr. Neville?" "Well now," replied Neville reflectively, "if you'd said the latch was liable not to work when one slammed the door, so that anyone could walk in, it might have gone a long way toward saving a man's life.” "Then you'd better hang him," returned Cripps with finality, pulling down his spectacles again and replac-

ing his things in the little leather bag, "because, apart from that slight difficulty with the key, it's fit to put on the front door of the Bank of England." "If the latch was not caught back, you mean, nobody could have got in without a key?" "Utterly impossible." "Could a man have opened it with a piece of bent wire? There was a bit of wire in the dead man's pocket." "No. And he didn't try, either." "Didn't try!" "Well, not to any extent. I've put the sections under a strong magnifying glass, and there isn't any sign of scratches such as you're liable to get on the inside of a lock when you tamper with it that way. Is that all you want me for, Mr. Neville?" "Yes, thanks, Cripps. Good morning." 

The expert shuffled out. Neville thrust his feet upon an open drawer in the desk before him and leaned back in his chair contemplatively. The sticking key had almost side-tracked him at first, but the expert view settled that problem. "It only goes to prove more and more," he said slowly, "that Seward was fastened up in the flat just as securely as if he'd been hermetically sealed in all round with solid masonry. If Australian Pete had had a key on him it would at least have shown how he got in." "No man-holes or trap doors anywhere, of course?" "I spent five hours in the place yesterday and couldn't find a crack or a hole big enough to let a rat

"WANTED!" 97 through. And one of the most amazing things about it all is that Seward himself is our chief witness on that point. He swears, and sticks to it, that the door was securely latched and every window fastened except the one right bang in front of him where he was working in his study. And that one wasn't actually open. However obsessed an author was with his writing, a burglar couldn't have come in there unnoticed, four feet from Seward's nose, crawl out past him into the library, stab himself in the back and then strangle himself. There's evidence, of a sort, enough to hang Seward twice over, only as I know evidence in murder cases it doesn't quite ring true." "You mean if he'd been the murderer he would deliberately have left the front door wide open?"

The Inspector rumpled his hair in perplexity. "Either that, or a window, or something. But as it stands for the present it seems to me too conclusive. 

Too perfect to consist of sheer unadulterated bluff." "But if Findon hadn't disappeared?" 

Neville's eyes snapped. "In that case, my friend," he said with a dry smile, "William Cayley Seward would now probably be locked up on the charge of having murdered Peters, and I have no doubt whatever that after a careful trial he would be well and truly executed. But as things are, unless he's done away with the girl too-and I won't exclude that from the range of possibilities—the moment hasn't arrived to prefer a charge against him."

The doctor got up and flung the end of a cigar through the open window.

"Had the girl Findon any money?" "Not much, as far as we can find out. Possibly a pound or so, but not more, unless she got hold of it in some unusual way. It seems she gives what she can to her mother. Any particular reason for asking?" "No," said the doctor, "except that money becomes a vital necessity when you're trying to evade arrest. 

If she'd once got over to France-" "Unless she caught a channel boat that night, Mary 

Findon hasn't left the country," said the detective. "Every port of exit was closed to her first thing the next morning. No, she's probably in London at this very minute one of the millions." "Ah!" said the medical officer, straightening his tie and moving toward the door. "You don't play the piano, do you?" "Piano? Me? No. Why?" "Besides, you don't keep such things as musical instruments in Scotland Yard. It's a pity!"

The detective took snuff very deliberately. Exactly what was simmering in the mind of this doctor who professed to take no interest in criminal matters? 

Neville liked Mappin, and admired his astuteness. 

From time to time he threw a remarkably interesting sidelight on things. He ought to have been a detective instead of a medical man with the outward semblance of a retired Anglo-Indian colonel on half pay. That was why Neville had spent all this time churning the case over with him, analysing the known factors and exploring the possibilities.

'WANTED!" 99 "I'll buy it, doctor?" "What? A piano?" "If necessary-yes.” "You might do worse. Or hire one. And then when you land Mary Findon in here (if such a thing happens, though I have a growing belief that it won't), get someone to thump out the air 'Ain't She Sweet?' 

And watch her face. It might tell you quite a lot."

The Inspector sat still, stroking his chin. The point was not lost upon him, but he was thinking that if he had the servant girl in the room with him at that moment he certainly would not wish to sit down at a piano and play to her. And anyway, for the present, there was a deplorably deep gulf fixed between him and Mary Findon. "That reminds me," he said, "we made rather an astonishing discovery this morning. While I was at

Regent House yesterday I took the opportunity of running the rule over that pianist above Seward's flat. 

A weed of a youth-about nineteen-who took the flat, furnished, on his own, and has been living there for two months having a good time, as he calls it. Night clubs and so on. Admitted he smokes fifty cigarettes a day, and hasn't the remotest idea of work or a career, or anything above the jazz level." "Some father's pampered darling?" 

Neville laughed. "Wait a minute. He bolted this morning. Maybe the murder underneath gave him a bit of a jolt. Maybe he was at the end of his tether. But he just cleared out, owing money to tradespeople all round. The place

is littered with unpaid bills. The caretakers rang me up in a deuce of a sweat at nine o'clock, and at first I imagined it might be a very important angle of the 

Peters murder." "Sounds like it, anyway." "Yes, I should have been up there now, only not long after, the caretaker rang up again to say one of the youngster's creditors had traced him. He'd gone home to mother, where he'll probably get a good spanking." 

The doctor pursed his lips. "You're sure that's all there is in it? No possible link with the murder, I mean?" "I felt sure of that when I saw him yesterday. But 

I'm going to look him up again. Anyway, we shan't lose sight of him."

As the doctor left him, Neville's eyes drifted to a printed notice bearing the reproduction of the photo on his desk, which must already have been scanned by some thousands of people. By to-morrow morning it would have been seen by millions, for he had circulated it among the newspapers. And they would leap at a plum like that.

WANTED In connection with the murder of John Anderson, alias Peters, on the 1st September, 1927, Mary 

Findon, aged 22, of —

The detective's glance flicked away from the notice. 

That ought to get the woman, unless someone was

"WANTED!" 101 helping her to hide. And even if she herself didn't commit the crime, she knew who did. That, at least, was obvious-unless William Cayley Seward did it, and had done away with her also.

His mind drifted back to Slim Joe, and again to the fact that every hour increased his desire to rope Mary 

Findon in. To-morrow morning her orbit would be vastly reduced. The modern press had its uses. There wasn't a village or hamlet in Great Britain where his crying need would not be known. Mere murder did not greatly stir the general public, but where there was a distinct mystery attached to the affair, and when the name of a popular novelist was also involved, they would certainly sit up and take notice. 

Until the exact truth was known all England would be asking who stabbed Australian Pete and who strangled him. Was it the work of one criminal or of two? Was Peters actually murdered in that room, or had he been carried there, perhaps from one of the other flats, dying or dead? Above all, how did the murderer get into the flat? And also, how did the man who was killed enter?

With this publicity making the task of hiding more difficult, Neville felt that the key to the whole thing -namely the whereabouts of Mary Findon-would be known to him before the public began to snarl, and highly paid writers of editorial articles sat comfortably in their padded chairs asking if the police of England proposed to let a crime of this sort pass undetected, irrespective of whether the victim was a known criminal or a Cabinet Minister.

Then his eyes went back to the woolly dog, around which his imagination had often woven themes. In that case there was obviously a woman's influence at work somewhere. Maybe she killed the Liverpool shipping magnate with her own hands-but who could tell?

At all events the thing in the dead man's hand led, by an easy chain of thought, to a woman. In London at the present moment, as likely as not, there was a child who would recognize that woolly toy as its own, and it would be a strange thing if the child's mother could not explain how the toy came to be found where it was.

Could some other woman have murdered Australian 

Pete and then, to protect her own neck, have lured 

Mary Findon away?

CHAPTER VII:  

(9.30 p. m., Saturday, September 3rd.) 

HE glow of a cigarette in a long thin jade holder came from a deep divan. The lights of the room were cunningly hidden. Before the divan there was a low coffee table on which stood liqueurs, an empty glass, a cup and saucer of delicate china, a half filled ash tray. Heavy portières embroidered with gold dragons hung over the doors. Here and there were excellent copies of famous bits of sculpture; statuettes, daring but in keeping with the taste of this room's owner. It was the fortress of a woman who in her own way loved beauty of a flamboyant type. 

Flames licked jerkily upward in the fireplace, eerily illuminating a large hideous idol that dominated the room, a bowl in which goldfish swam round and round, the mural tapestry, an Eastern gong on a red Chinese lacquer cabinet, and an open fan, the colours of which blended with the strong primitive reds and blues upon dark backgrounds everywhere.

Mingled with the odour of Egyptian tobacco there hung in the air faint traces of incense from a small bronze Buddha. An ebony grand piano stood over in the corner, open, with a sheet of music facing the empty stool; and near it, like some watchful, conscious sentry, 103

was raised a snake whose coils were of carved ivory, surmounted by a lamp beyond which stared the reptile's evil, eternally glittering eyes.

With a soft musical note a little clock drew attention to the speeding minutes. The woman stirred and caught a glimpse of her wrist watch, the hand of which pointed to half past nine.

There was something of the panther in her lithe movements as she slowly raised her head and put one foot to the floor, then sat, amber eyes fixed on a chair while the smoke from her cigarette circled and eddied ceilingward—a man's chair, blatantly incongruous in a boudoir that was even more feline than feminine. When alone, she did not hide the hunger in those eyes, which shone almost yellow in the fitful spurts of flame; eyes about which a fire smouldered; eyes needing no touch of bistre to enhance their charm.

The cigarette ash grew, curled and dropped, splashing after the fashion of a miniature bomb on to the Turkey carpet. The woman drew in her breath more sharply until it became a broken sigh, and her closed hand fell with an angry, almost hopeless gesture into the heart of a cushion. 

Slender and graceful, she rose; stood listlessly, gold dress shimmering; then moved with slow gliding walk to a mirror framed in old beaten brass; and looked into it for a long moment with ironical approval. 

No need for anyone to tell her that the allure of her Spanish mother's blood was there, softened by that of her English father. Against her fine olive skin there seemed to be blue lights in her sloe black hair which,

in disdain of modern fashion, was coiled loosely in the nape of a perfect neck.

Turning away, she gestured slightly with hands which in their way could express more than words; and, pouring out another tiny glass of liqueur, sipped it reflectively.

Walking over to the piano, she sat down, and with eyes half closed drifted into Liszt's "Liebestraume."

But to-night her mood was not to caress the cold ivory keys of a piano. Presently her fingers came down in an impatient deliberate discord. They ached for a touch that was more human. She placed another cigarette in the long holder, lighted it, and appreciatingly touched the curious green scarab that never left her little finger.

Underlying all her poise ran a fierce current of unrest which was the key to everything she had done these last few minutes. Her eyes fell on a gramophone cabinet. At random she picked up a record, placed it on the machine without troubling to see what it was, and dropped the needle into its groove. Then, resting gleaming forearms on the mantel-piece, stared down into the fire. The music ran on, grinding out its mechanical course: 

Ain't she sweet? 

See her coming down the street!

Now I ask you very confidentially- The woman's hands tensed and she stood stiffly erect. 

That brooding fire in the amber eyes flamed. Scattered to the four winds of heaven went her poise. The Span-

ish blood within her was unleashed, and in three swift steps she was at the gramophone. With crude force she thrust back the needle arm and, snatching up the record, hurled it in blind fury at the fire. 

It crashed against the enamel tiles, falling splintered in a score of pieces to the hearth. And lay there.

Soon her eyes were merely smouldering once more, but her breast rose and fell quickly. Both fists were clenched tight. She stood so for a full minute before becoming once more a creature of indolence, in a setting of her own creation. She refilled the tiny liqueur glass and curled herself up luxuriously on the settee. 

When the clock hands indicated fourteen minutes to ten, the door opened.

The woman started eagerly. There was an unguarded glow in her face as she turned. A name was already trembling on her lips, but the light died out of her eyes. They became coldly yellow. 66 ''Lo, Tony!" It was not an unfriendly greeting: just the casual tone reserved for friends. Friends, at least, whom she graded with Tony Parke. She was too exotic to philander with a man who, in her view, resembled a fresh April breeze as much as anything; too calculating to waste the gifts the gods had given her. It was not his hair that her fingers ached to rumple and smooth. Tony Parke knew that well enough. Everyone knew it by now, she supposed. 

Oh yes, life had played a pretty trick on her. For so long she had been mistress of her fate, holding up a finger here and a finger there, calling the tune and, without a qualm, watching them all dance. It had

been a game, demanding infinite finesse, but a game that had been worth while, for at times it had come near to satisfying the storm-torn soul of her. Now she was crawling in the dust. She, Anito Curlew, who had held so many in the hollow of her hand! She was herself enslaved at last in the very love game which she had for so long played with her tongue in her cheek, turning it into a mockery. Oh, the bitter irony! It was as if those affairs of recent years were recoiling upon her like a sword through her body. For Anito knew that, with all her power and all her experience, it was only by a slender hair that she held the man she loved. Or rather, had held him. Perhaps he would come back, but in her heart she was less sure. 

There was another woman, of course, yet for the present she was not thinking especially of that. The really dreadful thing had happened six hours ago, here in this very room. He had been sitting in that big chair which she had come to look upon as his. And then-. 

Ah! It seemed already more like six days ago than six hours. A cold shudder passed through her frame. 

Yet, even remembering what he had done, as she must remember it, to the last moment of her life, she would welcome him back gladly, thankfully-if he ever came back. But would he? Had this not been the end? The look that had come into his eyes during those awful moments still haunted her. It had spelled hatred, if not worse; for love and hate, she knew, were closely akin. But terror was different. Only half a dozen hours ago Anito had shrunk from the sight of terror in a man's face.

And still, in spite of everything, her heart would nearly stop with the suffocating joy of seeing him enter the room again. This was what love had done to her. 

He was like some alluring drug to her being. Even his coldness, save in those rare moments when he held her close and she knew that for a space he was hers, could not alter that. She would go on-could not help-loving him whatever he did . . . excepting one thing alone. Gnawing at the back of her mind always, was the idea of that other woman: a terrible thought, stirring strange disturbing desires. 

Tony flung himself in the big empty chair. "Playing Cinderella! But you fill the rôle charmingly. All alone, this evening, eh?”

The red speck at the end of her cigarette grew, and the smoke sailed upward. "Au contraire." The lie came glibly to her lips. Pride sped it on its way. Utter, empty loneliness for six hours-six devastating hours! "It is the first time I have had ten minutes to myself all day. One needs seclusion at stated periods to build up one's reserves. 

Don't you think so?" 

Tony half rose from his chair. "Shall I clear out, Anito? If you're tired—” "By no means," came from the woman with a light laugh. Another hour of such loneliness would have driven her to the border of insanity. "There's whiskey over there. Won't you help yourself?"

He got up, and from the depths of the cushioned divan she heard the tinkle of a tumbler, the hiss of a syphon.

"You are such a distressingly active animal, Tony, but you always refresh me. When you're here I feel as though I were taking a long walk over the moors in mid-winter. What have you been doing all day?"

Tony sank into the chair again, skilfully balancing the whiskey and soda on one of its arms. "Been on that blessed murder mystery of ours ever since breakfast," he said. "Feeling dog tired, too.

Found myself within a stone's throw of here at last, and couldn't resist the temptation to come into an atmosphere a million miles removed from that which has been oppressing me all day. For forty-eight hours, in fact."

Again the speck at the end of the woman's cigarette glowed redder. "Have they been caught yet?" came from her shortly. "Caught? Who?" 

She waved the long jade holder gracefully. "Someone has to be caught, always, in connection with such an affair, n'est-ce-pas?" 

Tony smiled grimly. How infinitely far from her sphere was a thing like murder! "Quite!" he answered. "And maybe it hasn't dawned on you that one of the people they may take it into their heads to arrest any minute is sitting here talking to you now.' 

"Tony! I don't think I like you. Don't say unpleasant things." "Sorry," said Tony, sipping from his tumbler. "But the fact remains that up to now I've only been able

to keep about two hops and a skip ahead of the best brains in Scotland Yard. As far as I can see, there isn't any logical reason why I shouldn't spend to-night in

Vine Street. Except, of course, for the fact that I have never yet committed murder. It's silly to contemplate the idea of Seward's maid having done it. 

You never saw her, but you can take my word about that. And as for Bill Seward! Well," he held his drink up against the flickering fire flames, "it's laughable!

Yes, even in spite of the seriousness of the situation, it's laughable to think of him having done it. To begin with, he had no reason to, and beyond that, his tastes don't exactly run in that direction."

Tony's eyes were becoming accustomed to the subdued light. That hideous Chinese idol on the right of him seemed to have grown clearer and larger. "Does one know," came the soft, throaty voice from the divan, "what anyone might do in really desperate circumstances?" 

For some absurd reason Tony felt a vague thrill.

It was as if the stage had been set for queer emotions. 

That damned idol with its malevolent expression; the uncertain light in which one saw odd things and imagined others; the faintly scented air; Anito's half- 

Spanish personality; her low musical voice. She was so different from any other woman he had known. She reminded him always of a slow sleepy river of illimitable depth; but a river which, when it arrived at the edge of a deep cliff, would awaken to tremendous power. There could be something dynamic about her if she were really stirred.

Then, for one unpleasant second, Tony found himself wondering, with sickening disloyalty, to what extremes a clean healthy minded fellow like Bill Seward might be driven in really desperate circumstances. And instantly felt rather ashamed of himself. Bill was in a tight enough corner already without his best pals entertaining even a shred of doubt. "In a way, I suppose not," he answered indifferently, and dragged his mind away from such thoughts. His eyes travelled from Anito to the fire; and so, automatically to that which lay in the hearth. "Busted a gramophone record, eh?" he said. "Looks as though somebody had given it a beautiful swipe with a coal hammer!"

He leaned forward and took into his fingers that fragment to which the label adhered. The woman on the divan made a slight quick movement, but checked herself; then lay still, smoking.

And now, as he idly read the name on the label, the thrill that whipped at Tony's nerves was less vague.

This was so uncannily apposite. It flung such a startlingly strong flavour into an atmosphere the dramatic quality of which he had already sensed. It brought him back with a jerk to that bewildering thing from which his mind had barely been able to escape, day or night, since it had crashed into his life. There flickered before his eyes a sharp picture of another room, a man standing with an expression of dread as he looked down at a still prone figure. And in Tony's ears again there seemed to beat the thumping of a pianist in a flat overhead.

Unconsciously his fingers tightened upon the fragment of disc, until it snapped. "Queer!" came from Tony in an undertone. Then, aloud, "Did you break this 'Ain't she Sweet?' record?" 

Anito did not move; the rings and spirals of smoke eddied and ascended regularly. Presently Tony heard her voice, low and vibrant. "Yes, I broke it."

Sheer imagination, doubtless, but there seemed something definitely sinister about the woman as she lay there, in that strange room, dull colours reflected from the barbaric uncut jewels slung carelessly on her neck.

In her tone, too, there was more than a hint of repressed feeling behind those four words. "Deliberately?" He hung fascinated upon her answer. "Yes." "Why?" "Because I hate it!" The quickness with which this last answer came left no room to doubt its truth. 

He held the two shattered remnants of the disc in his hands, turning them over and over as if they might supply answers to a dozen questions that were darting into his brain. "Anito, tell me, why do you hate this tune so much?”

The woman got up from the divan, and for one heavily freighted second he believed that a momentous revelation trembled upon those red lips. Her hand rested lightly at her own throat and then the spell broke upon her laughter. "Why does one love anything or hate anything?

Tony, you are irritating to-night. And I am not in a mood to suffer irritation gladly." She had glided over to the piano and her white fingers drew a few lingering notes from the keys. Then of a sudden she was playing a haunting old Spanish melody. The music rose and fell, tearing at the heart, filling it with passionate longing and unrest. As the notes died away Anito's voice reached his ears. "Music can be a very cruel thing, Tony. Don't you think so?" But she did not listen for his answer. Her mood had changed. This woman with her countless attractive facets was mocking now. She was picking out a tin-pot tune that was the rage in music halls; but broke off mid-way and drifted into the power of Schumann's "Night Watch."

Thereafter Tony did not speak. He sat back, drinking in its melancholy until the woman closed the piano gently. Her eyes, alight with emotion, went to his. "I had nearly forgotten you were there," she said, standing by the side of that coiled snake, one hand resting upon it lightly. "Perhaps after all I am a little tired." "Then go to bed," said Tony, accepting dismissal. 

But he paused. All the time that she had been playing, one question had echoed and re-echoed in his thoughts. 

If ever it was to be answered, this was the psychological moment. "Do tell me, Anito, why did you destroy that record?"

His eyes held hers, challengingly for a space, and in them he saw a quick flash of resentment.

"Do you never want to destroy things that you hate? 

Good night, Tony."

He left her, unsatisfied: perhaps unreasonably so, he reflected. She had a perfect right to break anything she possessed if it gave her the slightest gratification; and with her temperament there was no knowing what she might do. And yet―.

Tony walked on, irresistibly drawn back to that other peculiar thought. Why "Ain't she Sweet?" of all the records in the world? And her flat refusal to talk about it! One could understand that in a way, simply because Anito Curlew was Anito Curlew. There was always something intriguingly elusive about the fascinating woman. One seemed to get within sight, so to speak, of that which was going on behind those yellowish amber eyes of hers-and then the impression wavered. 

The night wind played agreeably on his temples.

He decided that his imagination was perpetrating tricks on him. Bill had been right when he spoke about one being aloof from other murders. This flat affair had sunk right in. It permeated his thoughts from first thing in the morning until last thing at night. He couldn't get away from it. No wonder that in such a room as the one he had just left, where an undercurrent of the mysterious was everlastingly suggested, he had given too free a rein to fancy. 

He even began to persuade himself now that a musician of her standing might revolt at the very sound of "Ain't she Sweet?" when it began to get on her

nerves; and to make certain that it could offend her no more she smashed it. "Do you never want to destroy things that you hate?'" Was there an ominous tang in those words of hers? What if she were driven to a last extremity of desperation! He visualized her slender white fingers tightening about a throat-and then laughed aloud, so that a passing pedestrian turned and concluded that 

Tony had dined too well.

But Tony did not notice that. He was still only emerging from the witchery of Anito and her subtle surroundings. Normality was returning. Of a sudden he felt the pangs of a keen appetite, and half a minute later sat back in a taxicab, cheerfully contemplating an imminent attack upon welsh rarebit and good beer.

He caught himself humming an air-a catchy familiar thing. Then stopped and swore.

It was the tune that had driven Anito to demolish a gramophone record.

T 

CHAPTER IX:  

(11 a. m., Monday, September 5th.) 

ONY was in the office of the Monitor, glancing critically at a signed article of his in that morning's paper. 

Unpunished Murderers

By Anthony Parke. 

It dealt with some of the more spectacular crimes of recent years, in connection with which the culprit had never been brought to the scaffold, in spite of all the skill and tireless pertinacity of Scotland Yard which never forgets and never forgives until the scales of justice show an even balance.

Still reading, Tony extended one arm as a bell jangled near. "That Mr. Parke?" It was the voice of the office switch girl. "Yes." "One second. Someone wants you.'

There was the faint click of a plug being inserted, and then: ""Ellow!" 116

"Hello, who's that?" "I told 'er once, I want to speak to Mr. Anthony 

Parke, if he's there." "Well, this is Mr. Parke." "Oh!" A pause. "Is that you, sir?”

The newspaper man's lips twitched. The voice was unfamiliar a boy's voice. 

"Yes. What is it?" "Why-why, I wanted to 'ave a talk to you, sir, if you don't mind.”

Parke put down his newspaper and drew the transmitter a little nearer. "Well, you're doing it, sonny. Who are you?” "Wait a bit, sir. Is it you what wrote in the paper about murderers than didn't get hung?" "Hanged." "Pardon, sir?" 

Parke grinned. "Never mind. What is it you're after?" "Just a minute, sir. Are you the same gen'leman who was in the flat when that feller Peters was found murdered?"

A swift change swept over Parke. His whole being became concentrated on the telephoned vibrations of a distant voice. "Yes-yes, my lad. First of all, tell me who you are?" 

There was hesitation, and then: "I'd rather tell you after I've talked to you, sir," came the answer a little dubiously. "I don't want-" 

Again a pause.

"Don't want what?" There was something about the boy's voice that intrigued him intensely. "Well, I'll wait till I've seen you, sir, if you don't mind." 

Parke held back an exclamation of impatience. Boys wouldn't ring you up in connection with a murder case and refuse to give their name unless they had something to say; and any single scrap of information dealing with the Mayfair mystery, however trifling it might seem to other people, was of more interest to Anthony Parke even than to Scotland Yard itself. For they had the crimes of a whole country on their shoulders, whereas Parke was mightily concerned for the present only with this one. “All right,” he answered, hiding the eagerness which had taken possession of him. "Come straight here and see me at the office. I'll wait for you." "I reckon it'll be worth about a quid," came the voice over the wire. "If it is, will you-” "Don't you worry about that, sonny," Parke strove to convey assurance over the wire. "If what you've got to tell me is worth it, I'll pay you well." "I'll come now, sir.
