It'll take about 'alf an hour or more by 'bus. You'll be there all right, guv'nor?" "Listen, sonny. Don't come by 'bus," Tony said distinctly. "Jump into a taxi and tell the man you've to reach the Monitor office quick." "I 'aven't got any money, sir. Only 'bout a shilling." "Don't you worry about money. Do exactly as I say. Tell the cabbie I'll meet you at the door and pay him. Now, hurry up."

"Right-o, sir." And a distant receiver was hung up.

Parke looked up at the clock, leaned back in his chair, got up, and sat down again, all the time fidgetting with a cigarette. Something was a-quiver within him. His brain was racing over the possibilities of the situation. Up to now no new light had been thrown on the tragedy. It remained inexplicable as during those trying hours on Thursday night when the vengeance of the law was first being set in motion. Tony 

Parke was too old at the news-gathering game not to know that this might lead to nothing. A chance remark the lad had heard-possibly one of the countless guesses that had been made in London alone about the flat murder during the last few days, utterly futile and unhelpful. 

And yet, instinct keyed Tony Parke up. There was always the millionth chance. At one instant the idea of telephoning to the Yard and inviting Inspector Neville to come along for the interview flashed into his head. Perhaps it would be better to make sure the boy had picked up some useful scrap of information, instead of a mere red herring, before troubling Neville, who was an extremely busy man; and yet the conviction was growing in Parke that his time and money -or rather the office's money-were not being entirely wasted in the matter of some unknown youngster's journey through London in a taxicab. 

But he did not communicate with Neville.

A few minutes later found him standing before the portals of the Daily Monitor building, anxiously scanning the passing traffic for anything that looked like

a taxi with a boy in it. He threw his cigarette away, lighted another, and then threw that into the gutter, consumed with increasing doubt as the minutes sped. 

It was nearly half an hour already since he had bid the lad hurry up. Perhaps, after all, by some miracle the youngster was the one person in all the world who could throw a faint light on the mystery-and perhaps also, at the last moment, for one of twenty possible reasons, his courage had failed. Maybe he was sitting down to dinner even now, somewhere in Wanstead Flats or Bermondsey, or wherever else he happened to live, sadly perturbed about the "quid” he was missing, but thankful he had kept his mouth shut after all. "If he doesn't come, I shall know it was important," 

Parke muttered. "Unless that kid was pulling my leg." 

He reflected for a moment. "No, on second thoughts, he wasn't trying to pull anybody's leg. His voice didn't sound that way. You can tell, even on the tele—”

A taxi swerved out of the traffic and stopped with a jerk. Even before it came to a standstill the face of a shock-headed youngster of fifteen-a keenly alive face framed with hair of that peculiarly red quality which shingled women the world over might envywas poked out of the window. His eyes met those of 

Tony Parke with swift understanding. Hadn't the gent said he would be there waiting! 669 "'Alf a crown, guv'nor. That's what he promised to drive me 'ere for," was the business-like greeting as one none-too-clean thumb was jerked in the direction of the chauffeur. A taxi all to himself across London town had been an elegant adventure in itself, to be entered

upon with due regard for the price. But those alive grey eyes of his were on Tony Parke as the taxi was paid for and dispatched. The first flashing glance had been that of the true Cockney gamin-intuitively searching as it was mercilessly thorough. "Now, sonny," Parke turned to him, not too eagerly, "come along in."

A few paces nearer the great doorway, with its air of importance, the boy suddenly hung back. Parke noticed the check instantly, and made a lightning decision. "Let's go somewhere quiet, shall we?" he suggested easily. "Too many people in there, and we don't want anybody listening, do we? I know!" He crooked a finger to a passing taxi. "Jump in." “Where to, sir?" The boy flashed the question warily. He was by no means sure that trouble in some form was not looming ahead for him. He hadn't known

Parke more than about one full minute, and he was prepared to bank on him-up to a point. But he was taking no undue chances, considering the purpose for which he had come here. Life in the jungle of East 

London, where you keep your wits at work or go under, had taught him much. "My home is not so very far away," said Parke, "and we can be undisturbed there."

The boy got into the taxi-cab, surveying his companion with increasing trust in those big grey eyes of his. The driver steered down to the Embankment, and as the vehicle sped westward Parke lighted a cigarette, wondering how best to play this intelligent little fish

with the nervous energy of a young race-horse. His feeling that after all there might be something in the wind had not diminished. "Ever been in a cab before?" he asked with outward unconcern. "Once. When mother was took to the 'ospital." "Oh dear! Is she all right now?" 

The boy nodded. "That was last winter." "And you're the eldest?" 

Again the boy jerked his red head forward. Each of them, in his own particular fashion, was fencing. "I say, mister, I don't want to have no truck with the cops."

There was something agreeably reassuring in Tony 

Parke's smile. "I'm not a policeman, you know." 

The youngster's gaze was steady. "No, but-" he broke off. "Now, don't you worry," said Parke. "I'll see you don't get into trouble. You needn't even tell me your name until you want to." "They call me Tinker-Tinker Brown," the boy replied. It was a quick compliment the delicacy of which Parke appreciated. He had begun by liking 

Tinker at first sight: he was beginning to like him still more. "All right, Tinker," he said. "Now we're friends, eh? And what you say is just between you and me." "That's right, sir." The lad was beginning to relax and snatch glances out of the cab window. Al-

ready the driver was threading his way past the 

Houses of Parliament and onward. "Shall I tell you now, sir?" "Wait a few minutes, Tinker. We'll be all by ourselves in two ticks, and then you can talk as much as you like." "You live up 'ere, sir?" He had begun to survey his companion with new interest. Tony Parke was dressed by a good tailor, and carried his clothes with an air. A toff who looked like that, and threw money about on taxi-cabs as if it was nothing, commanded even Tinker 

Brown's awe. "A little further on, in Mayfair."

Tinker settled back on the cushioned seat and watched the streaming traffic. By the time they reached their destination the boy had summed Parke up sufficiently for his purpose and was less taut mentally. He followed the other indoors without hesitation. "There," said Parke at last. "Sit down and make yourself comfortable. Take it easy.' 

Tinker perched himself awkwardly on the edge of a deep lounge chair, hugging his cap. "It's like this, sir," he began with a hint at obstacles, "I ain't done nothin' wrong, see? At least I ain't sure, but maybe the p'leece—” "Do stop bothering about the police, Tinker. I'm quite sure you've done nothing wrong. And even if you had, you can take it from me I've got some pretty influential friends at Scotland Yard. In any case I'll stand back of you. We're friends, remember."

Tinker wiped the palms of his hands on his trou-

sers. Now it had come to the point he was getting hot. "But don't forget what you said, guv'nor! You promised you wouldn't split, whatever I told you."

Parke began to think rapidly. So far he was utterly in the dark. But how far could he bind himself irremediably to the vow this youngster demanded? In a case of murder, or anything remotely connected with murder, one had to be extremely careful in withholding information. For a moment he felt himself suspended between two difficulties. And he knew, just as 

Tinker was aware, that he wouldn't go back on his word once it was given. On the other hand, Tinker would close up tighter than an oyster if he took fright. "There's only one thing, my son, before you start.

We can't shield anybody in any way if they've committed murder, in case the wrong man got hanged.” 

Tinker's eyes opened wide. "But I ain't murdered anyone, sir." 

Parke chuckled. "I don't suggest that you have, sonny. In fact nobody would. But don't you see, if you told me anything that might lead to the murderer being arrested, I should have to make use of your information." "I don't want to get pinched, guv'nor, see? There's three kids at home and mother's got the rheumatics so's she can't work." 

Parke began to perceive an opening. "Look here, Tinker, I promise to let you go out of here whatever you say to me, and if you wish you needn't even tell me where you live. How will that do?"

"All right, sir. An' if it's worth a quid-" "If what you say is worth the money, I'll give it to you straight away."

Immense relief came into the boy's face. He slid one hand into his trouser pocket and a moment later there was lying in his hot young fingers a plain gold cigarette case, square in shape.

With an effort Parke remained still. 

The boy took the case from one hand and held it out with the other. "Oh, Lord," commented Parke. "Of course you've had this in your hands a dozen times?" "'Course I 'ave, sir. Is it worth a quid to you?” 

Parke made a wry face. "Yes, my lad. And a bit more, maybe. But if only you hadn't fingered it from one end to the other, I'd have been glad to give you twenty." "I ain't spoilt it, sir, 'ave I?" There was real anxiety in his tone-a pound's worth of it. "You haven't spoilt the cigarette case, but I'm sorry to say there can't be any trace of the murderer's fingerprints left on it now." "Then-then I was right, wasn't I, sir? I mean it is the same one, isn't it?" "You can bet your sweet life on that, Tinker. How did you come to get hold of this?" "I found it, sir. At least a feller threw it away." On the last words Tony Parke became acutely intent. "You saw him?” "Yes. He chucked it into a garden. I 'opped in and picked it up."

"I see," said Parke, his temples beginning to throb. "If that's all you've got to be afraid of it's nothing, Tinker. You can take my word for it." He drew out his wallet and deliberately counted five one-pound notes, and placed them on the table by his side. Even when Parke spoke again the lad's eyes were still glued on the money. "Now, son, if you'll help me I'll help you. Far from running any risk of being pinched, you've done the best day's work of your young life, and because of it, you'll be able to help your family as you never have before. You've done the right thing, and I can't tell you how glad I am that you let me know. But you must try to remember every detail of what happened. 

It is very, very important."

Tinker moved in the chair and tore his eyes off the wealth that was to be his. "There warn't any details, sir," he said, slightly disappointed. "'E just pitched it through the rails and I_" "Begin at the beginning, Tinker. Where did this happen?" "Near where they did that feller in, at the flat." "And what day was this?" "Last Thursday night, sir, same's I'm tellin' you. 

I was coming along Grasmere Street-" "Have you any idea what time it was, Tinker?" "Time, sir? Didn't notice that." "Was it dark, then?" "No, sir. Just beginnin' to get a bit dusk, like." "Say eight o'clock?"

"Dunno. I didn't bother about the time." "But, think! What time did you leave work?" "It was my afternoon off. I'd been to see my aunt, and on my way home." "All right. Now carry on." "I sees this feller walkin' along in front of me.

He'd come out of one of the houses there." "Did you notice which house?" "No, sir. I didn't pay partic'lar attention. 'E was goin' along, quick like, a good bit in front of me.

I don't think 'e knew I was there, because the road bends. You know, sir?" "Yes, I know, Tinker. Go on." "Well, he didn't see me, maybe because I was comin' round the curve. But I saw him. He was just passin' some railin's outside a private garden when he stopped, and I saw his arm go out. He chucked this through the railin's, into the bushes there." 

Parke tapped one foot reflectively. "But it might not have been this that he threw, Tinker." "There wasn't nothin' else there, guv'nor. I know, 'cause I looked." "Very well, finish your story."

The lad shuffled in his seat again, warmed up now that he was holding his audience. "When I came up to the spot I had a look through the railin's first. Couldn't mistake the place, see, because it was opposite a pillar box. And then I saw what he'd chucked through. Of course I didn't know that he'd committed a murder, sir."

"Of course you didn't, Tinker. Carry on." "Well, sir, it was lyin' right in the garden, among the bushes. I could just see it, but I couldn't reach it. Didn't know it was gold, either. Thought maybe it might fetch a tanner, understand? And after all it wasn't like stealin'. 'E 'ad chucked it away." "One moment, Tinker. Did you see anyone else about in Grasmere Street?" "No, sir. I don't believe there was anybody there, 'cause I looked before I slips along to the gate. I 'opped in, and slips out again. Then I forgot all about it till I took my coat off before I went to bed. I shoved the case in a drawer, for fear some of the kids at 'ome would get it."

He drew a deep breath and paused. "That was Thursday night, wasn't it, sir? I didn't know anything special about the murder next day. I was busy. I work for a laundry, see, deliverin' the washing on Fridays. And Saturdays, too. It was last night that I got 'old of a paper, and I come over queer when I read that bit about the cigarette case 'aving been stole from the flat where the feller was murdered. 

Dented a bit, it said, near the fastener. You can see it there, sir, like it says in the paper. And I got scared." "You needn't be, Tinker," replied Tony, his eyes on the peculiar dent. "Maybe not, sir, same as you say, but if the cops didn't believe I'd found it-" 

Glancing at the boy's keen ingenuous face, Tony 

Parke grinned.

"I think we can safely assume they wouldn't question it, Tinker." "And besides," the boy went on, "they might say I pinched it, me goin' into a private garden like that." "In the circumstances I'm sure they won't say anything of the kind." "Y'see, sir, I don't want to get the sack. I've 'ad that job at the laundry for over five months now, and it's a good 'un. They pay me twenty-three shillin's a week and-" 

His eyes went to the big grandfather clock steadily and sedately tick-tocking away in a corner. He got to his feet with a jerk. "Oo! it's late, sir." There was alarm in the boy's expression. "I didn't let on where I was goin', and if I don't get back quick-" Shrewdly his gaze went to the five one-pound notes. 

Parke's mind swept to the Daily Monitor office where a dozen youngsters round about Tinker's age were employed as messengers. Not just errand boys. 

They had to exercise intelligence, carrying out somewhat complicated instructions, and at times, in an emergency, act on their own initiative. What a treasure

Tinker would be, fitted into his proper groove on the Monitor! "Sit down, son. I haven't nearly done with you yet. 

And if you're fired from the laundry I'll fix you up with a job which I rather think you'd prefer to gathering up other people's washing. And better pay, too."

Tinker didn't try to hide the dawning hero worship. "I'd like to work for you, sir."

.

"Well, not so fast. You're employed by a laundry at present. I want you to put your mind on last Thursday evening in Grasmere Street. And particularly on the man who threw this cigarette case away. You didn't see his face, by any chance?" "No, he had his back turned. That's why." 

Parke swore softly. Oh, the pity! The infinite pity of it! "Then you wouldn't recognize him again?" "How could I, guv'nor, if I didn't see him?"

Parke beat his clenched fist on the arm of his chair. "The point is you did see him, son. You're apparently the only person in the world, too, who, perhaps unconsciously, has got hidden away in the recesses of his brain some clue to the identity of that person. 

Think hard; what did he look like?” 

Tinker scratched his head in perplexity. "Dunno, sir. It's a longish time since, and though it wasn't dark, it was sort of dusk. He wasn't a little feller. I remember that." 

Parke brightened. "Was he very tall?" "Not exactly very tall, but seems to me he was about as big as you, sir." "How was he dressed? Think of anything-anything that might have stuck in your memory." "Couldn't say what 'e was dressed like now. I b'lieve 'e 'ad an overcoat on though." "Can you say which way he went?" "Yes, I remember that, sir. Afore I 'opped it into the

garden, I watched 'im, see, till 'e was out of sight. At the corner 'e looked back over 'is shoulder for a second, an' then turned to the left."

That didn't help much. One could but turn either to the right or the left at that corner; and all London lay beyond. "But you saw his face then?"

Tinker shook his head gloomily. "'E was a good way off by then. No, it's no use. I ain't goin' to tell no lies about a murder, sir." "All right, Tinker. Nobody wants you to. You've done your best, I'm sure. And earned your fiver. Here it is. Now, what about trotting round with me to the place where you found the cigarette case. I want you to point out the exact spot." "I can do that all right, sir. Same as I said, it's nearly opposite a pillar box."

Parke half closed his eyes. Meticulously he was weighing up the significance of Tinker's story. It was of the first importance-in a way. And yet only in a way, when you came to look at it from every angle. 

The inevitable inference was that the red-headed laundry boy had actually seen the murderer. Almost conclusively the gold cigarette case, with the unmistakable dent on it, proved that much. 

But the boy retained no mental picture of that man. 

And by handling the cigarette case himself probably a score of times during the long interval, quite apart from the rubbing it must have had in his pocket, he had utterly and completely destroyed all trace of any

finger-prints thereon. If it ever bore any. Yes-if?

That was a thing Tony had not thought of until now. 

Every criminal of a higher order than the mere thug knew the advantage, these days, of wearing gloves when he was working. 

If the murderer had taken that precaution, it was quite possible that when Tinker picked up the case it had no finger-prints on it-except those of Seward. 

His, naturally, would be stamped all over it. More damning evidence against Seward in that case. And where there was no big definite proof in a problem like this, the police of necessity fell back on the tiniest details, piecing one fragment of evidence to another, building and building up until at last the structure seemed firm and strong enough to justify them in effecting an arrest.

Tinker's story only seemed to prove that a man walked away from the flat, carrying the cigarette case out with him. There never had been any question about the murderer having gone away. Except perhaps in the mind of Detective Inspector Neville. Poor old Bill again! Even that didn't remove suspicion from Seward.

Because what more natural than for the police to say Bill went away after committing the crime, to dispose of the cigarette case and other things, and then returned? It even fitted neatly in with Neville's theory about Bill.

If only the lad had preserved some recollection of any one distinctive characteristic about the man, it might have been enormously helpful. "Tinker," he said at length, "if I give you my solemn

assurance that no possible harm can come to you in consequence, are you willing to repeat what you have told me to the Scotland Yard detectives?"

The lad was a shade uneasy. Clearly he didn't know exactly what that course might let him in for. And yet he was willing to pin a whole lot of faith to Tony Parke. "If you say it's all right, guv'nor," he said slowly. "Well, you see, there's this," he pointed to the cigarette case, "and it's a rather vital point. You needn't be afraid of the detectives. I'll see you don't come to any harm. That's a promise." "When'll I have to go to Scotland Yard? If I'm not back soon I'll get the sack for sure. They don't stand any messin' about at the laundry." "I'm afraid you ought to go to the police at once, after we've had a look at that place in Grasmere Street. 

I'll tell you what, Tinker." Vaguely, the germ of an idea was forming in his brain. An idea that held almost a definite promise of failure, because everything was so inconclusive. "I think in any case you'd better take the bull by the horns and throw up your laundry job." "It ain't so easy as you think to get another, sir." "I know all about that, but I'm certain I can fix you up at the Monitor office-I'd do it now over the telephone, but we haven't time. If that should fail, I'm sure I have enough influence in one quarter and another to get you a billet at more than twenty-three shillings a week, but meanwhile, just for a little while, I rather fancy you might be a bit helpful to me."

Tinker glanced about him in the unfamiliar en-

vironment of a well-furnished establishment. So far, pushing a laundry barrow was his sole wage-earning accomplishment. "What sort of work, sir?" "Well, I haven't exactly had time to think it out yet, son. But you're engaged, if you like, at thirty shillings a week. How's that?” 

Tinker glowed. He had been wondering for weeks whether he dared ask the laundry people for a halfa-crown rise. "I'm on, guv'nor," was his only retort.

The germ idea-such as it was-in Tony Parke's head was beginning to take more shape. "I'm afraid you may have somewhat long hours; and you'll probably find the work I have for you will be dull-duller, even than gathering up people's washing-unless-unless by any remote chance-" He broke off reflectively. "That's all right, guv'nor," Tinker chipped in. "When do I start?" "You've started," replied Parke crisply. "Now, my lad, let's toddle round to your pillar box."

A few minutes later Tinker was pointing with certainty through the railings of one of the houses in 

Grasmere Street. "There y'are, sir. That's where it was, just at the roots of those bushes. Too far to reach without going inside. I'm quite sure this is the spot." "Be quiet, Tinker. Let me think a minute." Parke's brow became troubled. How little it helped, after all! 

How infinitely little! "He came along this way of

course," Parke indicated the direction, "and at that corner he went to the left, eh? You said so, didn't you?" "Yes, sir. I'd slipped in that gateway and was watchin'."

Parke walked a hundred yards down the street and then retraced his steps, following the route Tinker had taken when he first caught sight of the supposed murderer. Mentally reconstructing the scene, he paused at the bend of the street that Tinker had mentioned, and with his eyes traced the man's route from Regent House, past the pillar box, to the corner round which he finally disappeared. They could visualise the whole thing so that it was maddeningly clear, though, with the exception of the cigarette case remaining, it had all faded as completely as a moving picture fades from the silver screen when the last inch of film has clicked its way through the projector. "And that's that!" Parke muttered. "Come on,

Tinker. We'll breeze along to Scotland Yard now, and see how it strikes the professional brain. And if they think it sheds a lurid light on the situation-well, I'll admit their greatness!"

CHAPTER X: LO CHANG 

(Monday evening, September 5th.) 

RAUCOUS Voice close to Seward's ear made him wince. "Mayfair flat murder-inquest!"

That was an ordeal from which he had only just emerged, on the fourth day after the tragedy, and the atmosphere of the court still clung. 

The newsvendors' bold placards were flaunted at every street corner. Seward could see the pennies rapidly changing hands for the printed sheets. Soon all London would be gloating over those headlines with avid eyes. 

How the pedestrians seemed to snatch their papers!

After holding out one grimy hand for a copper, the hawkers flicked a news sheet from their bundle with mechanical swiftness, and then repeated the same action for the next customer, ceaselessly reiterating their grating cry the while "Murder-inquest!" He supposed people at street corners were always served with their papers in this express fashion, but until now he had never noticed it.

Close by, the Walrus, as Bill and Tony called the 

Scotland Yard sleuth with the drooping moustache, was dogging his foot-steps, making no attempt to conceal 136

LO CHANG 

137 his mission. The effect of having a human shadow near like this was cumulative. Try as one would, it was difficult to forget the detective's existence for more than five minutes, and you had to control a certain sense of exasperation. 

Seward's glance caught a shop where he had his shirts made. He longingly recalled that you could go in by one door, walk straight through to another, and emerge in a street well out of Scotland Yard's immediate vision. But what was the good of doing that, except to secure a temporary respite from surveillance? 

To throw the sleuth off the trail would be easy enough, in many ways, but to do so would be foolish, for several reasons, chief among which was the fact that it would only multiply suspicion.

Once, and once only he served that trick on the Walrus. It was on Friday. The thing had to be done that time. They could pry into his affairs and chase after him as much as they liked now; but his business for a little while, on the day after the murder, had been of such a nature that the police had to be evaded. With a somewhat anxious face the Walrus had picked his trail up again an hour or two later as Seward returned to Tony's flat, and ever since had clung close with brotherly affection. 

No, Bill Seward did not intend to repeat the trick.

As a matter of fact, a sort of understanding was growing up between him and the Walrus. In traffic he paused occasionally, and looked round just to give the poor devil a chance and to show there was no ill feeling.

Presently Seward found himself alone with Stella.

Sleep, for her, had been a mockery these last two nights, yet, despite the blue shadows round her eyes, they held a sweet tranquillity. The peacefulness of this room of hers was but a reflex of the girl herself. "Well, Bill?" Behind the question there was a world of eager interest. He kissed her tenderly. "The inquest was adjourned again," he told her, "pending the discovery of fresh evidence. Now forget about the beastly business. Tony's coming along in a minute. He's speaking to your father in the library.” 

He touched her soft cheek lovingly. "Why are you looking at me like that?" "Don't you know? Can't you understand? Bill, I wasn't there, you see. And there's nothing-nothing whatever-interests me about that case except one issue." "I know, dearest. You mean where I come in." "Of course. Bill, were they did they-" It was not easy to put her fears into words. "Bless your dear heart, they were very decent about it. Only formal evidence was taken last Friday. Today the coroner's questions were more searching. The jury could have returned a verdict of murder, but it is obvious that the police anticipate some new light being thrown on the mystery before long, so the enquiry was adjourned for a week." "A week! What a long time a week can be, Bill! Do you suppose they'll get at the truth by then?"

The man's face was faintly lined as he looked into

LO CHANG 

139 the fire. Some trace of the continuous strain was being stamped there. "At times I wonder if the truth ever will be known, Stella. Unless the murderer confesses, which in the circumstances is indeed unlikely, or inadvertently gives himself away, I don't see how he's going to be found. The detectives will dig, and keep on digging, but at present they are up against it." "Don't think I'm losing courage, dearest," came from the girl. "But that is one of the things I'm worrying about. If they can't get hold of Mary Findon, is there any danger that they might try to make a case out against you?"

Bill pondered, not upon her question, which he had already looked at from every possible angle without coming to any satisfactory conclusion; but on how to answer Stella. "Everyone," he said, "realises the absolute necessity of finding Findon before we can possibly learn precisely what happened. On Thursday night, when we were all at the flat, it never crossed my mind that she could be connected with the murder in any way." "You think so now?" "Well," Bill answered carefully, "how far she may be mixed up in it I can't guess, but I never will believe that she herself has committed the crime unless she actually confesses. I will admit, however, that each additional hour she stays away makes it more convincing that the girl could at least put Scotland Yard on the right track."

Tony entered the room, following a distinguished

looking grey-haired man who stooped slightly. The latter nodded to Seward, and in passing gave him a friendly tap on the shoulder. "Well, my boy, no fresh news?" "Nothing of importance," Bill answered. "The evening papers are screaming for the head of somebody on a charger, but, as they don't indicate whose head, that doesn't expedite matters much." "Daddy, there's something I talked over with Bill at lunch," Stella said, "and we decided to consult you.

We can trust Tony, I know. It's about Lo Chang." 

Mr. Merlyn was polishing his glasses with a handkerchief. "Lo Chang?" He breathed on one of the lenses. "And what has that old rascal been up to?" "You tell daddy, Bill," said Stella. "I hate the very thought of creating suspicion against him.” "Suspicion?" This quickly from her father. "I hardly think one can go so far as to call it suspicion," Seward put in. "But in view of the complete mystery that surrounds this Peters affair, I feel we ought at least to put the thing squarely up to you."

Then coldly, and without embroidery, he told how the Chinaman had been sent to the flat in Regent House at about the time the murder was committed, and of his excuse for not delivering the letter.

The suggestion obviously worried Mr. Merlyn. "There is nothing whatever of the criminal in Lo 

Chang," he said, after a moment's reflection. "I can vouch for that, after knowing him a good many years."

LO CHANG 

141 "But," it was only because of her love for Bill that Stella forced herself to ask the question, "suppose when he got there he found there were burglars!" 

Tony, to whom all this had come out of a clear sky, suddenly gripped the arms of his chair tightly. A thin vein on his forehead began to stand out. "And there were burglars," the girl went on. "We know that much. Daddy, dear, if it happened, how far would Lo 

Chang go? What did he do?"

Her father stared straight before him in silence for a full minute. The suggestion had a sinister ring about it. Mr. Merlyn knew the Chinese too well to dismiss it summarily. And yet Lo Chang had consistently given him devotion for full fifteen years. "I see," he said quietly. "You know of nothing whatever beyond this that would seem to implicate him?” "Nothing."

Mr. Merlyn nodded. He was thinking of the ease with which the hounds of the law could be brought baying at Lo Chang's heels, perhaps unjustly; and of the undoubtedly perilous position in which Seward stood the man his daughter loved. Bill himself hardly seemed to realise the gravity of the situation. Nobody who knew the fellow intimately would suggest his guilt, but the police did not know Bill; and Mr. Merlyn, after first learning the extraordinary circumstances of the case, had dreaded the moment when they might take his future son-in-law into custody. "Well, Bill," he said at last, weighing the delicacy of the position, "we must go at this without fear and

without favour. You and I must put all our cards flat down on the table, face upward. What action, exactly, do you suggest?" "Why, as things stand I don't think we can decently do anything." 

How like Bill Seward! Stella's lips moved, but she did not speak. "You are sure about that, my boy?" "Quite," replied Bill, "because there's nothing definite to pin on to him. He'd get a square deal if we told Neville, but for the moment somehow, I feel it would be rather unsporting to drag old Poker Face into it without at least something a bit less fanciful than the present idea."

There was obvious relief in Mr. Merlyn's expression. He had tried, sincerely, not to prejudice Bill one way or another. "Nobody here," put in Tony, "is as familiar with the mentality of the Chinese as you, Mr. Merlyn. If he knows anything about the murder, would he be likely to admit it? To you?" 

Stella's father shook his head slowly. "You may know an Oriental for half a century and still not be able to determine what is going on inside his head. The whole outlook on life of the Chinese is so different from ours. Death itself doesn't mean very much to them individually. Moreover, if, in doing this thing, Lo Chang had been actuated solely by the desire to steal-a preposterous notion when one bears in mind his consistent, innate honesty-I believe that he

LO CHANG 

143 would have admitted the truth when I faced him with it." "I'm not thinking of theft, sir," said Tony. "I'm wondering, suppose his only motive was to protect 

Bill's property-Stella's new home?" "If that were so," replied Mr. Merlyn, "I'm afraid

Lo Chang's mind might work in a more subtle way. 

In such circumstances he might keep his secret for ever." "And if Bill were arrested?" The question shot across from Tony. "In that case, unless I know nothing of Lo Chang, he would come to me with the truth." 

An unlighted cigarette lay between Tony's lips. He was trying in as detached a manner as possible, to assay the precise significance of Lo Chang's introduction into the situation. It was one more complication, and, unless old Poker Face could be induced to talk, it left them as far off a definite solution as ever. 

Of indefinite solutions, Tony could propound several, all in the realm of pure speculation; but in his heart of hearts he sensed that the real answer must come with the roping in of Mary Findon. With her as a pawn, the police might immediately be able to make one big sweeping move in this game of life and death -if she were still alive. Was she alive? He began to doubt it. Suicide, maybe. Or, if it was a man who ended 

Peters' life, might not he have treated Findon in the same way to ensure her eternal silence?

And yet-there were other slender trains of thought

that came back hauntingly to Tony Parke. Dismiss them as he would, they crept into his consciousness at odd moments when the brain, hammering at the crude facts known to Scotland Yard, wearied of going round and round in a never-ending circle. How much did the police know, after all? Neville was astute. In a dozen cases Tony had learned to admire that official's cold, unimaginative way of sifting, sifting until at last the chaff was all set on one side. He was ruthless as he was tireless. His insight where human nature was concerned was uncanny. But even Neville could only deal with factors that were before him. As time went on he would peer round the corners, prying into the secrets of men's lives, ever widening his field of enquiry, but as yet his vision was limited. 

Sitting there, biting on the end of that unlighted cigarette, Tony found himself again pursuing those slender trains of thought which were so far from the ken of Scotland Yard. As far, even, as the idea of a 

Chinaman having committed the murder. And, as ever, he thrust the bare notion aside. Bill's words in that first hour on Thusday night came back afresh to him. 

If only one could remain aloof!

How much easier it all was for the Scotland Yard mind, unfettered by prejudices, likings or dislikes!

Everything they got hold of was thrown into the mill by them, and the grinding of those wheels was impartial as granite. But they had to get hold of something first; had to disentangle, tear and rend, until a stark fact lay bare in their hands. After that they could be trusted to deal with it.

LO CHANG 

145 Out of these jumbled reflections a single thought suddenly emerged clear. "By the way," he asked, "is music, I mean European music, meaningless to Lo Chang?" "Not meaningless, perhaps," replied Mr. Merlyn, "but I fancy he would have little interest in it. Why?" "Nothing much. Just a notion that strayed into my head. Surprising how many silly ideas bob up that way." "It is all like a crossword puzzle created in a nightmare," agreed Stella's father. 

At that moment Tony was thinking of impressions left a score of times every day, by the yellow fingers of Lo Chang, on glasses, tables and chairs; and also he was thinking of a kris, the handle of which might be half telling a tale, revealed by modern scientific methods. Only half telling it. For if on the kris, or elsewhere in flat 13, there were finger-prints other than those of Bill, their value was negligible until the experts could compare them, whorl by whorl, line by line, with those of some known individual.

He lowered his voice instinctively, and for a time the boy listened avidly, his big eyes wide open. Within reason he would have done anything in his power. 

But before long it became apparent that Tinker didn't understand how he could achieve that which he was

being asked to do. With a feeling akin to despair Tony saw this, but stuck to his guns. "Now, my son, off you go," he said, "and for goodness sake don't throw. cold water on it. Slender hopes come off sometimes. If there's any chance whatever of success, it's worth while."

The boy nodded, but without undue keenness. In that moment something in Tony Parke wavered; came near to breaking. The utter futility of his scheme became overwhelmingly apparent.

He had thought of it in the first place with his own enthusiasm aflame and the spur of loyalty digging deep into his side. Imagination, run riot, had tinged the idea with vague possibilities; loyalty had driven him to snatch in desperation at anything and everything for 

Bill Seward's sake. 

Tony Parke had awakened each day, ever since

Thursday, with a foreboding in regard to Bill. He knew the ways of Scotland Yard. They had to carry on according to their own view. He could cite a dozen murder cases where the detectives were quite sure of one man's guilt and still left him apparently free to go and do whatever he wished, perhaps for weeks. They themselves might be satisfied that he was the murderer, but that wasn't enough. Not nearly enough. 

Their real job was to wait until they had sufficient evidence to convince twelve men in a jury box. An arrest before that moment, whatever their own personal convictions, might well lead to failure, for, according to the law of England, no man could be arraigned twice on the same charge of murder.

TINKER'S VIEW 

159 That diabolical net of circumstances which fate threw over Bill in the first hour was there yet. Gaps still existed in its meshes; but cunning men (skilled in their craft in spite of all the gibes and taunts which were sometimes thrown at Scotland Yard because they were not impetuous) were working ceaselessly, seeking to mend every rip and tear in that net. He hadn't spoken to Bill of this, but there had been moments when the thought of what it all might lead to had nearly driven him into a panic.

These things flooded his mind now. He had gamely held on to hope by the skin of his teeth, doggedly bolstering up his own spirits, chipping Bill now and then and hiding his secret fear. But in this hour he could visualise circumstances in which Scotland Yard might stay its hand no longer.

It was a puzzled Tinker Brown who went away. Out in the street he pushed back his cap and, as ever in moments of dubiety, scratched his head. He, too, was beginning to see there was something radically wrong with his new guv'nor's scheme.

I 

CHAPTER XI:  

(Tuesday afternoon, September 6th.) 

F you turn off Brewter Street, Spitalfields, into

Ship lane, you will there find peace. Ship lane leads to nowhere in particular. At the end it winds and narrows to a passage, with an upright iron bar in the centre, so that not even a fish hawker may take his barrow past that way.

The road is cobbled and fairly well lighted in these inquisitorial days when constituted authority insists on interfering with the lives of others. Once upon a time belike you would get bludgeoned for the sake of your coat and that which its pockets contained, if you were so foolish as to wander down that lane after sundown; for even a cut-throat must live somewhere, and in those good old times a man wanted by the law could keep his head on his shoulders for many a long month by seeking seclusion in the warrens in and about Ship lane.

But all that is changed. Some of the very best hiding places have been ruthlessly torn down, and the street of many yesterdays has a comparatively open and smiling countenance. It may be as well to button your coat up carefully before you pass through, but otherwise the chances are that if you don't interfere with anyone, you will be allowed to go unmolested. 160

Just beyond the Red Lion, a hostel which is steeped in rich tradition (though, since being rebuilt it has lost its fine savour) there is a poky shop where a hatchet-faced woman sells groceries in small quantities, candles, string and such like.

Then come two cottages, the worse for time's ravages, after which you may reach an old iron and junk store. Here, like a human spider, lived Izzy, amid an astounding accumulation of things for which a host of somebodies had no further use; and most of which things you would conclude were hardly saleable. 

Broken fenders, piles of soiled newspapers, cog-wheels with teeth missing, iron hoops now rusted, a chair or two with seat missing or back broken, and heterogeneous bundles.

But the junk was of less interest than Izzy himself-long-nosed, hawk-eyed, stooping. One gathered that his home lacked a bathroom. He was old and sinful. That fat wallet tucked so carefully away upon his person represented only that which he counted as petty cash in this clandestine business of his. One day he would retire and run no further risks; but first he must make a little more money. Yes, just a little more. 

Toothless, he sucked at an empty pipe for its flavour alone, sitting on a chair well within the shadows. Izzy loved the shadows. You might have imagined he had picked his wardrobe at random from the flotsam and jetsam thrown up under his roof, but there you would have misjudged him. That long coat had covered his back for ten full years while he kept vigil there, and once in the long ago he had paid sixpence for

the bowler hat which now looked a rusty green. 

An obscure character, Izzy? Not at all. He had many friends, or rather business acquaintances. But they did not always come here boldly, with God's beautiful sunlight shining upon them. And even then the elect watched for one of two signals as they poked their noses into his doorway. Quite simple signals, these. One was a thumb held up, the other a thumb pointing downward.

Nothing could be more easily understood. If Izzy had reason to suspect that the police were in an inquisitive frame of mind, he depressed that dirty thumb, and the visitor shuffled along, down Ship lane, to whatever other sphere interested him. But if the thumb bade the man enter safely, it was not of broken fenders nor bundles of rags that they talked. 

Izzy was a shrewd old fence. Once, in his sixties, he had been bitten at his profession (though that was not the first time he had seen the inside of a prison), so that now he was cunning as a weasel. One had to be, especially as plain clothes men came fussing about his store occasionally, poking among the rubbish, emptying bundles and raking them over as with a fine toothed comb, and finding nothing that an honest old junk dealer might not legitimately harbour beneath his roof, his high pitched voice uttering protestations the while.

Latterly, Izzy had been especially wary. It would be a sad misfortune to be imprisoned at his advanced age. He liked to keep in with the Scotland Yard folk. 

He had given them a tip or two (where the effort cost

him nothing, or gratified a desire for revenge) and they appreciated his co-operation. One couldn't exactly say that they closed their eyes in consequence, but it paid to keep on a friendly footing with the "busies." 

Izzy was far too foxy to be caught the same way twice. Somewhere, of course, a sword might be hanging ready to fall on his defenceless head, but he took few chances.

There were secret hiding places here of which he alone knew, where a few rings or gold trinkets might lie for an hour or two, but they never remained there long; and, mostly, such things did not come to his store at all. Within that darkened doorway Izzy preferred to make verbal arrangements only, arranging the traffic among others in stolen articles. 

Down Ship lane there drifted a thin slip of a 

Cockney youth with restless roving eyes and bulges about his pockets. Emerging from the narrow passage where the iron upright barred traffic, he glanced over his shoulder, saw only a few children about their business of playing, and two garrulous women leaning out of opposite windows conveniently situated for such a neighbourly act, discussing their affairs and those of others.

The youth passed on, as if making for the comforts of the Red Lion, but at the entrance of the junk shop he paused, and, after one more rapid survey of the lane, entered.

Izzy had given no signal to him. That was reserved for certain initiated individuals only. All the world was welcome to come across the threshold of an honest

trader, on legitimate matters. Izzy's sunken eyes were fixed on the Cockney youth, who nodded, nervously. “Well?” The junk dealer had a guarded way with strangers. The heavy lids blinked like those of a vulture, over lack-lustre eyes. There are paths in the underworld which one must tread gingerly.

The youth came nearer. His voice was husky and barely audible. "I've got some stuff—”

Izzy removed the pipe from his mouth and laid it carefully on a ledge. "And who might you be?" "That don't matter," ," the youth answered. "My name's Smith. A pal of mine told me to come to you."

For a few moments his frail body was shaken with coughing. He looked consumptive and starved, but the baleful eyes of Izzy only saw a man who would be thankful to sell anything he had for next to nothing. 

Such clients brought cheer to the old man's heart. He appreciated their position-and took the fullest advantage of it. "Pinched the stuff?" Izzy blinked disapprovingly.

His tone implied that there were men who even did such things as that, though it was no use bringing tainted merchandise to him. 

The man who called himself Smith shook his head and again coughed. "No, I found it, straight.”

Izzy's lip twisted. New clients generally said that, or something equally foolish.

"Well, I can't waste all day with you," Izzy declared. "Show it to me."

From his pockets the man produced a miscellaneous collection: half a dozen gold and onyx seals, a small gold patch box such as the beauties of other years carried, a gold and ebony box, a miniature silver candlestick with snuffers attached, and a flask bearing a crest.

He set them down on the corner of a table, next to an iron pan which had lost its handle. "That lot's worth a good bit of anybody's money," he said with a crude touch of the salesman. 

Izzy fingered them one by one. He lingered especially over the seals and the engraved flask, produced a pair of steel rimmed spectacles, and examined the things more closely, with an air of disdain that had become seasoned with long years of practice. "Found 'em, eh?" he crooned, then peered over the top of his spectacles at his client. "They were lying right on the footpath waiting all convenient for you to pick 'em up, I'll bet. Eh? Eh?" 

Smith nodded. "That's right, mister. How about a couple of quid' for the lot? Blime, I can do wiv it." 

Izzy spent another three or four minutes, turning the things over, though he had seen all that was necessary in the first quick glance.

He was trying to make up his mind. A situation such as this needed careful thought. Lying under his nose were the things missing in connection with that Mayfair flat murder.

There wasn't any doubt about it. Izzy was an expert in his own profession, having been a receiver of stolen property for more than a quarter of a century; and days ago his experienced mind had automatically tabulated this list of articles. 

And here was a man, shaking with nervousness, obviously so hungry that, if driven hard enough, he would part with everything he had placed on the table for three half-crowns. Izzy had a wonderfully accurate way of gauging such matters. "Couple of quid, eh?" he observed indifferently, pulling a few coppers from a pocket. "I'll make it thirty bob, if you like, but I haven't got so much on me. You'll have to wait while I fetch it."

The hungry man's eyes glistened. Izzy blinked. It was all right. He had played his cards correctly in a decidedly delicate game. Until then he had felt unsure of his ability to handle the thing properly. "I'll come back," Smith said. "How long will you be?" "Just got to go round to where I live," croaked Izzy. "Back in about twenty minutes."

Smith made a movement as though about to gather up his property. "You can leave 'em there," said Izzy, taking a large door key from a nail. "I'm going to lock the place up.” 

And Smith left them there. "I'll be back in twenty minutes, then, see?" he said, in a hushed voice; and strolled on past the Red Lion, in the direction of Brewter Street, with the leisureliness of one who has but to kill time.

Izzy went the other way, bowler hat clamped well over his ears, mouth tinged with caustic humour.

He was back in his shop, sucking at the empty pipe, in less than a quarter of an hour. But for a touch of unrest that showed only in his hands, he hardly stirred until the figure of Smith entered the doorway again. "Got it?" the youth asked eagerly. "Yes, I've got it," agreed Izzy, fiddling with a box of matches and failing to light his pipe. That took up a full half minute. Then he began to fumble awkwardly in his pocket for the money. Before he had time to count it out a stifled exclamation burst from the man who called himself Smith.

A policeman had entered the door and shut it behind him with a deliberate movement. "What have you got here?" asked the officer gruffly. "I don't know, guv'nor,” replied Izzy in an unctuous tone. "This young man is just offering to sell me a few things.' 

The young man was wedged in the shop behind the broad frame of the policeman. "Where did you get these?" demanded the law. "Found 'em, mister," replied Smith, his face white and hands trembling. "S'welp me, I ain't tellin' no lies." "Ain't you?" retorted the officer in his best business manner. "Well, we'll see all about that." Swiftly he had run his eyes over the oddments on the table. "In possession of stolen property to begin with. That'll do just for a start." He placed a firm hand on the cringing youth's shoulder. "Here, you," to Izzy, “do

those things up in a box or something. And you'd best come along to the station. They'll be wanting your statement." "I tell you I found 'em, mister," Smith protested, endeavouring feebly to wriggle out of the conclusive grasp. "I heard," answered the policeman, taking out a pair of handcuffs and slipping one over the youth's thin wrist. "Now, don't try any of your larks on with me." "Ain't that the blinkin' limit!" protested Smith. "Haven't I told you I found the things, lying on the footpath?" "Maybe," remarked the policeman, impatiently watching Izzy doing up the parcel. "There ain't nothin' wrong in findin' things!" Smith went on.

The policeman turned to him abruptly. "When do you say you found them?" "Three or four days ago. Last Friday-no, Thursday. Blime, that's straight!" "Thursday, eh? Same night as the murder!" 

Smith's face went ashen. "Murder!" he faltered. "Wh-what murder?"

Izzy had finished making up his parcel. The policeman drew Smith toward the door. "Come on," he ordered peremptorily.

The prisoner's knees were giving way. The policeman held on to an arm to steady him. With Izzy in their wake, they passed out of Ship lane-a trio attracting many dull pairs of eyes, some of which brightened. Anyone being arrested always stirred a fleeting

emotion. While it lasted it was as good as the pictures, every bit. "Mister," Smith muttered shakily as he shambled along, "I ain't done nobody in. Never did. Found the blasted things, same as I've told you." "That's all right," replied the policeman as they turned the corner. "You tell them at the station, if you like."

Izzy plodded along after them, torn between the joyous reflection that he was making a distinct hit with Scotland Yard, and regret at having missed a beautiful bargain. It nearly made his heart bleed to think of the figure some of those things would have fetched in markets which at least to him were always open. ?

CHAPTER XIII: THE PRISONER 

(Tuesday afternoon, September 6th.)

W ITH abruptness that astounded them, two garrulous old ladies who had called on Detective Inspector Neville at Scotland Yard found themselves dismissed. While one of them was expounding a theory which had seemed most interesting to her, the great detective had applied his ear to a telephone receiver, purred back a brief reply, handed them over to a red-faced man whose manners, in comparison, were deplorably deficient of polish, then made for the door, putting on his hat as he went. 

The garrulous old ladies, who had come all the way from Fulham in consequence of having seen Mary Findon on a Wimbledon 'bus the previous afternoon (they had both noticed her picture in the papers, so there couldn't be much doubt about it), looked along their noses at the red-faced man. "And this is all the thanks I get," said one, "after paying a threepenny fare! No wonder people get murdered in their beds if the police don't pay any more attention to-" "The Inspector's been called away in a hurry, ma'am," explained the red-faced man a trifle wearily, as he ushered them a stage nearer the street. 170

THE PRISONER 

171 "But I thought you were looking for Mary Findon!" "So we are, ma'am. The job is to find her." 

The woman's complexion began to tone with that of the police official. "But what did I come here for?" she retorted. "Wasn't I telling the gentleman-" "I'm sure we're very much obliged to you, ma'am,” the man said patiently. "It all helps." "Helps!" she sniffed and set her course for Fulham with the determination to have her own front door bolt seen to. Nothing like a good sound bolt!

Detective Sergeant Trim did not permit himself the luxury of a smile until then. He, personally, that day, had interviewed a dozen people who had seen Mary Findon, or knew where Mary Findon was, or knew someone going by another name but who might be 

Mary Findon. The resemblance between Mary Fin don and girls long missing from places as wide apart as Bristol and Margate had been pointed out, and letters by every post added conundrums to those already set about Mary Findon.

This sort of thing always happened when a nationwide search for one individual began, but when the only clue for the public was a none-too-clear photograph of a girl with shingled hair and somewhat close fitting hat, giving a tout ensemble which more or less coincided with that of one girl in every score, the police were liable to get their brains tied in knots. 

By this time Neville was a mile away, and in due course he stood before two men of widely differing appearance who, as he came into the barren police sta-

tion room, rose from their hard wooden benches. Unerringly, his glance slid off the oily smile of Izzy to the other unshaven, cowering creature. "Andy Marks, eh?" he said without hesitation. "Gave your name as Smith, too, I hear." "That's right, sir. Didn't want to get into no more blinkin' trouble." "You do seem to find it, don't you, Andy? And giving a false name to the police doesn't help. Just finished a good stretch, haven't you?" "Been out three weeks, sir. Eighteen months you got me, for that Clapham Common job. Can't get work anywhere. I was fair starvin'." "You've searched him?" he asked, turning to the station sergeant. "Yes, sir. He'd got nothing on him, though. Only a broken penknife and a dirty handkerchief." "No keys?" "No, sir." "No, he wouldn't have that key on him now,' 

Neville observed as if thinking aloud. 

He felt a sneaking pity for the coughing, shivering wretch. Some of the human material that passed through his hands was inherently bad-spawn of the gutters which, if you raised it a foot, would slide back automatically to its natural level. But Neville never had put Andy Marks into the worst category. In the first place the fates had set him adrift among the uncharted shoals of London's underworld with a feeble constitution, short sight, an intellect that was not of the sharpest, and the handicap of a convict father.

THE PRISONER 

173 Already he had been in and out of prison half a dozen times, but none of his crimes had been characterized by violence. Murder, one would have thought, was the last thing to expect of him, but the annals of Scotland Yard showed only too often that the most unexpected types ended their lives dangling from the end of a rope. "I'm afraid you're for it this time," the Inspector answered bluntly.

The last dregs of colour had drained from the little 

Cockney's face. "What this about a murder, sir? You can't charge me when I ain't done a thing." 

Neville turned to the smirking old Jew. "What's your name?" he asked sharply. "Isadore Lenski, sir." Izzy wiped his hands on an invisible towel. "Do you know this man Marks?" "No, sir, no. I never have no truck with his kind. 

I'm an honest dealer, sir." "In what?" "Junk, sir. Just junk."

Neville took a pinch of snuff to jog a laggard memory. "I've seen you before, somewhere, haven't I?" "Oh no, sir, not me." "I remember faces, and yours is the sort to stick. 

Have we ever had you inside? Don't tell lies."

Izzy's vulture-like eyes blinked. The conversation was taking a turn he had not anticipated-a personal and distinctly disagreeable turn.

"I did have a bit of trouble once, sir, but it wasn't with you, and it's a long time ago." "And you're an honest dealer in junk now, eh? 

Well, Lenski, tell me what you know about this." "He came into my little shop in Ship lane," said Izzy, "with the stuff that was stolen when that feller was murdered on Thursday in Mayfair." "And how did you know it was the missing stuffyou who keep a little junk shop in Ship lane?" "Well, sir, I read the papers. Business is bad and I get a lot of time on my hands." 

Neville fingered his chin. Izzy was uncomfortably aware that those mild blue eyes were delving down into the secrets of his soul. "All right, go on." 

Izzy, still wiping his hands on the unseen towel, shrugged. "What could an honest man do?" he said. "I fetched a cop."

The little Cockney's hands clenched and his face became convulsed. In another moment he would have sprung at the Jew, but a burly policeman's arm restrained him. "You! You sent for the cop?" Marks screamed; and then became incoherent. The Inspector made a signal and the Jew was shepherded away from danger by the policeman. "Pull yourself together, Marks," said the C. I. D. official. "If they've only been making a tool of you, it won't be so bad. But," gone was the suave Neville manner that led the unwary into pitfalls, for other

THE PRISONER 

175 methods were needed here, "I want the truth or you'll have to go through it. Now, stop snivelling. Tell me, was Joseph Lowther with you on this job? Slim Joe, he's called." 

Marks shook his head. "It wasn't a job, sir. That's what I've said all along." 

Neville was implacable. "When did you last see Slim Joe? You know who I mean. The confidence man who wears an eyeglass."

Either the youth was acting cleverly or there was something he did not understand. "Don't know the bloke, sir. I don't, straight. That's gawspel truth. 'As he committed a murder?" "There's as much evidence against you now as there is against him," Neville answered. "Whatever he's threatened you with, Slim Joe and his crowd can't get at you here. We've got you, understand? And somebody's going to swing for this business. You aren't telling me you did the job by yourself?"

There was abject terror in the shifty eyes of Andy Marks. His knees began to weaken, and he sank back on to the wooden bench shivering, both trembling hands clasped tightly about his throat. "Has he had anything to eat since he came in?"

Neville asked, turning to the policeman in the doorway. "9 "Not yet, sir. He hasn't been arrested long.' "Well, get him some coffee and a sandwich, quick. 

Can't you see the poor devil's on his last legs because his stomach's empty? I'll come back in a few minutes."

Neville went out and, when he returned, stood si-

lently over the pitiful form of the wretched Cockney who gulped his food like an animal. Then Marks faced the C. I. D. man less cringingly. "You can't make me swing for it," he said, fingers twisting and turning the torn old cap in his hands. "You can't, when I ain't done nothing to get pinched for. I tell you I don't know who you're talking about. 

I found the blasted things last Thursday. Wish to Gawd

I'd never seen 'em now. Wrapped up in a newspaper, they were. In a corner, on the footpath." "Where?" "At the corner of Sherwell Road. I didn't know what was inside till I opened the newspaper. 'Strewth, I was 'ungry. Hardly had a bite all day. And thenwhen I saw what I'd found-I could tell it was some things that had been pinched. But I didn't care. I was 'ungry, see. Maybe you've never wanted grub like that. 

I'd 'ave gone straight to a pawn shop and chanced my arm, but they were all shut.” "What time do you say this was?" "Dunno." Marks broke off with a spasm of coughing that shook his frail frame. "Ten o'clock, maybe. 

I threw the paper away and stuck the stuff in my pockets. Asked a gent at a cawfee stall to give me a bob for one of the—” "Never mind that just now, Marks. I want to know a good deal about your movements, earlier than that, on Thursday night." 

Marks' lips were quivering. The fingers of one hand kept stealing to his throat. "You don't-you don't say I killed that bloke in

THE PRISONER 

177 the flat, do you, sir?" The last ounce of his courage was ebbing. "Tell me what you were doing on Thursday evening," was the curt retort. 

Cunning crept into Andy Marks' rat-like eyes. He was here, in the power of the hated police once more, after vowing never to see the inside of a prison again. 

They were clever, these cops, but they didn't know all he knew. Maybe they never would, if he kept his own trap shut. The thought helped him. "You find out!" came from the prisoner obstinately.

Neville's sympathy with the man snapped. Though 

Marks could never have guessed it, the Inspector had, up to that moment, been almost prepared to swallow the fellow's story. For one thing it dovetailed in with the supposition that the burglary at flat thirteen was all a fake a supposition supported by Tinker Brown's discovery of the gold cigarette case which had been pitched carelessly away. And if once they could satisfy themselves at Scotland Yard that burglary was not the motive, the process of narrowing the circle would automatically begin.

But now Andy Marks was knocking the detective's house of cards a-flutter. His sudden stubbornness put a fresh complexion on matters. He knew well enough that they had his finger-prints filed away at the Yard, and that if he had left traces at Regent House they could have laid their hands on him somehow during the five days which had elapsed since the murder. 

Did Andy Marks go with Australian Pete to Seward's flat, one of them having first secured possession

of Mary Findon's latch-key? Or did they happen to meet there, unknown to one another? That seemed more likely. Andy Marks probably entered the premises first, in which case it was he who had forced open the drawers with a poker. If he wasn't wearing gloves for the job, he was cunning enough to have held it with paper or a cloth, to avoid leaving tell-tale means of identification. While he was hastily examining the contents of the drawers, Peters walked in, presumably with the intention of blackmailing Seward.

The rest, Neville argued, was simple to guess at. 

Thinking himself cornered, Marks threatened the Australian, who snatched up the poker to defend himself.

His finger-prints were all over the poker. Marks had to find some other weapon. In a panic he seized the kris off the wall and, during the struggle that followed, thrust it into the other man's back, maybe when Peters had fallen. Then, seeing the man was still alive, and believing that his only chance of getting clear of it all lay in the fact that dead men tell no tales, he knelt on the Australian and clutched him round the throat until he lay still.

If that was so, it didn't matter a row of pins to Scotland Yard whether Seward had been a crook in other years or not. Chance had merely removed a blackmailer from his path, though that same chance had come uncommonly near. to putting a noose round the author's neck, while he actually was sitting within a few yards of this struggle to the death, pounding out the last pages of a novel on his typewriter behind a padded sound-proof door.

THE PRISONER 

179 This, Neville reflected, would incidentally show that the burglary was no fake. According to Andy's own story, he was half starved at the time. Valuable things were lying around the room. It would be the work of a few seconds only to grab anything he could ram in his pockets and then slip away from the scene, closing the door of the flat as he went out. Maybe he threw the cigarette case away as soon as he got outside because he found his pockets were bulging suspiciously. Something had to go, so he pitched the thing through the railings.

These thoughts flashed rapidly through Neville's brain as he stood staring down at the defiant figure on the bench. It was only a theory, and as an explanation it failed to include the disappearance of Mary Findon. And even in that moment which pointed to triumph, he reflected that Dr. Mappin would give him a dig about "crimes of passion." The medical officer had been so cock-sure that Peters did not meet his death at Findon's hands. "Lock him up," said the Inspector, turning to the police station officials. “A charge of being in possession of stolen property will hold him for the present, but before he goes in front of the magistrate in the morning I may have him identified in connection with the Mayfair murder." 

Then he rang up Sergeant Trim at the Yard. "Look up the address of that lad Tinker Brown," he said. "It's somewhere in Fish Street, Whitechapel, but I forget the number. Hang on till you get him. 

Take him in to Headquarters. I'll wait there, how-

ever late it is . . . what's that? Yes, we've made an arrest."

It was ten o'clock that night when a nervous redheaded lad was put in front of half-a-dozen men and asked to point out any one whom he had seen before. 

Five of them, being police in plain clothes, or other equally innocent souls requested to stand there, had easy consciences: the sixth was Andy Marks.

Tinker's eyes ranged the six. He looked from them to the Inspector. "You mean the bloke what committed the murder don't you, sir?" "Never mind that, Brown," snapped the C. I. D. official. "Just do as you're told." "Well," declared Tinker, his eyes fixed suspiciously on the craven Marks, "don't know as I've ever seen any of 'em before."

Neville bit his lip. He had been aware that there was little hope of the boy conclusively identifying Marks. From the first Tinker had explained that the light was failing while he hid in a gateway and watched the progress of that mysterious man to the end of 

Grasmere Street. True, the man had glanced back at the corner, under the glow of a lamp, but that was some distance away. "You are quite sure. Look carefully." "Lookin' don't make no difference, sir," replied 

Tinker. "It's true what I said." "All right, my lad. You can go now."

Tinker sped away, relieved to be free from the

THE PRISONER 

181 dreaded influence of cops. It was true that they had treated him with no unkindness since he became mixed up in this murder business, but there was a sinister flavour about police stations. Also the face of one of those six men still haunted him. "O-oo!" he muttered, hurrying along, "I wonder if they're goin' to 'ang 'im!" 

Neville, also, on his homeward way, was wondering whether they were going to hang Andy Marks. If so, the end was already more or less in sight. Less rather than more, as an actual matter of fact. A lot of evidence remained to be dug out yet. They were only on the outer edge of it so far. Still, perhaps they had their man under lock and key, and that was a big part of the battle. In the police court to-morrow morning he would be remanded. That would give them another seven days at the Yard. If there wasn't enough evidence to justify the capital charge being brought against the prisoner by them, he would be remanded once more, and if Marks committed the crime they would have him nailed before long.

A sordid end to a case which had begun by being peculiarly interesting. One which had fooled him completely for a time, and even now-if this really was the beginning of the end-he had only stumbled on the solution of the problem by sheer luck. 

But was this to be the solution? Detective Inspector

Neville asked himself that question a number of times as he walked a large part of the way home. He might have answered himself in the affirmative with assurance in his younger, immature days at the Yard, be-

fore bitter experience had rammed home the lesson that a game of this sort was never won until the verdict had been given. But now he had mental reservations. 

For the moment he dared not think that they had blundered upon something important. Too often he had seen big fish slip out of the net after it had seemed to be closing upon them perfectly. And by big fish he meant actual murderers of whose guilt he was morally certain. And some of them were rubbing shoulders with the multitude out in the world to-day just because the law of England demanded proof up to the very hilt before one of her subjects could be led to the scaffold.

While Neville walked home, Andy Marks was sitting in a cell, head propped up by his elbows. He, too, was wondering whether they would hang him. Every time his eyes closed he fancied he could feel the jerk of a tightened rope, and he wanted to scream. They'd got him, and he couldn't walk out. This, too, after he'd made up his mind to get to the colonies somehow and make a new start.

Then, as he leered up at the little trap door through which eyes sometimes peered at those within, a twisted grimace, grim caricature of a smile, appeared on his lips. 

If he'd got the nerve to keep his mouth shut he might be outside again soon. Very soon.

But-panic seized him again—if they worked up a charge of murder against him, how near would they get to-to? His hands clutched his throat once more. He

THE PRISONER 

183 jumped up and beat the impregnable door. For the first time in his life there was dawning within him the real passionate lust to kill, for killing's sake. To wreak vengeance on Izzy, the fence.

CHAPTER XIV: ENTER, JAMES BLENKIN 

E (Tuesday afternoon, September 6th.)

ARLIER that afternoon Detective Sergeant Trim had a visitor. He passed a rapid professional look over the young man who gave the name of Blenkin. A shop assistant perhaps, or of that type. 

The fellow's manner was inclined to be nervous, but that alone did not mean much at Scotland Yard. Sometimes old women who had only lost an umbrella came here looking as scared as if they had just picked the 

Chief Superintendent's pocket.

This chap was about twenty-five, had cut himself twice while shaving that morning, had a dab of paint on his coat sleeve, wore a Freemason's gadget on his watch chain, and was stockily built. Quick tempered, probably, but decidedly not of the criminal ranks. "You can't see Inspector Neville just now," said 

Trim. "He's busy." "Then I'll wait," replied Blenkin doggedly. "I don't promise you can see him for a long time," 

Trim pointed out. "I'll wait till I can," retorted Blenkin with the air of one who has made a resolve and means to carry it out. "What's your business with Inspector Neville?" 184

ENTER, JAMES BLENKIN 185 

"It's private. At least, I prefer to discuss it with him alone." He hesitated for a second. "You might tell the Inspector it's about Miss Findon, though."

The Sergeant opened a large book in which he had written so much about that elusive person. "If you've got something to report, you'd better tell me." "Didn't I tell you it's private?" Dawning choler showed in the young man's eye. The Sergeant thought it over, coughed twice, and strode away. Three minutes later Blenkin was alone with the man he sought. 

Then his nervousness became more apparent. "I thought I'd better come and see you, sir. My name's Blenkin-Jim Blenkin." "So I am told," replied the Inspector affably. "And what do you know about Mary Findon?" "She's my sweetheart, sir." 

Neville fingered the snuff box within his waistcoat pocket. "Sweetheart, eh?” His eyes were caressing the young man. "You've left it rather late, haven't you? This is

Tuesday and we've been combing all England for five days in the hope of somebody giving us information which would help." 

Blenkin swallowed and fidgetted in his chair. "Maybe. But there wasn't any reason why I should come before. No special reason, that is." 

Neville's interest was deepening. "But there is now, eh?" "Well, in a way. I work for Graham and Thatchers, the grocers near Grasmere Street. That's how I got to

know her. She comes in there nearly every day—or rather used to before this happened. And two or three of the other fellows there knew her name. Friends of mine. I'd told them, you see." "Told them you were going about with her?" "Yes. And now, well-" he broke off, drawing one hand to and fro on the arm of his chair. "They're beginning to talk?" suggested Neville. "Yes." The lines about his mouth hardened. "Not exactly telling me to my face that I know where she is even if—if something's happened to her. And I don't, sir. I wish they'd got the pluck to come out with it straight, but you can see what they're thinking. They suspect me. Some of the questions they've asked show what's in their thoughts. I've had enough preying on my mind this last few days without that worry too. So 

I came to you, sir." "Very sensible of you, Mr. Blenkin. But what exactly do you mean by things 'preying on your mind' this last few days?" "Why, isn't it enough when all this sort of thing happens to the girl you're in love with?" "Of course. I only wanted to be sure there was nothing else. Now tell me, when did you last see her?" "Tuesday night-two days before she disappeared." "And you have no idea where she is now?” 

The man's face changed. "I'd give my right arm this very minute, if only I knew." 

Neville nodded in sympathy. "You're very fond of her?"

ENTER, JAMES BLENKIN 

187 "I am. And," he added with fire, "I could pretty near kill anyone who breathed one word against her."

Neville straightened the blotting pad in front of him thoughtfully. "Quite," he said with understanding. "But in the circumstances you mustn't wonder at tongues wagging.

Frankly, as the Scotland Yard official in charge of the case, I regard it as peculiar that she does not come forward." 

Blenkin's face blanched. His lips quivered with anger, and there came an ominous blaze to his eyes. 

But he held himself in check. Neville hated baiting the fellow, yet needs must when the devil drove. He watched the effect of his first charge, then took the next step. "You see," he went on, carefully choosing each word, "we have reason to think she was on friendly terms with some other man besides you." It was an arrow shot at random. If Mary Findon parted with her key, to whom did she give it? What a multitude of questions could be answered if only that simple problem could be solved! 

Blenkin leaped out of his chair. "Yes, but what the hell had he to do with it?" came the swift demand. "I don't know," replied Neville soothingly. "Sit down, Blenkin. We shall never land anywhere if you get so hot under the collar. Let's talk this out sensibly. 

Now, first of all, who is this other man?" "I wish I knew. My God, if only I got the chance," his fist was clenched, "I'd make him talk.' 

---

"You think he is in some way responsible for her disappearance?" "I-I don't know what to think, sir. Lying awake at night I've imagined all sorts of things. At times I've felt sure it's his doing, then I've been doubtful about it. I can't believe she'd be mixed up in such a business. Not my Mary. You don't know her as I do, sir.

If he's been up to any tricks he'll have to reckon with me!" "But how comes it that you don't know who he is? 

Has Mary never even mentioned his name to you?” "She knew I—I'd punch his jaw if she did.” "You've no idea even where he lives?" "Do you think I wouldn't have been at him before now if I knew?" "How long have you and Mary been fond of one another?" "Three months or more. There's never been an angry word between us-until about ten days ago." "And then?" In that office Detective Inspector

Neville was no sentimentalist. The ramifications of the 

Mayfair mystery appeared to be widening even still further. "She made some excuse for not going for a walk the following night. ENTER, JAMES BLENKIN 

189 you understand, sir, I was afraid of having a real row about it in case I lost her altogether." 

Neville readjusted the blotter once more. "Search your memory, Mr. Blenkin. Is there anything Mary mentioned at any time that would give us the slightest idea about him?” 

Blenkin answered without hesitation. "I've never seen him and I've no more notion than the man in the moon whether he's young, old, tall or short. I know nothing about him except that he just butted in a while ago, and Mary-well, I won't say she turned me down for him, but she went out with him more than once."

The Inspector sat back and with a blank face regarded his visitor. Here was another stumbling block down another possible route of discovery. "All right, Mr. Blenkin," he said suavely.
