Unless," his brow darkened, "unless she's gone off and married that other chap." "Oh! You really think that possible?" 

Blenkin bit his lip. "It's hard to say, isn't it? I'd be surprised, but there, you never know what a girl might do if she'd got crazy about him!" "That's true. Yet I'm afraid, Mr. Blenkin, there is a chance of an explanation which, from her point of view, would be much more serious. You know we have

to look at every possibility. The longer this goes on, the more likely it is, I am afraid, that you may never see her alive."

The young man looked away from the C. I. D. official for a space and then his eyes returned. "I know, sir," he said pluckily. "That idea sort of -sort of gets me at night time when I can't sleep. 

I don't like to let myself think anything's happened to her, though." "Well, if she is dead we are not aware of it yet.

Our attention has been drawn to dozens and dozens of women all over the kingdom, some pulled out of the river, some who have lost their memory, some under arrest for various offences, and so on. Several times it seemed very likely that we had found her at last, but we have been unable positively to identify anyone as Mary Findon so far. For one thing nobody knows what she was wearing when she went out of Regent House, and that has been a very considerable handicap." "You'll let me know if there's anything I can do, sir?" "You may be very sure of that.”

Blenkin was on his feet. He had come to Scotland 

Yard with bitterness in his heart against the whole world, and particularly against these officials who had plastered the country with notices announcing that his girl was wanted "in connection with" murder. But he was leaving with a liking for this C. I. D. official. 

A kindly man who was only doing his duty. Not cut out for such work, probably. A bit too human.

ENTER, JAMES BLENKIN 

191 Neville's eyes followed him to the door, and that kindly soul was wondering in a purely professional capacity whether Jim Blenkin, whom his associates in a certain grocer's establishment suspected of being responsible for Mary Findon's disappearance, was really as much in love with her as he professed. Detective Inspector Neville got some queer fish through his hands in the course of his kaleidoscopic duties. 

After years of experience one could classify them in three minutes-up to a point. But if they had everything to hide they grew incredibly cunning. And to the murderer, as a rule, the protection of his own skin was everything.

He rang a bell, and presently Sergeant Trim stood before him. "Jim Blenkin-you have his address?" "Yes, sir, I entered it." "Send Macintosh up to keep an eye on that man when he leaves work for the next night or two. The fellow's all nerves and jumpy. Been going about with the girl. Says he doesn't know what has happened to her. My own personal opinion is that he's telling the truth, but nothing would surprise me in connection with this case-not even if he walked in here tomorrow and confessed he'd committed the murder. 

Have they got that corpse at the Greenwich mortuary identified yet?" "No, sir. But the doctor says she's been drowned at least ten days." "Then she's no good to us. What about the woman at Reading?"

"I told Parker to telephone, as you said, sir, if he thought it might be her. But he hasn't come through yet." "Another wash-out, probably," snapped Neville. "Give me that list of reports on all the women. I want to go through it quietly. I'm not satisfied. Oh, Trim! 

About Slim Joe. What does Webster say?" "Came through on the 'phone ten minutes ago for instructions, sir. Slim gave him the slip in the Strand." "Slim would!" muttered the Inspector bitingly. And yet Webster was almost the best man they had at the Yard for shadowing, as Neville was well aware. Sankey was the crack at that game, but he was on another job. 

Anyway, Webster could keep the most sensitive criminal in sight for hours without himself being spotted, and he had a genius for anticipating wily tricks such as crooks habitually employ to throw a possible pursuer off the trail. But Slim Joe was a master. "The only way to get that kind safely under observation," Neville added, "is to lock him up, and even then you mustn't trust your own eyes or ears too far." 

But Neville had no intention of locking Slim up yet.

He was building, as the police so often had to build, on the axiom that even the cleverest rogue is likely to give himself away sooner or later if you leave him to his own devices.

CHAPTER XV:  

(9 a. m., Wednesday, September 7th.) "W HAT'S happened to our friend the Walrus?" 

The question came from Tony as he looked in both directions from his window in Manning Square. 

Seward, consuming an after-breakfast pipe, was immersed in the morning newspapers which, these last few days, had switched a decidedly unpleasant limelight on him. They lay scattered on the floor as he had tossed them aside one by one. "What, isn't he there?" "I don't see him or his horsey looking partner. Looks as if Neville had called 'em off."

Seward regarded another set of headlines about the flat murder. Ran his eyes rapidly down a column which consisted mainly of a re-hash of the known facts; an interview with the caretaker at Regent House who was unable to give any help because the crime took place while he was having his supper; and journalistic speculation generally about the mystery. With a snort, Seward threw that paper also on the heap. "Called 'em off, eh?" he observed. "I wonder what that means? Perhaps the perspicacious Neville is beginning to have doubts as to whether I skewered Australian Pete." 193

"Or else," replied Tony, "he's merely changing his tactics." "You mean leaving his infernal trap doors, as he calls them, a little more widely open?" "Maybe he intends to have you shadowed in a way that is less obvious." "It couldn't be more obvious," retorted Seward, “and a change will be rather welcome. You've never had professional sleuths dogging your heels, have you?" "No. And after this I don't crave that particular form of amusement. I think you're a miracle of forbearance in the circumstances. It would have made me as peevish as the devil." 

Seward ran a penknife round the inside of his pipe. "Peevish? Well, I suppose that's what I feel really. 

Extraordinary how it gets on one's nerves! You know well enough what the authorities would say." "They'd tell you an innocent man has nothing to fear," said Tony. "Yes, bilge of that sort. Which may be approximately true, for by the time a British judge and jury have sifted the facts in a case they get pretty near the truth. It isn't fear, or the indignity of it all that I mind. 

Theoretically it shouldn't affect one in the slightest. 

Actually it takes away a sense of personal freedom. 

I never realized before how sweet that particular thing is. I remember once in Brisbane-" 

Tony looked at his friend quickly. "When were you in Australia, Bill? I never heard you mention-❞

Seward smiled. "Don't put on that detective look, Tony. You're just a plain ass, but you can't help it. In that second-rate grey matter of yours, is there any record of the fact that I went to Penang to get local colour for that first book of mine?" "Penang, yes, but—” "Well, wouldn't you have buzzed around a bit, once you got out East, if you'd been on my job?" "Sorry, Bill. The mention of Brisbane rather hit me in the eye, that's all. Take my advice, though, and keep your mouth shut about Australia just now. It isn't the healthiest of all possible topics, in view of the fact that the dead man was an Australian. Can't you see the official mind leaping at the suggestion of some association between you and him out there, or even of some old feud? I mean any such notion springing up now would seem doubly significant to the police."

Seward sat puffing at his pipe for a few moments. "I s'pose you're right, Tony. See what it is to be suspected of murder. Don't ever commit that sort of crime unless you're sure you can get away with itor unless you hate the chap so intensely that you're going to find it a pleasure to swing for him.” "How do you know that I didn't kill Peters?" replied Tony with a grin. "I don't," agreed Seward resignedly. "It's the hardest guessing competition I ever entered. I've got to the stage where I'd say 'I give it up,' if that were pos-

sible. As far as one can see, some bored Archbishop might have done it to pass the time away. If I were a really absent-minded sort of soul I'd almost agree that I might have done it myself." "That," put in Tony, "certainly would explain several otherwise incomprehensible features of the case, anyway." 

Bill yawned. "Tony, you don't suppose, do you, that a man could commit murder in a sort of day dream, just as he can walk ten miles through the streets of London when he's fast asleep?" "I don't see why not. The human brain plays queer pranks. But if you'll take my tip you won't amuse yourself by setting that before Neville as an interesting possible solution." "I suppose you're right, old chap. Dear me, it's surprising how many things about which you have to keep your mouth shut when there's a gent with a walrus moustache leaning up against the railing opposite with designs on your neck."

It was thirty seconds before he turned round, telepathically conscious of being looked at, as some people are. Tony was regarding him intently, eyes slightly screwed up. "Well! What's up now?" "I was thinking, Bill." "You surprise me!" "Don't jest for a minute. I'm serious. Damned serious."

"So am I. Life is a grim, earnest, brief affair, especially when they're trying to shorten it for one. 

What's the idea?"

Tony came over and perched on the edge of the table, hands in pockets. "You may resent what I'm going to ask you, old son, and I can't help that. But you and I don't need to throw any bouquets at one another. You know where 

I stand, absolutely." "Side by side with me; we're clasping each other's hand as we're gently drawn together toward the scaffold. Am I not voicing a great truth?" 

There was no responsive touch of banter in Tony's expression. "I'm not particularly concerned about my own position in this business," he said, "and I don't believe Neville is either. Not enough to make me feel uncomfortable, anyway, though it was a bit hectic on Thursday night for a little while." "You haven't got the cranium of the really out and out criminal, Tony. But appearances are very deceiving, old dear. You're the kind of fellow who generally gets let off with a fine, or even a kindly word of warning from soft-hearted magistrates." "Though I presumably," Tony went on no less earnestly, "had the chance to kill Peters, why on earth should I have done it? The police aren't fools. No motive, you see. Bill, I want you to drop this airy persiflage for a few moments. I want to know something, and you needn't answer unless you like. Tell

me, has anything happened lately that you don't intend to talk about?” 

Seward put down his pipe and looked at his friend. "What on earth makes you ask that?" There was no resentment in his tone. "I'm a bit worried, Bill. Maybe it's only my fancy, but if anything that seemed vital came out-" "What sort of thing?" "Oh, I don't know. Anything." "Then you needn't be scared, Tony. If you're harbouring a suspicion that I may have played my little part in London's underworld, I shall take another of your half-crown cigars and feel no shame." "I wish you'd stop drivelling, Bill Seward, and answer my question as frankly as you feel inclined to." "Right. Then here goes. I'm keeping nothing up my sleeve, dearie. That is, I'm keeping nothing from you so far as the murder is concerned." "So far as the murder is concerned! " "That is what I said." "Then you sleeve?" are keeping something up your "Not what you may be thinking. What I said was, nothing connected with the murder, nothing directly connected with it, that is." 

Tony's face became more grave. "But," he demanded, "how do you know-how can you be sure it has no connection with the murder? Or -just as bad, how can you be sure that Scotland Yard might not think it had?" "Tony, you'd make a marvellous failure as a de-

tective, with that face. I can read your thoughts as if they were printed in letters of fire." "And what am I thinking?" "That the horrid noose is tightening about my throat." "Not tightening, Bill. But what I'm so afraid of is, it might tighten, that's all. I don't believe you know how damnably convincing they can make circumstantial evidence look." "They can't make it look much worse than it does already. You'll admit that, surely.' 

"But they can, Bill. Failing direct evidence, they keep on worrying and worrying the thing, getting one peculiar circumstance here and another there until finally the case for the prosecution may seem appallingly convincing." 

Seward shook his head. . "You've got hold of the wrong end of the stick this time, Tony."

The newspaper man walked to the far end of the room and back. And then kicked a chair. "It isn't my faith in you that's at stake, Bill, as you know. I'm only wondering what those cold-blooded blighters at Scotland Yard may get up to if every tiny detail of your statement isn't found to be meticulously accurate." "It would bring rather a plague of hornets upon me, old lad. But you needn't fear." "I don't want to press you too hard, but-this something that you can't tell me about! I wish to God you'd tell me the truth. We've got to be prepared. Suppose

for one moment it had been Neville who had cornered you on the point." “I should ask one person's permission to speak, and then spill the beans." "Then for goodness sake why don't you?" "Because I don't wish to." "This means, then, that it is a point of honour not to divulge what you know about this particular thing, whatever it is?" "Exactly." "Oh Lord!" Tony kicked the chair again. "All right. 

You know best, I suppose. Life is hard when one has congenital idiots for friends!" He looked at the clock. "I'm due at the office. Bill, your parents were most unfortunate in their choice of offspring," he added, nearer the door. "Weren't they?" agreed Seward. "But why especially?" "Because," retorted Tony Parke, "they picked one who ought to be publicly pole-axed for his stupidity.”

And, because there was such close understanding between these two men, Seward was aware that Tony was actually a little angry with him. As the door closed, Bill smiled-the sort of smile that would have perturbed Tony. 

But it faded a moment afterwards, leaving his face drawn and tired. These last six days had aged Bill Seward. He was living in an atmosphere of long-drawn-out suspense, which had sprung up about him when he was already jaded with six months' intensive work on the novel that marked a big step forward for him in his

career. It was to have been a glorious achievement; now it was only a long gruelling task to look back upon, ending, with galling irony, in-this! In one moment on that dreadful first night he had seen vividly how the clouds were closing upon him, and never had they lifted for a second.

It was as if a mammoth, diabolical spider had chosen that hour in which to begin weaving a vast suffocating web about him. Those days to which a man had the right to look forward as the happiest in his life, watching over his bride in a far country which seemed to be made for them alone-all this was a dream which had ruthlessly crashed. 

And now Tony, the best pal a man could hope to get on this earth, was looking at him differently. Bill could see it, could feel it. 

He walked to and fro in the room with a quickening desire to smash something, to break loose, if only for a while, from the intolerable strain. 

Then he remembered a quiet peaceful drawing room less than a mile away. He hungered for the tenderness of Stella who, after all, still remained the one sweet note in a discordant world. She was waiting for him now. The thought of it gave him comfort. 

Ten minutes later he stood before her. "Any news, Bill?" was the girl's first quick enquiry. "No." He had come here feeling broken and discouraged, aching for peace of mind and the comfort of Stella's companionship. Yet he had entered the room with laughter in his eyes. This brave girl, at

least, was not to see any sign of his courage oozing. 

She was close to him now. Bill's arms were tenderly drawing her nearer, and her warm young cheek was pressed against his. "Being here, with you, like this, after everything that has been happening the last few days, seems too good to be true," he whispered; and Stella knew that for the moment they were on a plane far removed from tragedy. "It's a little door looking into heaven all the time," he breathed softly. "Dear old stupid boy, and only last Friday you wanted to release me from our engagement." "I offered to, Stella. But I didn't want to. You know that." "As if I could. Just at the moment when you needed me most.' 

He put one hand at each side of her head and, bending forward, kissed her hair reverently. But when her eyes were raised to his, something was in their depths which troubled him. The softness had gone. "What is it, dear?"

The girl was fingering the lapel of his coat, her brow clouded. "I was wondering." "Wondering what?" "I don't know, quite, Bill. I'm not clever like those

Scotland Yard men, but," her eyes were flashing, "we can't go on like this much longer. We can't!” "Things are bound to break soon, dearest." 

The girl shook her head. "Not quickly enough for me, my sweet. Oh, if only

I didn't feel so maddeningly helpless! If only I could-" she sighed deeply. "There is nothing you can do, dearest." "If there were, Bill, I shouldn't be here now eating my heart out." "Stella, I wouldn't have your name dragged into this on any consideration. You don't know what minds some people have. They stare at me in the street now-not at Seward, the novelist, but at Seward, the man who is mixed up in that rotten Regent House business. It would drive me mad to think that morbid thoughts were in the heads of men and women when they stared at you." "That wouldn't affect me, Billy, if only I could help in some way." "There isn't a daily newspaper in London," he went on, "that hasn't flaunted my photograph before countless thousands of the sensation-loving public. And they'd make you squirm too, if they got half a chance. 

No, dear, we must keep you out of it. The only thing to do is to sit still and wait." 

The girl's fingers tightened. "Wait! Wait! Oh, my dear, isn't that just what we seem to have been doing for years, all concentrated into six horrible days?"

She walked away from him, a rebel, and looked down into the street. A white hot sacrificial desire was burning within her to throw her woman's wit into the balance of things. What would she care who stared, or what anyone thought or said, if only Bill's name was cleared?

CHAPTER XVI: ANITO 

(Wednesday evening, September 7th.)

DOOR closed. The sound of its familiar click just reached Anito's ears. Standing there, one hand resting on the coiled ivory snake that formed a standard lamp, her eyes were fixed on the portière through which a man had passed out a moment before. Unconsciously she was biting so hard on the long thin jade cigarette holder that her sharp white teeth met and the broken thing fell to the floor. 

Her glance followed it.

Was that a sort of omen? So many things-human things which her life touched were broken or breaking. She felt eerily superstitious this evening. In other days, before Anito cared, men had steered devilwards and ridden hard because of her, sometimes even to her amusement. She hadn't cared particularly. But now it was different. It didn't matter about any of the others. 

To her they were nothing less than nothing. All her world was made up of one man—and she was afraid. 

The first faint prick of it had come like a thief in the night. It was a sickening, haunting fear that grew hourly, causing her heart to beat swiftly for a while until, with a forced laugh, she had thrust the spectre aside. But it had come back, jeering, and was not so 204

ANITO 

205 easily to be banished a second time. Since then it had grown bolder. It lived with her here in this room, in sunshine and shadow. Now, amid the half light, it was mocking . . . mocking pointing to one trivial incident and another, trifles in themselves but more significant in the aggregate. Icy cold fingers seemed to be reaching out at her heart: she could feel their first death-like touch. To her, in this moment, the features of the great idol which dominated the room became more hideous. She even fancied a leer had crept into the eyes. 

Throwing her head back and breathing deeply, she clenched her hands, stumbled across the room to an electric switch. The soft glow of cunningly hidden lights helped, for the time, to banish her dread, and she drifted over to the piano, but there was no peace, no rest in the music which sprang from its strings. 

Then she spread her white arms forward and bent her head, thoughts centred on Tony Parke.

She had seen him once only-in a crowded restaurant-since last Saturday night when he had asked her questions about a shattered gramophone record in the hearth. A gay restaurant, in an hour of especial merriment, when wine was flowing and laughter rippled from table to table. Tony was with a party at the other side of the room. She and he had exchanged signals of greeting, and for the time, perhaps, forgotten each other's existence, when the sound of the orchestra drowned the chatter, and there came a lull as a negro's voice rose clear and plaintive:

"Ain't she sweet? 

See her walking down the street! 

Now I ask you very confidentially,

Ain't-" Anito had known the colour was going from her cheeks. 

That haunting spectre of fear!

There had been a dozen people sitting between her and Tony in the restaurant. Was the accursed tune sending those searching eyes of his in her direction?

Was it? She had but to raise her head to know the answer, but prudence had held her in check. Her fingers toyed with a glass. She fought against some influence which tugged at her, an influence which became almost overwhelming, because she wanted, terribly, to know where Tony was looking.

Of a sudden her resistance had broken, and with a sweeping glance she had seen. Tony, aloof from all that folly for the moment, his expression oddly serious, had had his eyes fixed on her.

Here in her own room now, the girl remained for some time with her head sunk forward, deep in thought; then, in an effort to throw off this mood, began playing again.

After a while Anito became conscious that someone had entered the room. The flow of notes broke off and she looked round, but her fingers returned to the keys. ""Lo, Tony!"

ANITO 

207 He sank into that chair which seemed to beckon a man. "Don't stop playing, please.” He lighted a cigarette and stared into the flickering fire, wondering what secrets this strange room, with its exotic tang, could tell. He wondered, too, why he had come here this evening-really why. Anito always intrigued him. She and her home had an atmosphere of their own. In her way she was an extraordinarily brilliant woman, and brains had a fascination for Tony Parke. But he knew it was not entirely her personal charms which had attracted him to her boudoir on a fine September evening when his "copy" for that night's Monitor was only half written.

Why had she so hated a gramophone record that in an ungoverned moment she deliberately smashed it to pieces? If it had merely offended her artistic taste, would she not have placed it in a waste paper basket or asked her maid to throw it away? But that had not been satisfying enough. She had to wreak personal temper, if not passion, upon it. Had to smash the thing, as if she were sacrificing it on the altar of that exquisitely ugly joss. 

He frowned. The joss-China-Lo Chang-"Ain't 

She Sweet?"-Anito-the murder in flat thirteen!

Were there any links between all these things, or was his imagination, which for six days had been running round and round in vicious circles, really beginning to take absurd flights?

If coincidence was at work, where did it begin, and

where did it end? He could visualise that scene at the restaurant on Monday night when this half-Spanish girl had heard the notes of the tune she detested. Unconscious though she might have been of the fact, her whole bearing altered the instant the orchestra began. 

Her shoulders had straightened perceptibly, and her smile had died. Then he had sensed that her glance would come across to him. He could have staked his life on it. Perhaps she would look in his direction laughingly. Somehow that was what he had hoped for. 

But when she could resist the impulse no longer, it had been a swift secretive glance that she flashed at Tony, turning immediately afterwards to her companion at dinner.

And now he, here in her boudoir, realised that the piano was silent. Anito's voice cut sharply across the still room. There was a taunting curl about the corners of her red lips. "No, I did not commit the murder, Tony!" "Good Lord!" broke from him. "I never even dreamed-" Her fingers drew a few lilting notes from the instrument. "You hide your thoughts badly. There was accusation in your eyes." "But-Anito! For heaven's sake don't tell me that you thought I was-" 

Her beautiful shoulders were eloquent. "At least it is a new emotion in an otherwise dull old world," she answered. "Have you not all been suspected in turn?"

ANITO 

209 "My dear girl! You-and a particularly brutal crime of physical violence! Why, even the occupant of a lunatic asylum wouldn't suggest such a thing." "And yet, my friend, have I not told you that you hide your thoughts so very badly? I read in the Monitor, of course, your vivid description of the scene in the flat. The bizarre effect of that pianist overhead thumping out the modern idea of a love song while a blackmailer's soul was being sent to an unknown destination. It gave me the creeps. You write well, Tony, but you misjudge me." "It is you, Anito, who misjudge-" “I," she went on, ignoring the interruption, "was in my bath last Thursday when your Australian's soul began its journey a mile away from here. And before that, Lucille was brushing my hair for some time. So -voilà! Is that not what you call in the police courts an alibi?" "You never needed such a thing, Anito. If I gave you any such impression the fault was mine. When one is turned aside from a normal life, and plunged into the bewildering complications of a case of this sort, one's fancies play silly pranks. It becomes an actual obsession. I seem to be living with only one object in life now, and that is to help old Seward. God knows he needs it. The slightest suggestion of a hint in any direction sets one's imagination aflame." "Then you admit—” "Please hear me out. Anito, the night I came in here and found someone had sort of waded into a gramophone record with a coal hammer-that record—a tune

which is most particularly on our nerves just nowdo you wonder I began to ask myself absurd questions? By the way, now that we've talked it out, perhaps you will be kind enough to satisfy my curiosity. 

Why do you happen to hate that tune so?" 

Was he quite as ingenuous as he seemed? Perhaps!

This question and answer flickered through the girl's mind. Only—perhaps.

Anito closed her beloved piano gently-even more gently than usual. "You have an infinite capacity for wearying me, sometimes, Tony. Don't you know that direct crossexamination merely irritates a woman?" "Sorry." He laughed lightly without mirth. "I never do seem to shine where women are concerned." "Our little secrets, which are not really worth calling secrets," the girl went on, "must be dragged out by more subtle methods. If you want to know, there is a most discordant out-of-tune barrel organ which plays under my window nearly every day, until I want to throw things at it; and that particular air seems worse than all the others. Can you picture me hurling large pieces of coal at an Italian in the gutter?" "It doesn't strike me as your ideal form of amusement." "And yet it would give me immense joy. But one doesn't talk about that sort of thing." 

A while later when Tony was going, Anito had sunk down among the cushions on the divan. Tony was on his feet when his eyes were attracted to something lying on the floor where it must have fallen unnoticed

ANITO 

211 from a table. Descending the stairs, he examined that which he had picked up—a pair of men's white doeskin gloves, on one of which he had seen a distinctive ink stain.

Anito was more of an enigma to him than ever. In some indefinable way, far below the surface, he was conscious of having annoyed the girl. It was perfectly true that nobody save a lunatic would contemplate the notion of her committing murder. He had known her for years and, in an odd way, numbered her among his real friends, perhaps because he had never fallen in love with her. Once he had trembled somewhere near the brink of that error, but, with a swift riposte at the psychological moment, Anito had saved their friendship from wreckage.

How much of that rigmarole that she had just told him about the barrel organ was true? Somewhere tucked away in his memory was a vague idea that he had seen a notice in that quiet backwater where she lived. 

Walking along now, brooding over unsettling thoughts, he saw a policeman standing at the corner. "Would you mind telling me, officer," he asked casually, "whether barrel organs ever play here in Stanmore Gardens?" "They're not supposed to, sir." "I thought not. It's forbidden' in some places, isn't it?" "I should summons anyone I caught at it here. 

Somebody been complaining, sir?" "Why no," replied Tony easily. "As a matter of fact I like barrel organs. Good night, officer."

And, brooding more deeply than ever, Tony walked on. Maybe Anito was subtle, but it so happened that she was one of those individuals who have a detestation of lying. Why, therefore, had she deliberately invented an out-of-tune barrel organ-a particularly objectionable instrument the owner of which might find a "pitch" in Stanmore Gardens once, hardly twice, and certainly not nearly every day. 

He walked still more slowly, clenched hands thrust deeply into trouser pockets. A vision of that pitiful creature Andy Marks, whom he had seen in the dock that morning, rose before him. When, when was the curtain going to be lifted from all this mystery? 

At the flat he found Seward immersed in the late evening papers. "Anything fresh, Bill?" The question had become a habit. "Nothing important."

Tony picked up one of the journals, and appeared to be studying it a while, but the printed page had no message for him. He was worried-more perturbed this evening than at any time since that fatal Thursday evening when the bottom had been knocked out of everything. He had a figurative card up his sleeve, and if this had been anyone in the world but Bill Seward he would have played it in the way which would have been most effective. Even now, the temptation was great, but— "Those are your gloves, aren't they?" he asked, tossing them across.

Bill put down the Evening News, noticed the ink

ANITO 

213 stain at the base of the glove thumb, and glanced inquiringly at his friend. By chance Bill had pointed out the ink smear on a new glove that very morning. It was dawning upon him that the question had not been entirely casual. "Thanks, yes. Where did you-" he broke off. "Oh, yes, of course." "Bill, don't tell me to mind my own damned business. Everything that you do just now concerns me.

I thought you and Anito had had rather drifted apart." "We were never particularly attached to one another." 

Tony grunted. "You were interested in her." "So is everyone who knows the lady. They couldn't very well be otherwise." “Well, I'll put it differently, then. I thought you had become less interested in her." "That wouldn't be quite fair to Anito or to me. We have had no quarrel. The only thing is-" "Stella!" Tony put in bluntly. "Quite so," Bill agreed readily. "As you know, Anito certainly has a most attractive personality, and, though Stella was never small enough to be jealous, I thought it was a little more sporting if I gave Anito a miss."

Tony was silent for a moment. He had ceased his sentry-go. "Will you tell me why you went to Anito?"

Seward's expression was hardening a shade, and still he was good tempered.

"You're very persistent, Tony, but I don't mind. I went round at her definite request. I had a note from her this morning asking me to go. Is that all you want to know?" "Not nearly," retorted Tony. "I want you to tell me what Anito wanted." 

Bill was stuffing tobacco into the bowl of a pipe. "It didn't amount to a row of pins," he said, dismissing the subject as Riggs entered the room.

CHAPTER XVII:  

(9.30 p.m., Wednesday, September 7th.)

HAT same night Inspector Neville was sipping a whiskey and soda contentedly in front of a blazing fire on Tony Parke's hearth. One gathered that the cares of the day were for the moment behind him. He was reflecting luxuriously that the world would be a pleasant place to live in if he could always afford to smoke such cigars as this which the Daily Monitor man had given him.

Besides, his mind was less uneasy on the subject of the Mayfair mystery than it had been for some time. 

That confounded affair still consisted of loose ends, none of which could yet be pieced together to make a coherent story, but intuition told him that he was drawing nearer the stage when something intelligible would emerge out of the chaos. For six days now he had been throwing out tentacles in every direction, and a good deal that he had achieved would doubtless prove to represent wasted labour; but this was inevitable in such a complicated problem. 

There had been one or two questions concerning details that he wanted to ask Tony, and, finding himself near, he had called. A few minutes had ended those formalities. Afterwards the cigar had tempted 215

him; the chair was easy; the fire looked good; and the whiskey was pre-war. Neville stretched his legs out and relaxed after a strenuous day. 

But it was the kind of relaxation that could easily have been misjudged. These physical comforts alone would not have kept him there. Apart from the specific business on which he had called, the Inspector had wanted a quiet chat with Parke; a casual talk such as this. In spite of the reservations expressed by Dr. 

Mappin, Neville had come to the conclusion, days ago, that this newspaper man had not been in flat thirteen at Regent House when the murder was committed. 

Moreover, Neville was morally sure that Parke did not know exactly what happened in Seward's library on that fatal evening. But where did Tony Parke's knowledge in connection with this thing end-and where did it begin? For some reason which he was not yet able to analyse, Neville wanted to get further inside Tony's thoughts. Partly this was due to instinct and partly common sense. Intimate friend as Tony was of Seward, he must have straggling memories tucked away, any one of which might, with lightning rapidity, open the door to immense possibilities.

For the moment Tony and Neville were discussing Andy Marks, who, according to programme, had been remanded that morning for a week, charged with being in possession of stolen property. "He looked more fit for a hospital than a police court dock," commented Tony. "Yes, he's going through it," agreed Neville, appreciating the bouquet of that wonderful cigar. "Col-

lapsed altogether in his cell after the case was over. 

Weak heart. For a time they thought he was going west. Shouldn't be surprised if he talks before long.

Creatures of his kidney generally do. No backbone when the slightest squeeze comes." "What's the betting that he committed the murder?" "Even money. I wouldn't go further yet. In the last twenty years I've had so many disappointments after being a bit too optimistic. Still, it began to look suspicious as soon as he turned mulish the first time I questioned him. I can't get away from the notion that 

Slim Joe used Marks as a tool. Naturally Slim isn't going to say so if he thinks there is any chance of that fact not coming out. And you may depend on it he'll never give the truth away."

Neville took another sip from his tumbler. "I always think those wretches like Marks never get a sporting chance in life," said Tony. "Not a chance like you had-or your friend Mr. 

Seward. Didn't you say he was at Repton with you?” "Bill Seward? Yes. And a real corker he was, too, even in those days." "What sort of career had he in mind then?" "Medical research. His father was a Harley Street specialist, you see, and Bill was moderately keen on taking up that work. No money in it, of course, but he's pretty well off. I don't think he was built for it, though. Too imaginative.” "When did he take to writing?" “All of a sudden, some years ago. Threw the other

job up in an hour, one night after going to the theatre with me. We'd seen a strong dramatic play, and it seemed to give him a fever to write a better one. He wrote a play, but it wasn't much of a success. Afterwards he tackled a novel: that made him famous." 

Neville folded down a leaf of his cigar. "I never can make out how writers get their ideas.

He had to travel a good deal, I suppose, and meet all kinds of people."

Tony flashed a glance at Neville, and in that second realised what had been in the detective's mind all the time he had been smoothly leading up to this question. He had taken half a dozen sips at his whiskey and soda, but the tumbler stood there by his side now, nearly as full as when he began. "Yes, Bill has more friends than anyone I know," replied Tony, slightly ruffled. "By the way," he got up and paced the room, determined that this was the moment boldly to face the one big issue that mattered, "do you still suspect Seward?"

The detective's pause before he answered was barely noticeable. "I don't remember saying that I did suspect him."

Tony Parke was standing with his back close to the window, long legs akimbo. Neville was sitting sideways to the fire, his eyes following a wreath of cigar smoke which eddied and grew thin until he could see through it.

And through it he saw a hand creeping into the room.

The hand came past the door, which stood a few inches open. 

Without moving, and momentarily holding his breath, the detective let the cigar drop into the hearth. His whole body tensed, eyes fixed on the bent fingers before him.

But in that instant there was a crash of breaking glass and the light went out as a bullet came through the window, struck the bulb, and thudded against the wall. "Damn!" came from Tony Parke. He lunged forward from the window, and, stumbling against a chair, fell. 

As if a spring had been released, Neville shot out of his chair across the floor, tore the door open, and dragged into the room the most astonished person in all London. 

It was Tinker Brown.

Releasing his grip on the lad, the detective peered through the flickering light from the fire, then rushed to the window, flung it wide open and stared downward.

There was a car just turning the corner out of sight. 

Otherwise, as far as he could see, Manning Square was deserted. 

Three sharp blasts came from his police whistle, and with one glance at Tony Parke, who had already risen to his knees, the detective dashed out into the hall and down the stairs, brushing past the figure of a Chinaman who was ascending.

In the street Neville blew his whistle again, until two policemen came at the double. "There's a man been shot," he explained rapidly. "Search those gardens quick!"

But, even as he and the two officers scrambled over the low railings into the enclosed space with its cover of trees and tall bushes, Neville sensed that an active man could have run to the far side a full minute ago and slipped out, unobserved, at the other end of Manning Square. Apparently that was what he had done -unless someone had fired the shot from that car which he had seen turning the corner. 

By the time they finished their search, a small crowd had begun to collect. Realising that nothing more could be done outside, Neville dismissed the two policemen and returned to Parke's flat.

There the blind had been drawn and the light was again burning. Tony was standing in the middle of the room mopping the side of his head with a redstained handkerchief. Under his feet were the fragments of a shattered electric bulb. Riggs, his man, having telephoned for a doctor, was just hanging up the receiver.

In the hall, with impassive face, and arms folded as ever, stood Lo Chang, with a book in his hand. He had been told to deliver it here, but for the moment nobody was paying any attention to him. Something had happened. In case there was any answer, he could wait.

Neville pushed the Chinaman forward into the room,

then, with one of his sweeping glances, took in what he could of. the situation. "You hurt much?" was his first question. "No," replied Tony cheerfully. "Just a graze above the temple. Nothing to speak of.” "Close shave for you, young man!" 

Tony grinned. "It hadn't my name on it, so it doesn't matter! 

What's happening down below there?" "Nothing!" snapped Neville irritably, turning his attention to Tinker Brown. "And what was your hand coming round the door for just then?" "Mr. Riggs let me in, sir. 'E told me to wait, 'cause

Mr. Parke 'ad a gen'leman with 'im. I only wanted my parcel." "What parcel?"

Tinker pointed to a brown paper package on a chair just within the door. "That's all right, Inspector," Tony put in. "It's something I told the lad this morning I'd get for him. 

An overcoat, as a matter of fact. Said I'd leave it there on the chair in case I was out." 

Neville's attention then switched to the Chinaman. "And what's this?" he asked sharply.

Old Poker Face. The thought was just dawning on 

Tony! This was Wednesday. Only on Monday he had cornered the Chinaman. Tackled him without scruple because nobody else would. Battered at the wall of inscrutability behind which the real Lo Chang lay hid. 

The stolid flat face which never changed had angered

him, and Tony hadn't cared much what he said, because things were getting desperate. And all the time maintaining his quiet respectful air with maddening composure, Lo Chang, in his squeaky little voice, had repeated his old story like a parrot. "Lo Chang met fliends. Velly solly!" 

Tony was thinking furiously. Maybe death was not a thing the Chink feared, but even a Chinaman would avoid it if possible. And apparently there was only one person whom Lo Chang had to fear.

For the space of five full seconds Tony went on dabbing the side of his head with the handkerchief. 

Suppose it was all fancy! Bill had consistently laughed at the very idea of Lo Chang being implicated in the murder of Australian Pete. Bill hadn't just been hit by a bullet, it was true; but a hangman's rope was dangling over his head.

It was a big decision that Tony had to make. He had but to tell what he knew, and in a few minutes Lo Chang would be on his way to the nearest police station, there to be detained while the most minute enquiries were made as to his movements on Thursday night. In that way Stella would be dragged into it. 

The detestable limelight of publicity would inevitably be thrown upon the girl and her father. How many times had Bill said he wanted to avoid that at all costs! Reluctantly, but in deference to Bill's wishes, he finally answered. "He's only come with some message, I expect, Inspector. From a friend." "You know him?"

"Oh yes. We know one another, don't we, Lo Chang?" 

The Chinaman bowed and with one hand presented the book he had patiently been waiting to deliver to someone at this extraordinary establishment. "For Meester Seward," he hissed in his sibilant 

Chinese way. "Seward, eh?" came from Neville in quick surprise.

He in turn was beating his thoughts against that mask of an Oriental. The book was for Seward, too! What new subtle link was this in the chain of mystery? 

Which of the many broken ends would it hold together if only he knew where to make it fit? Why should the 

Chinaman want to shoot Anthony Parke? These and a dozen other questions crowded the detective's thoughts. "Were you down in the street just now when that shot was fired?” 

The Chinaman shook his head. "But you must have heard it!" the detective persisted. "No, meester." "Hold your arms out," the detective ordered bluntly. 

Lo Chang looked from Neville to Tony Parke, and his queer smile dawned-a smile that might have meant anything because it consisted solely of a wrinkling of the skin, leaving the slanting eyes untouched. It was as if he recognised that some foreign devil's jest was afoot and he wished to be perfectly polite, without quite understanding what it was all about. "Put your arms out!" thundered Neville.

Still smiling like some sort of automatic doll, Lo Chang obeyed, and with skilled hands Neville swiftly patted the Chinaman's voluminous clothing from shoulders to ankles until he was satisfied that no revolver was there concealed. "Thrown it away-if he's used a gun," Neville said, making a mental note to have every niche of the enclosed gardens searched for a weapon at dawn. “All right. He can go now."

The Chinaman turned his expressionless eyes toward 

Tony as though for confirmation. "Yes, you can go, Lo Chang."

The man again bowed gravely and padded out of the room with his quick silent step, four pairs of eyes following him. "See him out, Riggs," Tony said. "And you had better hop off home too, Tinker." "'Ow's your 'ead, sir?" asked Tinker, who throughout had not lost sight of the, to him, all-important fact that his hero had escaped death by a hair's breadth. "Fine, thank you. Now push off, son, it's getting late. 

Don't forget your parcel."

The door closed behind the other three. Neville and 

Parke were left together. The detective's first act was to feel in his waistcoat and take a large pinch of snuff more noisily than usual. "Quite an entertaining evening, what?" observed 

Tony with a dry laugh, holding his head near a looking glass and trying to see what damage there was. "Sorry to have invited you into a sort of shooting gal-

lery, Inspector, but I assure you this sort of thing doesn't happen often." He swung around. "And," he added, "while we're on that subject, I'm not altogether sorry it happened."

There was a query in the twist of Neville's eyebrows. "Don't you see," Tony waved a hand toward the window, "this lets Bill Seward out?"

Neville was still standing, his big head bent forward in intense thought, both hands holding on to the lapels of his coat. "Eh?" It was as if he had hardly heard. "I say it lets Bill Seward out," Tony almost shouted in his joyous discovery. "You don't doubt that it's all part of the same thing, do you?" "Perhaps and perhaps not," Neville flung over his shoulder as he walked across the room and pulled up the blind until a hole neatly drilled in the glass was exposed. "Don't you believe anything but your own eyes and ears in this case; and don't trust them too far.

There's some influence at work that we haven't put our finger on yet. Yes, that's it. We haven't even begun to put our finger anywhere near the real heart of the truth." 

Following with his eye the line the bullet must have taken, he marked a place high up on the wall opposite. 

Placing a chair on the top of a desk, he climbed up in silence until he found that which he sought, embedded in the woodwork. "Bullets talk," he said, producing a penknife and digging the thing out. "There!"

"For one thing Bill hasn't even got a revolver," persisted Tony.

Neville was tracing the path of the bullet backwards beyond the window. "Yes, as I thought!" he exclaimed bitterly. "The man who fired that shot was standing in the trees down below there when he pulled the trigger. And he's got away with it, damn him!”

The door opened and the doctor walked in. "Hello, Parke," he began. "What's this I hear? Shot?" "Oh, it's nothing much," said Tony, presenting the side of his head for examination. "Lord, you've been lucky, man," said the doctor a minute later. "Just a scratch. A fraction of an inch deeper and your number would have been up. I shall want some hot water."

Neville had drawn the blind again and sunk into the deep chair by the fire, scarcely lifting his head while Parke's slight injury was being dressed. He had other things to think about-absorbing things. 

It was like looking down at some titantic battle of chess in which the pieces were all human beings refusing to obey the laws of the game and thrusting an intangible curtain before his eyes so that he could not see when greed or passion or fear drove them to make a move that was not allowed.

There was Seward who, but for a missing link of evidence, should now, by all the laws of common sense, be on his path to the scaffold. If that one link were found, nothing could save him.

There was Andy Marks, craven and obstinate. 

Neville had spent an hour with him that morning, his wits keyed up to their finest point, exercising every art in his power to get one degree nearer the truth. 

Marks had just glared at him—and shivered. 

There was that cunning unknown quantity, Slim Joe, who, according to Australian Pete's letter, bore an unforgettable grudge against Seward-if Seward and 

Bunty were one. But if Seward had ever associated with crooks, he had been clever enough to keep out of prison. They had nothing against him in the records at Scotland Yard.

Also there was the Chinaman, who just now had been a picturesque high-light flung into the situation for a few moments, but only for a few moments. 

Neville was more inclined to believe him merely one of those red herrings that fate dragged across his professional path as if to test his judgment and his temper. 

There was the servant girl who had vanished into thin air. For a few hours that very afternoon Neville's hope had been stirred in regard to Mary Findon. A woman discovered wandering near Hull, unable to give any account of herself, had been almost positively identified by the Yorkshire police as Findon. It was only when her finger-prints reached London and were compared with those found on Findon's personal belongings in her own room, that this hope also crashed. 

Then there was this inexplicable shot in the night. 

Who tried to murder Anthony Parke? And why? Was it because Parke knew too much?

Always with Neville, also, was the maddening prob-

lem of the method of entry employed at flat thirteen by the dead man and by the person who killed him. 

If Seward did not let the deceased in, who did-unless 

Australian Pete used Findon's key? And if Seward did not commit the crime, how did the actual murderer get in? Was Mary Findon, after all, a member of a calculating blackmailing gang, who had played her part and then gone back to her old haunts, wherever they might be, there to masquerade under some other name?

What else was there enmeshed in this tangled skein? 

The influence of some super-criminal, or even, perhaps, of another woman. Ah, that might be it! Neville's eyes narrowed. Another woman! It was not by any means the first time that such an idea had been entertained by Neville. But why did she come into the complex story? And where? 

While the doctor was dressing Parke's injury

Neville sat still, staring into the fire. The healing of wounds did not interest him. He was vastly more concerned with the question of who could have stood down in the darkness among those trees in Manning Square and pulled a trigger. The Chinaman? If so he must have climbed the railings and entered this block of flats rapidly. Why should someone want Parke, the newspaper man, to share the fate of Australian Pete, the blackmailer? 

He forgot, even, to retrieve his half-smoked cigar from where it lay, waiting patiently, until the doctor had gone so that he could resume his talk with Tony.

It might lead to nowhere; but again, who more likely than Tony Parke to have some deep-seated suspicion as to the identity of the person responsible for this last murderous attempt?

CHAPTER XVIII: FEAR! 

(Thursday, September 8th.) 

NDY MARKS shivered. He was sitting huddled up on the edge of a bed. In the first thin streaks of dawn which filtered past the little barred window into his cell, he looked ashen and haggard, as though the stamp of death was already upon him.

The lank hair fell over his brow in unkempt disorder. Hands and arms drawn up to his chest, he stared stupidly at the wall before him, occasionally darting rat-like glances at the small grille through which he knew a pair of watchful eyes would peer at him again before long. That had been happening all the time he had been there. They couldn't leave him alone in his agony. He had all gone to pieces. Those inquisitive eyes had got on his nerves. Every moment he was anticipating them, and he wanted to scream when they came. 

As the rays of light strengthened, the little Cockney's face was drawn to the travesty of a window. Another day-of hell. There was torture even in the sight of sunshine. It belonged to the great "out there," where you could walk about and go where you pleased and do things—a world which he had lost. 230

FEAR! 

231 But even prison was not the sword edge that was cutting at the heart of Andy Marks. Fear was eating into his soul: fear that sent shivers through his frail body every few minutes and made the sweat stand in beads on his skin. These prison officials had not been harsh, but they suspected him of murder; and none of this would have happened if it hadn't been for Izzy the fence. His fingers stretched out and were drawn in like claws when the face of that Jew rose before him.

The cunning and obstinacy which took the place of courage within Andy Marks were wearing thin. Every hour since they had locked him up he had writhed under the thought that the police were plotting and planning his death. If he fell into a fitful sleep it was only to dream of the cell door opening and the executioner standing there with dreaded straps in his hand; and to awaken with the realisation that the nightmare might come true.

He knew that Neville, the Scotland Yard Inspector, would bring the capital charge against him if he could, and was doing his best night and day to get evidence. 

Maybe the police didn't need much more in the way of evidence than the things missing from that cursed Mayfair flat. Marks' knowledge of the law was fragmentary and distorted. He wished a thousand times every day that he knew how much they would have to prove. Anyway, they'd got him locked up, and could take their time.

Sometimes he had read about murder cases, but the memory of them did not help. Besides he couldn't

think properly here in a prison cell, with the possibility of death hanging over his head all the time.

Last Tuesday when they arrested him and began to ask questions, he had seen a loophole for a little while.

If he kept what he knew in his own head they couldn't do much to him-so it had seemed. But fear-the dreadful nerve-destroying doubt of what might happen-had torn his flimsy assurance to shreds. 

Now, after a night that had reduced him to a pitiable wreck, he was coming to heel. Andy Marks was not built of the stuff that makes master criminals. His limbs kept jumping spasmodically and his little shifty eyes had in them the look of a craven dog about to be whipped. 

He leaped up, shaking from head to foot. Someone was staring at him through the iron grille! Last night, in his maddened state, he could have flung himself against the grille and shouted wildly; but that was past. He was arriving at a decision-he must come to a decision-to end this torture. 

When the door opened he glared at the uniformed official. "I've got something to tell Mr. Neville, see?" he snarled.

The warder, to whom the moods of prisoners was a commonplace sight, regarded Marks critically. "Oh, you have, eh? Well, Inspector Neville isn't waiting on the door-mat for the likes of you." "I've got to see 'im," said Marks, clenching his fists, his voice rising. "Now! Don't you understand? Now! 

Blime, go an' fetch 'im, I tell you."

FEAR! 

233 "You be quiet, my lad," came in rough kindliness from the official who towered over him. "Scotland Yard 

Inspectors don't get up of a morning till they get their cup of tea in bed and their shaving water brought. 

You have a swig at this hot coffee and eat your breakfast. You'll feel a lot better then." "Eat! Gawd's trewth! 'Ow can I eat? This is killing me. I can't bear it any longer-I can't."

He collapsed, moaning, on the bed, shaking from time to time like a horse in the presence of a snake. 

All the fight had gone out of him. He was beaten. At any cost this unbearable suspense was to end. 

He raised his head when at length Detective Inspector Neville entered the cell. "Well, Marks, you asked to see me?" "That's right, sir. I couldn't stick it no more, see?"

Hardened though he was by a life of this sort of thing, the Inspector's pulse quickened. 

"So you're going to tell me just what happened, eh?” "That's right, sir. So's you can't get me 'anged.' "I'm listening," said Neville. "You won't go on tryin' to string me up if I tell you the truth, will you, sir?" The youth's weak eyes wandered to the thin shaft of sunlight that percolated through the narrow slit high up in the cell wall. Andy 

Marks loved liberty, and hated prison. Some men grew accustomed to it; even laughed at a stretch, but they were of a sterner grade than Andy. The mere memory of deadening confinement, iron discipline and endless monotony made him shudder. He would almost as soon die as spend dragging years at Dart-

moor-almost. Only he was afraid of death. The thought of it terrified him. "Where did you get those things you were trying to sell to Lenski?" asked the Inspector. "Found 'em, same as I've told you before, at the corner of Sherwell Road, not far from where that feller was murdered. No use me tellin' you any different, 'cause it's gospel truth. Round about ten o'clock, that was." He hesitated. Now the critical moment had come, he thought longingly of the freedom that might be if he said no more. "And what about that job at Regent House?" the 

Inspector prompted. 

Something within Andy Marks broke. The remnant of his shrivelled spirit awakened. "I don't know nothing about that," he cried shrilly, leaping to his feet. "Never went near the blasted place. 

You won't believe that, will you? So I've got to prove it, and go to quod for years. For years!" He beat his hands against his emaciated chest. "Me! I've got to send myself for a long stretch, so's you can't 'ang me." Neville remained silent, aware that the battle was half won. "You want to know where I was at eight o'clock last Thursday night, don't you? Well, I'll tell you. I was on a job, see? Down in Bermondsey. I was starvin'. Met a pal who told me 'e knew how to get into a warehouse down there in Ruston Street, only he wanted someone to keep a look out in the yard." 

Momentarily the Inspector half closed his eyes.

Much of London's daily round of crime was automatically tabulated in his memory, but he had to think a

FEAR! 

235 moment before some details of a Ruston Street warehouse robbery came back to him. "We slipped into the yard just before the gates were shut," Andy went on, "and got behind some cases that were piled up." "What time was this?" "A bit before eight. A quarter to, maybe. They shut the gates at eight, and then there was nobody left in the warehouse except the night watchman. After a while we broke a window and my pal went in. He was going to hand the stuff out to me, but 'e couldn't find it, or something. Kept me there for about twenty minutes. Then I heard someone coming towards me round the pile of cases. I thought it was him, but I wasn't sure, so I picked up a thick bit of wood." 

He broke off and pressed his hands to his cheeks. "It wasn't my pal. It was the watchman. I didn't see him till he was about three feet away, and, bein' all excited like, I soaked him one with the stick. I thought at first I'd killed him, and I 'opped it. Climbed over the yard gate, and started to run. But somebody saw me and began hollerin'. I turned down a passage that came out into Thraxton Road. There were plenty of people about and I knew I'd be all right if I stopped running."

His eyes returned for a moment to the tantalizing shaft of sunlight. "That's just what 'appened, sir. Miles away from the murder. I know, 'cause I kept on walking an' walkin' till I was ready to drop before I got to Mayfair. Then I saw that parcel of things I was tryin'

to sell on Tuesday when one of your fellers pinched me." "What was the name of the pal you were working the warehouse job with?" "I don't know, sir. Don't know his right name, I mean. He's called Stiffy down our way. Saw in the paper next day that 'e'd been caught and the watchman was recoverin' in 'ospital. That's all I cared about." "Well, Marks," he said, "if all this is true, we can't hang you. You have that consolation. But you've got a bad record already. This means penal servitude." 

When he was again left alone, Andy Marks sank back on to his comfortless bed. His agony of unrest was over, but black depression had settled upon him. 

The bread and coffee near were untouched. He had no stomach for food. They'd got him, and would keep him now. Maybe he'd never be out again. The doctors had warned him about his cough. Told him another stretch would most likely finish him.

The air in there was stifling him. He didn't want to die. He wanted to live-so that one day Izzy Lenski's eyes would bulge out when he saw what was coming to him.

CHAPTER XIX:  

(11 a. m., Thursday, September 8th.)

EVILLE rose from his chair with instinctive courtesy. For several seconds neither he nor the girl spoke. They were unaware even of the dingy, untidy Scotland Yard office with its eternal atmosphere of drama-two human beings concentrated wholly upon one another.

It was the girl who relaxed first. She had dreaded this first contact with the Inspector, not for her own sake, but because she had visualised him as a man of a different, harsher stamp. He was shrewd, but more human than she had judged from his picture in the press. "Miss Merlyn, I understand?"

Stella took the proffered chair, fighting back a swift throbbing in her throat. "You know, of course-" she began. "You are the Miss Merlyn who is engaged to marry 

Mr. Seward?" the C. I. D. official suggested, seeing her hesitation.

Again she studied him for a full two seconds before answering, and by then had made a decision. "Mr. Seward is my husband."

The narrowing of Inspector Neville's eyes was barely 237

noticeable. What new light was this news bringing upon the Mayfair mystery? "Indeed! I was told-" "We were married quietly on Friday.” "Ah! And kept it a secret!" "From everybody. Only my dear father knows. We have so many friends that we decided it would be impossible to tell some and leave others in ignorance. 

Nor should I have disclosed the fact now, but I believe I can safely ask you not to speak of it." "Scotland Yard is crammed with secrets. At the moment I see no reason why your confidence should not be respected. But I shall at least be glad to learn the reason of this secrecy." "It was my husband's wish, for obvious reasons. It was I who suggested that we should get married in spite of everything, and my husband who suggested that we should keep the news to ourselves. We decided not to let anyone know, not even Mr. Parke, my husband's dearest friend. Perhaps you understand?" "I think, perhaps, I do," agreed Neville. "May I ask if Mr. Seward knows you have come here to-day?" "Not yet. But I shall tell him when I go back."

Neville's big head sank a little forward as he sat in a characteristic attitude of attention. His thoughts had darted about in more than one direction during the first few moments of this interview. "By the way-last Friday, you say. At what time exactly were you two married?" "The hour originally fixed-two o'clock." "That explains something," said Neville, a twinkle

lurking in his eyes. He was thinking of acrid words that had passed over a telephone wire somewhere about two o'clock last Friday when an anxious man had telephoned to headquarters explaining that Seward had deliberately given him the slip. "You mean the trick my husband played on your detective?" Stella asked. "Precisely. We should have been rather easier had we known it had a romantic explanation.' 

"Wouldn't you have done the same, in the circumstances?" Stella asked quickly. "Think what it would mean at such a sacred moment to have a policeman dodging at one's heels!" "Maybe I would," admitted the Inspector. "And to explain that," said the girl, "is one of the reasons why I came here to-day. I feel better already." "You were wise to let me know. And your other reasons for coming?" 

Stella regarded him intently. "I suppose I must not ask for your secrets?” "You may ask, but I don't promise to answer." "There is one question, the answer to which I want more than anything else in the whole wide world." "Well?" "I love my husband dearly, Mr. Neville. Surely you know what must be in my heart?" "Of course." "And you won't answer it?" "How can I? We don't seem to have delved much below the bare surface of this affair so far. Much re-

mains to be explained. Until we have more facts in our possession-many more facts-everyone associated with the tragedy must necessarily remain more or less under suspicion." "Even my husband?" "I take you for a brave woman, Mrs. Seward," it was the first time anyone had addressed her by that name, and even in that calamitous moment it brought to her a rare pride and happiness. "As far as I can judge I don't believe you would wish me to answer you with anything but the truth, though it may hurt." 

The throbbing in Stella's throat almost stifled her.

Bill was suspected of murder! This she had known all along. But it had seemed less real until now. Her 

Bill! The man whose arms had held her close to him; who meant more to her than everything put together; who had laughed only an hour ago as she looked up into his dear tired face. She passed a hand over her forehead. "Even though it may hurt," she said steadily. "And now will you please tell me how I, as William Cayley 

Seward's wife, can help you in any way?"

Neville's eyes went to the little broken toy that lay on his desk. It suggested a train of thought. After all, his business was to dig down to the bottom of things, whoever suffered. "Am I to understand that you will not be afraid of the truth, whatever it may prove to be?"

The girl's eyes were squarely on his when she answered.

"I am terrified, Mr. Neville, of anything that is not the truth in connection with this case." "Then," came from Neville, "your faith in Mr. Seward is absolute?" "Absolute!" replied Stella. "Nothing whatever can shake it?" "Nothing!"

Neville swung gently in his swivel chair. "Not even," he went on, "if we found more deadly facts against him? Remember, the chain of evidence against him now is broken at the most in only one or two places."

A prayer for strength winged its way upward from Stella. "Not even then. But," she added immediately, "there is this man Marks whom you have arrested." "He is not charged with murder. If the barest sign of one of his finger-prints had been found in the Regent House flat, it would probably have led to his being condemned to death. But from what we have discovered to-day it looks as though the case against him is collapsing." 

Stella's lips were tightly compressed. "What can I do to help you, Mr. Neville?" "You have helped me already, more than you think.

One's judgment cannot remain entirely unswayed in the face of such tremendous faith. Now I am going to ask you a question. You said just now that it was everything but the truth associated with this murder that terrified you. Is there anything that you are keeping from me?”

"There is nothing that I even wish to keep from you. 

Don't you yet understand the spirit in which I came here?" "I believe I do. And, Mrs. Seward, permit me to say that I admire your high courage."

Stella closed her eyes and pressed her fingers upon them for a moment. "Lo Chang, our Chinese servant, was seen under the window of Mr. Parke's flat last night, just before a shot was fired." "That is so. When did you hear of this?" "My husband and Mr. Parke came round shortly before midnight, and warned us of what had happened." "I see. Please continue." "Now I am going to tell you something that you cannot know and I am determined you shall know.”

In a few direct words she told of the letter which 

Lo Chang said he had forgotten to deliver. "It may be merely coincidence," she finished. "In fact, I believe that is the explanation." "You have never known the Chinaman to steal anything?" "No, no. He is scrupulously honest. Always has been." "Do you know of any reason why he might have committed the murder?" "None." She hesitated. "Unless, naturally, in selfdefence, or in defence of my husband's property, perhaps."

Neville's big head nodded slowly several times.

"I should have been informed of this before," he said. "It opens up a new field." His mind was racing over the situation of the previous night. "Perhaps, as you say, it may be pure coincidence, but I am grateful to you, Mrs. Seward, for your information. One needs every possible help. It is a baffling case-in certain features one of the most puzzling within my recollection." "There is something else I want to talk to you about," the girl went on doggedly. "Indeed!" The detective was alert once more. "I mean Mary Findon. Oh, Mr. Neville, if only we could find her! How much that would help!” "Not necessarily." His face was hard. "And why?" "Because after all this time one is forced to the conclusion that she may not be alive." "But if she is alive?" "Ah!" The hardness was seeping away from his expression. "I am not a rich man, but I would put my hand well into my pocket to have a few moments' conversation with her." "Mr. Neville," an idea had darted into Stella's head, "you have examined everything in her room?” "With the utmost care." "Has a woman examined her clothes?"

Two little lines formed between the detective's eyebrows. What was this girl driving at? "I looked over all her things myself." 

Stella made an impatient gesture. "And what did they teach you?"

"Frankly, nothing," replied the detective. "You know comparatively little about the girl?" "I won't say that. Our enquiries, naturally, have been extensive. Her friends have supplied a good deal of information, though it has proved useless up to now. Also her sweetheart has come forward." "And they have told you the kind of things you really want to know?" asked Stella. 

The Inspector's fingers rasped along his chin. "They have given us highly necessary information. 

And yet, as you can readily understand, it is about the side of her life which people did not know that I want to learn. To me it seems almost certain that she was involved with undesirable characters. She may even be a thoroughly bad lot." 

The girl leaned forward. "Mr. Neville, will you take me to the flat and let me see exactly what garments she has?" "Why, if you wish. But I don't see " "Being a man, perhaps you wouldn't, but a woman can judge a great deal of the character of another woman by her clothes alone," said Stella. "Then let us go at once," replied Neville, rising. “I have a key."

Half an hour later the girl was standing in the room which Mary Findon had occupied at Regent House, a little collection of frocks, shoes, stockings and coats before her. Stella was looking at them reflectively. "Well?" asked the Inspector. 

Stella shook her head. "There's nothing here," she said, "that a thoroughly

respectable maid might not wear. Her things are simple, and clean, and tidy. There's nothing showy. No, Inspector," Stella added emphatically, "you're on the wrong track, I tell you. Whether Mary Findon is alive or dead to-day, she was straight!"

Neville's husky eyebrows were slightly raised. This girl's view did not entirely coincide with his own. There was a much bespangled jade green dress, for instance, which he certainly had not imagined Mary Findon could have bought on a maid-servant's salary, and the hats, he thought, were well on the saucy side. Still, it was easy enough to imagine circumstances in which Stella Seward might be far from communicative if she fancied Findon had a lover other than the commonplace grocer's assistant. Seward himself, for instance. 

Did she harbour some such idea? It was hard to guess. 

Women were marvellous actresses sometimes.

Some hours later Stella was speaking over the telephone. "This is Mrs. Seward. Is that Detective Inspector Neville?" "Speaking." "I have just had a long talk with old Mrs. Findon." "Indeed!" There was eagerness in the C. I. D. official's voice. "Yes. From what she says, I gather that you seem to be under the impression she may know where her daughter is hiding."

Then was a brief silence. Then:

"I hear," he replied. "Then you did believe something of the sort." "I did not say I had formed any definite conclusion," came from Neville guardedly. "Well, you can now. Mrs. Findon has no more idea where her daughter is than you and I have." "What makes you think that?" "I don't think it; I know." The line remained dead for a space. "You are still sceptical, Mr. Neville?" "I don't see how you can definitely know," he answered. "There are some emotions that only a woman can understand and be sure when they are not counterfeit."

As Neville hung up the receiver in Scotland Yard he frowned. For him it meant that just one more bare hope was smashed. Also, if the girl was not being sheltered, it meant one degree more of certainty that she was dead.

When he returned to the Yard from luncheon a ship's stewardess was waiting to see him: a quiet little woman who was clearly conscious of a big responsibility thrust upon her. "I work on the cross-channel boats, sir," she explained concisely, "and last Thursday night I was second-class stewardess on the Dover-Calais steamer. 

It was my last trip for some time as I'd arranged to take a holiday over there." "Yes, yes, go on," urged Neville impatiently. "I didn't see an English newspaper for several days, so until yesterday I knew nothing about the

woman missing from the Mayfair flat. Then I saw her photograph in a copy of the Monitor that was several days old. There was a girl crossed on the night boat last Thursday who was very much like her, sir.” "You noticed her particularly?" "Yes, because she was lying down in the ladies' lounge all the time we were at sea, and seemed very worried about something. "There were one or two other ladies in the lounge and I had to attend them, but I spoke to this particular girl two or three times. For instance, I fetched her a cup of tea and stood talking to her for a few moments while she was drinking it. The more I think of it, the more I'm sure it must have been Mary Findon." "Did you get any idea what she was worrying about?" "Not exactly, sir. But she was sort of scared of some man on board. She spoke of him as her husband." "Mary Findon had no husband." "How do you know that, sir?" "Well-why, of course, she might have. We know no passport has been issued to Mary Findon, in that name, but if she travelled as a married woman, that makes a difference. Did you see him?" "Only for a moment, sir, when the boat reached

Calais, and he had his cap pulled well down-it was blowing hard. But I heard him say something to her in broken English. He might have been a Frenchman or a Belgian. I was busy at the moment, though sir, and, to tell you the truth, I didn't take much notice of him."

"Travelling on a foreign passport, eh? That complicates matters." He put a hand out for the photo of the girl on his desk. "Can you recognize her by that?”

The stewardess studied the indistinct picture for some moments. "It's very similar to the print I saw in the paper, sir. Blurred. I can't swear it's the same girl, mind, but as it happened last Thursday night, and the picture looks rather like her, I came over from France specially to tell you, though I'm still on my holidays." "I'm glad you did. I'll get into touch with the French police at once, but, as it's nearly a week since, they'll have a job to trace the pair. They may be anywhere by now."

A while later he turned, as he frequently did these days, to the long list of possible Mary Findons reported from so many places. Some had been the fruit of idle fancy on the part of various folk; some had caused a great deal of investigation and had been tracked down as false clues; some had seemed tolerably hopeful chances until they petered out; and in more than one case a conclusive decision had been impossible.
