CHAPTER Xx: THE PRODIGAL 

(9 a. m., Thursday, September 8th.)

N the little back bedroom of the Cluney Street boarding house which was his retreat, Mr. 

Joseph Lowther dressed this morning as if it were no ordinary occasion. The clothes in his wardrobe, though not numerous, were excellent. Appearances were of the utmost importance to one of his profession.

He chose a neat blue serge suit with the barest hint of a stripe, and a tie to match. The polish on his shoes was the fruit of his own careful labour. Surveying himself finally in the cracked mirror over the rickety dressing table, he was moderately satisfied; and descended upon that abominable kipper which the landlady presented to her guests so frequently for breakfast.

Even though he only occupied the small back bedroom, Mr. Lowther would have been the star boarder at 33 Cluney Street had he chosen to occupy that comparatively distinguished position. He was so refined. Something in the City, probably. Stocks and shares was one guess, though some pictured him as a lawyer.

There was the usual mild flutter among the regular 249

lady boarders of middle age as he joined them at the table. To at least two or three of them he was at once a source of happiness and of despair. Mrs. Crabbe -her husband had been "wholesale" (leather) until he went bankrupt and died from the shock-who sat next to him, pointed out twitteringly that it was a per-fect morning, and hoped that he would at last answer in more inspiring words than "Isn't it!" Such an admirable successor to the late leather merchant! Even in other years she always had thought that Crabbe had abominable table manners when he took off his coat and settled down to a good feed. "Isn't it!" remarked Mr. Lowther; and concerned himself chiefly with the kipper.

Miss Wickley, who sat opposite, had put down her newspaper. At that distance she was distinctly handicapped, but hope sprang afresh in her elderly spinster heart each morning. It was obvious to her where Mrs. 

Crabbe failed. Mr. Lowther was intellectual. Latterly, Miss Wickley had taken to assaulting him with bright patches from the paper. Yesterday he almost looked interested when she spoke of the situation in the Balkans; the day before it was a new play. To-day she chose a subject much more intriguing: one which was being aired at many a breakfast table. "Have you seen what the Monitor says this morning about the Mayfair mystery, Mr. Lowther?"

Mr. Lowther's eyes were lifted from his kipper, then returned thereto. "Er-yes," he answered truthfully. "Murders are so thrilling-don't you think?"

THE PRODIGAL 

251 "Quite!" agreed Mr. Lowther, folding his napkin.

Mrs. Crabbe's eyes grew faintly watery as they followed him, at last, out of the dingy dining room. (She never dreamed of going first.) Was he blind to the fact that they would have been real companions for the rest of their days? 

At the front door Mr. Lowther drew a deep breath of relief and jingled in his pocket that which stood between him and destitution. Three coins, one of which was silver: the others were copper. Without seeming so to do, he cast a cautious eye in either direction. 

Ah, yes. There his shadow stood! A different one again this morning! If this sort of thing went on much longer Joseph Lowther must take to earning his daily bread honestly (a detestable thought from which he recoiled)—or starve. One could not carry on the profession at which he was adept with a Scotland Yard man perpetually on one's heels. And funds were getting perilously near the vanishing point.

The thing was becoming serious. Never since he left the shelter of His Majesty's prison had Slim been able to engage in the task of money making, although London was full of opportunities if one were unhampered. 

Only last night it had nearly made him weep to part from a chatty individual on the top of a 'bus, who had come back to England from some outlandish corner of the earth for the first time in twenty years with the avowed intention of having a good time. The Scotland Yard shadow was on the same 'bus, and Slim knew that nothing would please Neville much more than to have a double hold over him.

With walking stick tucked under his arm and monocle firmly fixed, Mr. Lowther now strolled forth jauntily, as one who revelled in the joy of such a fine 

September morning.

Never once did he turn his head, though he paused to glance into the windows of a shop or two, apparently unconscious of the detective who never lost sight of him.

Mr. Lowther finally strolled across Piccadilly Circus and, during one of those jams of humanity which momentarily hold up the traffic on the pavement, he dived into the Tube entrance, descended the steps, and, leaving by another entrance, boarded a moving 'bus without delay.

It was some minutes before he gave a little grunt of satisfaction. He had hardly hoped to succeed with such a crude manœuvre, but by the time Victoria was reached Mr. Lowther knew he was no longer followed, and mingled freely with the people at the station, athirst for the sinews of war.

His immediate programme was one that he disliked, skilled though he was. It lay beneath the dignity of one who ranked high at his own game. Besides, bungling this morning would have particularly unfortunate consequences. But he had already broken into that silver coin, and he had a mission to perform. 

With the innocent air of a curate, ten minutes later, he emerged from a small crowd; and, after a minimum of delay, pushed forward the swing door of a pawnshop. "I have come out without enough money this morn-

THE PRODIGAL 

253 ing," he explained affably. "Will you kindly lend me about five pounds on this?"

The assistant picked up the gold watch and chain, gave one quick glance at its interior, and, taking a pen from behind his ear, shot a hand out for a card. 

Then proceeded to fill it in. "A penny for the ticket," he suggested pithily.

Mr. Lowther placed the specified amount on the counter, and, having given a name and address which would certainly have baffled the postal authorities, picked up the five pounds. As it was after eleven o'clock, he called a taxi, which bowled him along to

King's Cross. 

Presently, in a train, the green fields were slipping past. The railway carriage had only one other occupant, whose head was hidden behind a newspaper. He turned over the pages noisily for a while, then flattened them on his knee—a middle-aged individual with beetling brow and sharp beady eyes. "I see they haven't caught that Mayfair murderer yet, sir," he remarked by way of opening a pleasant conversation.

Mr. Lowther started slightly. "Yes," he answered. "Most regrettable, is it not!" "What I think-" began the man with beetling brows, launching into a disquisition.

Mr. Lowther's attention wandered. He fixed his monocle and became more and more absorbed in the passing scenery; so that his travelling companion finally turned his attention once more to the news sheet.

An hour's run in the train landed Mr. Lowther at a small country station, where he set off at a brisk pace, resting on a gate mid-way, partly to observe the beauties of nature, but chiefly to be very sure that nobody was following.

It was a full two miles' walk to a beautiful old Tudor house covered with ivy-the home of the retired Dean of Trychester. Mr. Lowther turned in at the gate, strolled up the drive, and rang the bell. "Ah, good morning, Simmons," he greeted the butler with that air of friendliness without loss of dignity by which a butler gauges you to a nicety (and the art of acquiring which sorely tests the nerves of wealthy pork packers when they set up housekeeping “in style"). "Is your master in?" "Yes, sir,” replied Simmons, flinging wide the door in his best manner. And a few moments later the 

Dean looked up from his newspaper. "Why-Charles! Bless my soul! This is a surprise!

Didn't expect to hear of you again until you were in 

Honolulu or wherever it was you were going." "Bulawayo," corrected the smartest confidence trickster in the country, extending his hand. "Meant to sail some days ago, but business detained me, so I took this opportunity of paying a last visit. May be away some time. And you never know-increasing yearsand so on!" 

His brother's cheerful face crinkled. "I do believe our Prodigal is beginning to have had almost enough wandering." "Prodigal?"

THE PRODIGAL 

255 "Tush! Charles, tush! A figure of speech! Not that you haven't a strain of the Prodigal in you somewhere.

We all have. Even I felt the urge to escape from everything at times."

The visitor's eyes fell on a book lying open on the table. "Hello," he said with a smile, "I see you still take an interest in criminology." "An academic interest, Charles. As you know I have studied it for years. When you came in I was reading the leading article in the Times on the subject of that extraordinary Mayfair mystery. A very able article. Did you see it?" "No." There was a shade of unrest about the fingers of Charles. "While paying a tribute to the modern skill of Scotland Yard-the old school of detectives bore no comparison to that which we have to-day-the writer points out that further steps must undoubtedly be taken to ensure a still greater degree of efficiency in the punishment of those who take human life." "I don't think the Times can teach Scotland Yard much," observed Charles quietly.

There was toleration in the Dean's sunburnt face. "Forgive me, Charles, but I don't think you know much about such matters. Remember, I really have made quite a study of the subject. Too many violent deaths have been left without explanation this last year or two. This Mayfair problem, however, is, I admit, a particularly difficult one to solve." "I remember reading something about it," said

Slim. "The victim was a blackmailer, was he not? I can't see why they don't arrest that novelist. They're fools-" "On the contrary, Charles, I think they are a particularly able set of men who carry out their work very conscientiously. I fear you don't respect them sufficiently." "I?" There was a ghost of a smile lurking about 

Slim's mouth. "You misjudge me, Robert. I respect them much more than you think. Only it seems obvious, from the evidence given at the inquest, that the novelist, whatever his name is, must have committed the " "I disagree, Charles." The Dean was on his hobby horse now. "Such a thing is possible, but if I were in charge of the case I should be inclined to suspect quite another individual." "Indeed! Whom?" "Well, to begin with, Seward, the author, has explained that he had never seen the deceased before." "Do you believe it?" "I neither believe it nor disbelieve it. I should like to see the man before condemning him. One cannot tell very well from printed photographs. I happen to be a tolerably good judge of character. But, according to what I have seen in the newspapers, and reading between the lines, the man I should hunt for is the habitual criminal who is known to have had a serious grievance against the deceased. The person referred to in Peters' letter as 'Slim.' I beg your pardon, Charles?"

THE PRODIGAL 

257 "I didn't speak-just a slight cough which troubles me sometimes. I put it down," he went on valiantly, seeking to switch his reverend brother to another topic, "to this English climate." "As I was saying," the Dean resumed, "Peters writes of this man having a rod in pickle. Slim, I imagine, nursed a grievance for years in prison. I think that when the case for the Crown is completed you will find that either the person referred to as Slim committed the crime himself, or he was at the root of it in some way." "I wonder!" observed Slim. "What strange lives people of that order must lead!" "Where," the worthy Dean added, "could you find a more fruitful hothouse for the growth of hate than in the agony of captivity-the soul-destroying loneliness of life in a cell, the galling punishment of hard labour on a breakwater?"

The man before him quietly lowered out of sight a toilworn hand that had rested on the arm of his chair. "Perhaps I have too much imagination, Charles," his brother went on, without noticing the other's trifling movement, "but it is no effort for me to visualize that torture which a man could inflict upon himself, year after year, brooding over an injury which had sent him to such a purgatory." "Er-yes." "In his overwrought state," the Dean continued, "it might drive him to commit murder as soon as he could get his hands on the throat of his enemy. A wild pas-

sionate impulse of revenge which is unforgivable in a civilized community, but which is at least more understandable than some of the crimes for which men have been sent to the scaffold. Are you finding this room too hot, Charles?"

Charles had risen and walked over to an open window. "It is, a little, perhaps," he answered coolly. "Possibly I came up from the station too briskly. It is a much warmer day than when I last walked here."

His manner was casual. Even Detective Inspector Neville might have failed to distinguish that there trembled upon his tongue the one all-important question the subtle Slim had come from London to ask. He was idly turning over the leaves of an ecclesiastical magazine. "By the way, what day was it that I came here last?" 

Another leaf of the magazine fluttered over. Slim's tongue moistened his lips. "Ah-let me see, Wednesday, wasn't it?" answered the Dean. "Was it? Are you sure?"

The Dean slipped on his pince-nez. "Well, there can't be any mistake about it," he said, glancing at a calendar, "because you will recall that

I spoke of that being the tenth anniversary of my dear wife's death-the first of September. Ah! It was 

Thursday, I see." 

Slim smiled: it might almost have been a Chinese smile, for, while it was easy and pleasant, there lay

THE PRODIGAL 

259 secreted behind it the breaking of a tension which had tested Slim to the uttermost. "Yes, it was Thursday," he said. "I was just seeing if your wonderful memory was as good as ever. Besides, considering how rarely I give myself the pleasure of breaking bread with you, I shouldn't like to think you would ever forget the date on which I last paid you a visit. You didn't expect such sentimentality in a wanderer like me, did you, Robert?" 

The luncheon gong rang.

CHAPTER XXI: A CALL FROM BRIGHTON 

(2 p.m., Thursday, September 8th.) "N° ow, what is it?" Neville looked up with an expression that was almost sour; then, seeing who had come in, he nodded and sat back. "'Afternoon, doctor." "What's the matter with poor old Trim?" asked

Mappin, sitting on the edge of the littered desk. “I met him in the passage just now and he looked like a crestfallen rabbit." "Trim? Oh, he's all right. I lost my temper, but he knows I don't mean it. This is a trying world sometimes, eh?" "Something worrying you?" "Oh, not at all! Except that everything's going to hell and bits."

Mappin straightened his waistcoat, which was developing a tendency to advance at about the fourth button and so mar a blameless figure. "Mayfair case?" "M'm!" The Inspector uncrossed his legs, then crossed them again. "Ever put your fingers on a lovely ripe cherry in a dream, and then found you'd got nothing? That's what I have been doing for a week." 

He banged his fist on the desk. "Nothing!" 260

A CALL FROM BRIGHTON 261 

Mappin looked mildly sympathetic. "But you have some very interesting clues, surely?" "Interesting? Oh, yes, except that they don't lead to anywhere. When I retire from this job I should like to be a newspaper editor for a while, just for the fun of rubbing salt in the wounds of detectives who are spending all their time plucking at dream cherries. 

That sort of criticism is so easy! Have you seen the 

Daily Courier's screed to-day? 'More brains needed at 

Scotland Yard!'" "No. Do they give you any helpful suggestion?" "Yes. "Bring the murderer to the seat of Justice!'

My sacred aunt! Do they think we spend our time knitting? And now Trim came to me with a brain wave. During the last few days he's dropped hints that we ought to arrest pretty nearly everyone connected with the case. It's driving him batty, I think. 

He'll be suspecting me next." "What was his brain wave about?" "I'll admit it did get me all excited for a minute.

I would have said it was ingenious if we were only writing a play. You remember that bright bird who was playing the piano in the flat above Seward's? Well,

Trim's idea was that perhaps he, being just about on his beam ends, went a-burgling in the flat beneath." "And killed the other fellow who caught him at it!" "Something like that. Anyway, having committed the murder, he rushed back upstairs and went on pounding the piano as hard as ever, to throw everybody off the scent."

"Quite possible," said the doctor. "Of course it's possible. It seems to me that anything is possible in connection with this infernal case. 

But will you tell me how he got into the flat?" "Ah!" "That was Trim's brilliant answer. Then he had another brain wave and suggested the lad had an intrigue with the maid, and got the key that way." "Such things have been done." 

Again the Inspector banged his first. "Yes, and she may have had an intrigue with Australian Pete, or Jim Blenkin's rival, or Seward, or Slim Joe, or even someone else we haven't heard of yet, but she didn't hand keys over to all of 'em. The thing is to find out which one. Trim really did think he'd got hold of a good idea at last, and he's a wonderfully smart fellow in many ways, but that was where I saw red. The trouble is, doctor, they're all good ideas, and that's the best you can say for them."

Mappin unhitched himself from the desk and studied the pattern of the wall paper. "Well," he said thoughtfully, "did our young pianist do it?" 

Neville regarded the back of the doctor's head. "Maybe," he admitted, fumbling in his pocket for the enamelled snuff box, "but why not the caretaker of Regent House, or the fat old lady who lives in the flat bang opposite Seward? And what were you doing between a quarter to eight and a quarter past last 

Thursday night?”

A CALL FROM BRIGHTON 263 

"I couldn't prove an alibi," replied the doctor with a half-smile, "because I happened to be dining alone at a busy restaurant where nobody would remember me. But, come, Neville, you've got this fellow Andy

Marks. There's a tolerably good prospect there, anyhow." "Isn't there!" came from the Inspector caustically. "I've just had an interview with one Stiffy Lewis who was robbing a warehouse in Bermondsey at the time 

Peters was being murdered. The night watchman there was knocked out. Stiffy had a pal helping him, and has been swearing all along that it must have been his pal who hit the watchman. Stiffy didn't even know his pal's name—some of these crooks don't exchange cards before they go into business together-and as soon as we put Andy Marks in front of him he gave a shout of recognition. No, Andy will get five years, but he won't swing. He's worked himself into a perfect fever lately, fearing we might place a rope round his neck for a crime that we could never have proved against him, but he didn't know that, and got into a mortal funk. Andy was one of my dream cherries; only, to be quite honest, I never really suspected him." "Well, what about Slim Joe?"

The C. I. D. official made a wry face. "He's capable of anything-anything. There's a logical reason for murder in his case-or rather, there would seem to be, if you believed certain things." "You've faced him with Seward, of course?" "Lord, yes. Here in this very room. And it was

either a case of the lion lying down with the lamb for diplomatic purposes, or they've never met elsewhere. 

I don't know which." "Seward hasn't the appearance of a crook." "Nor have you, my dear doctor, but you can't prove you didn't murder a man last Thursday in Regent

House, and we can't prove you did. There have been moments when I could almost have taken the plunge and arrested Seward, but in the absence of Mary 

Findon, what jury would bring in a verdict of Guilty on the evidence, even though it is almost overwhelming?" "I believe at the bottom of your heart you still think Seward has done away with the girl too." "If ever we find her body, Seward's counsel may have a job to prove that he didn't kill her." "If ever? Then you have doubt about finding it?”

Neville shrugged slightly. "Who can tell?" "I see the mid-day papers are screaming about a new clue—a woman who was seen on a cross-channel steamer." "Yes. I let that out," said Neville, "because it gives them something fresh to bite on. It does no harm." "Anything in the story?" "Oh, it's true as far as it goes, but whether she's the woman we're after is another point. Anyway, she's disappeared into the mists. What do the French police care? They'll make some sort of enquiries, you may be sure, but they have enough troubles of their own to be getting on with."

A CALL FROM BRIGHTON 

265 "What about all the other supposed Mary Findons in various parts of the country, then?" asked the doctor. "Have they all petered out?" "So far as conclusive identification is concerned, yes. But, as you know, there is enormous difficulty in being positive about identification in some cases. 

And just by chance we have experienced more difficulties than usual. People often claim entire strangers, who have been pulled out of the river, as husband or wife. I haven't given up hope in one or two instances. 

There's a girl-”

Trim came into the room, casting a wary eye at his chief. "That warehouse watchman has identified Andy 

Marks as the man who hit him, sir." "Yes, Sergeant," observed Neville, with a touch of bitterness, "and the only thing we lack now to make nearly everyone happy except ourselves is proof that Slim Joe was swimming the Channel at the time of the murder." 

As Trim turned away his lips moved. One might have thought that he was murmuring a silent prayer, but for an added tinge of colour about his cheek bones. "By the way, doctor, you were stationed in the Far 

East once, weren't you?" Neville asked. "Years ago, yes." "Know anything about the Chinese methods of murder?" "A little. They have rather picturesque ideas on the subject. But what has this to do with the Mayfair case?"

"One moment. Speaking offhand, would you say there was anything of the Chinese touch in the way 

Peters was killed?" 

Mappin came back to his old perch on the end of the desk. He considered the point carefully before replying. "Frankly," he said, "it wouldn't have occurred to me, but now you mention the subject I can see where the Oriental influence might come in."

Neville ceased to sway to and fro in his swivel chair. "Splendid! Please explain. This may not be such a dream cherry after all." "Well, the strangling, in the first place, is suggestive. It has a distinctly Chinese flavour. Then there is the kris-definitely a Malay weapon. It's only a figurative hop skip and a jump from the Malay Peninsula to Hong Kong. I can imagine that anyone reared round about there might regard a kris as a familiar sort of tool to use in an emergency." "It never occurred to you, did it, that the man who strangled Peters might not be the man who stabbed him?" "I have thought of such a possibility," agreed the doctor. "But what were you going to say about China?" 

Neville nibbled at the end of one thumb, his keen blue eyes half closed as he stared blankly at the wall opposite. And then he told the doctor all he knew about 

Lo Chang. Mappin leaned forward with especial interest as he listened.

A CALL FROM BRIGHTON 

267 "That, at least," he said when Neville had finished, "looks promising." 

Neville sniffed. "He may have been in the thing, but he's got a better alibi than you, doctor. At least says he has. I hope to get further evidence on that point later in the day. Macintosh is down in Limehouse with him at the present moment, hunting for corroboration of Lo Chang's story. If he gets it we have the word of a bunch of Chinks. And what's that worth, in the circumstances? A Chink lies with his eyes as well as his tongue, and that's what beats me. I'm rather disappointed in the search that was made in Manning Square first thing this morning. I had three men crawling about the shrubbery for a revolver from daybreak till ten o'clock. Not a sign of a weapon, but there are foot-prints, made recently, in loose earth. They may or may not have been made by the man who fired at Parke last night. At all events we've had them covered over for preservation and taken their exact measurements."

The doctor had walked over to the window and was drumming his fingers on the glass. "Parke must know who would want to shoot him!" 

Mappin suggested. "You'd think so. But an oyster is quite chatty compared with Parke on that subject. I spent a long time with him last night.' 

"Well, what had he got to say?" "A lot and nothing. On the way home I figured it out that there are three alternatives. Either he's

stupid and can't see the truth; or his pal Seward tried to plug him and for some reason Parke is keeping his mouth shut; or there's something else behind all this, the very surface of which we have as yet hardly begun to scratch." “And of the three, which is your particular fancy?” "Unless Parke is stupid—and he seems to me more astute than any newspaper man I have met for a long time I would say, tell me what he is keeping up his sleeve and we shall be a long way nearer a solution of this problem." "You'll go as far as that?" "Yes, and further. My guess is that, if we don't make an arrest within the next few days, the body of Anthony Parke will be the subject of an inquest. 

And it won't be a case of suicide. The man-or woman -who murdered Australian Pete, who has got away with it so far, and who had the nerve to shoot at 

Parke last night, won't stop there. No, my friend, if

I'm any judge of criminal mentality he or she will go at it baldheaded next time, and the newspapers will be screaming still louder than ever for more brains at 

Scotland Yard."

The doctor's thoughts went back to Neville's original theory about Mary Findon, and there was a suggestion of a smile about his lips when he spoke. "You still cling to the belief that it may be a woman who murdered Peters?" 

Neville's gesture was expressive. "I believe there's a woman concerned in it somehow, and my conviction on that point grows stronger. Per-

A CALL FROM BRIGHTON 269 

haps it's the missing maid-the rational inference. Beyond that I won't commit myself for the moment. 

In your profession intuition may be a tremendous help, but for a detective to rely too much on that sort of thing is mighty dangerous. One after another, perfectly good clues have landed us nowhere, and yet I have a feeling that we're beginning to grope in the right direction-"

A telephone in front of him rang and he put the receiver to his ear. "Hello! Hello! . . . What's that? . . . Brighton wants me?" A pause. "Hello! . . . What's the matter with the telephone? . . . All right, give me another ring if they come through again." He hung the receiver up once more.

CHAPTER XXI: NUMBER SEVEN 

(Thursday afternoon, September 8th.)

NURSE, leaning over a bed, listened intently, her face suddenly alight with strange interest. 

Instinctively she drew back a few inches, then listened again to the hushed voice. Her fingers were trembling, but, though rigid hospital discipline asserted itself once more, she had a wild inclination to run as she went up to a man who was taking a patient's pulse. "Oh, doctor, what you said was true," the nurse blurted out. "The woman in number seven-she's just told me "

The doctor dropped his patient's wrist and stalked with unprofessional celerity across the ward. He looked down into a pair of beautiful hazel eyes in which consciousness was clearly reflected for the first time.

Nothing but her eyes were visible, number seven's face still being swathed in bandages. "Well, how are we feeling now?" he asked in a cheerful, bedside voice. "Where am I?" number seven enquired. "You've had rather a bad accident, but you'll be all right soon. This is the Brighton Hospital." "Brighton?" queried number seven vaguely. "I don't seem to remember-" 270

271 "Tell me your name," the doctor went on in the same quiet way, "and I shall be glad to communicate with your friends." "My name's Mary Findon." "Is there anyone you specially want to see?" You're not very strong yet." "A week! Oh, dear! All right! But please tell

It seemed to satisfy the girl for a moment. Then: "I remember now," she went on, "I'd arranged to go to the movies with another boy, but I didn't because I knew then that I only wanted Jimmie. I was too proud to tell Jimmie so, and I came down to Brighton by myself on a half day trip. I was dreadfully lonely and miserable. Must have been on my way back to the station when the accident happened. I don't know anything about that."

273 "Yes. The doctor only let me talk for half a minute, but I ascertained everything that matters." "Well, what did you find out?" snapped Neville irritably. So it wouldn't have been physically possible for her to have been in the 

Mayfair flat anywhere near the time the crime was committed, and the key was in her bag at the time of the accident. Hearstley says that someone in the crowd evidently picked it up and stuck to it, though I don't suppose it contained anything much besides a powder puff, a handkerchief, a few shillings and that key."

"But," asked Mappin, "if she's been there a whole week, how is it you didn't identify her before this?" "Several reasons. Face cut and head plastered with bandages. No pockets. No hand bag. No distinctive marks on her underclothing. Too much hung on our decision for us to depend upon guess work. Her condition has been so critical that the doctors said it was touch and go whether she'd live long enough to recover consciousness." "But weren't there any laundry marks on her clothes?" "Girls like her don't send their things to a laundry. 

Besides, they wear next to nothing nowadays. And the frock she had on at the time was evidently new. Nobody has been able to swear to it. How could anyone identify her? To be certain, I mean. And remember there were two or three other women in various parts of the country whom we couldn't be absolutely sure about." "Then she doesn't even know there's been a murder." "She couldn't." "This makes a material difference to the whole case as you viewed it," observed Mappin. "It shows the fatal danger of allowing imagination to have too free a rein at this job. I've been picturing a dozen sequels to the finding of Mary Findon, and they were all a million miles away from the truth." 

The telephone bell rang again. Neville's whole demeanour changed as he listened. When he spoke his voice cut bitingly into the transmitter.

275 "Get after him again. Find him. If it takes you all night, don't fail now. Find him somehow. I'm depending on you, remember."

Says his taxi was held up in a traffic block, though."

The Inspector bent his head forward, deep in thought. He was upon a cross roads. The next few hours were full of possibilities. 

It was after midnight, and no word had yet come to headquarters from all the detectives whom Neville had swiftly scattered in the hope of picking up Seward's trail. Every place that the author was known to frequent was being closely watched, special messages of caution had been sent to all sea ports, and it was practically impossible for him to get out of the country (if that was his object) unless he went by private aeroplane.

277 said Neville, rising as a signal that the interview had ended. "But," Tony insisted, "please tell me what it's all about." It was two o'clock before he went to bed that night, and the new day was still young when he arrived back in the office. His watch dogs stationed in various parts of London had been relieved, but no sign of Seward had been reported to the Yard. 

Eight o'clock-nine! Neville was pacing his office impatiently. One more hour remained. If his man did not turn up by ten the Inspector had decided to take a step which he could never retrace. 

Each time the telephone rang he snatched at the receiver. To-day a much wider police net had been spread. Last night the disappearance of Seward had been curiously significant, following the discovery that Mary Findon knew nothing about the murder. Now that he had not returned, Scotland Yard could but draw one conclusion. 

At three minutes to ten he sent for Trim. As the

Sergeant entered, Neville rammed his watch back in his pocket.

Not long afterwards tape machines all over London were tapping out the following message:

EX. TEL. 12.30 P. M. THE MAYFAIR MURDER. THE EXCHANGE TELEGRAPH COMPANY STATES THAT STARTLING DE- VELOPMENTS HAVE OCCURRED IN CON- NECTION WITH THIS INVESTIGATION. A WARRANT HAS BEEN ISSUED FOR THE ARREST OF WILLIAM CAYLEY SEWARD, AND SCOTLAND YARD IS TO-DAY COMB- ING LONDON FOR THE AUTHOR, WHO DISAPPEARED YESTERDAY EVENING AND HAS NOT BEEN SEEN SINCE. 

The sword had fallen!

CHAPTER XXIII: THE MURDERER 

(9.50 p.m., Thursday, September 8th.) 

ILL SEWARD'S head was spinning round and round at a terrific rate, and ached intolerably. 

He was coming up through a black tunnel of immeasurable dimensions, travelling faster than an express train. But for a time he was concerned with nothing save those revolutions.

Presently they slowed down to half speed, and finally, as his senses returned, he began to wonder what had happened.

He opened his eyes, shut them again under the impression that he was still in a world of phantasy, and tried to move, yet could not.

His brain was gradually clearing, but it was a full minute before he once more looked in front of him.

He was in a room. The light was switched on, so it was night. He was sitting in a chair-fastened tightly down, arms tied behind him. His legs, also, were pinioned. 

Standing before him, smoking a cigarette, was—

Gerrard Eltree. 

Bill was beginning to remember things now. The last event of which he had any clear recollection was raising a glass of wine to his lips. That was after 279

dinner. He and Eltree had dined here alone at this house in Hampstead. A lonely, old-fashioned place which Eltree had inherited from his dead parents. It was set far back from the road in wooded grounds. 

If you stood on the lawn and shouted your hardest, your voice might be heard by someone passing on the main thoroughfare. But Bill was not standing on the lawn. 

He gave a jerk at his imprisoned arm, then for a moment put all his strength into the effort to free himself. "It's no use struggling," Eltree's voice came, clear and cold. "What on earth have you done this for?" Bill demanded.

Eltree waited long enough to give his answer full effect. "I'm going to kill you."

The voice was passionless and hard. Eltree stood leaning one elbow on the broad mantel shelf, and flicked the ash off his cigarette into the hearth. "Are you mad, Eltree? Pull yourself together, man! 

Stop this fooling!"

The end of the man's cigarette reddened, and he inhaled deeply. "Does it look like fooling? No, Seward, you've given me a lot of trouble, and I blundered once."

Bill dragged at the fastening about his wrists until the pain of it stopped him. Eltree watched unmoved. "You can save yourself all that trouble," he said calmly. "I used fifteen yards of new thin manilla, and

THE MURDERER 

281 spent half an hour making a good job of it while you were unconscious. You walked into the trap beautifully, Seward. By God, I thought I'd killed you properly the first time, a week ago."

An ugly note crept into his voice as he spoke again, more heatedly now, flinging his cigarette into the heart of the fire. "You don't think I'd blunder a second time, do you?" 

A clock struck the hour. Ten o'clock. Bill counted each beat, wondering desperately the while how best to play for time. Eltree's eyes, filled with brooding melancholy, were fixed on him. They often had that look. In other circumstances it lent a mysterious attractiveness to him. Those eyes kept one wondering.

A good many people had found Gerrard Eltree fascinating-women especially.

Bill now vividly remembered telling Tony Parke recently that he had pulled Eltree's leg about his “moving picture eyes." "I shouldn't do that," Tony had answered. "They might look appealing-if that's the word-on the screen, but they're a sort of legacy. I knew his father slightly. Blew his brains out on the steps of Monte 

Carlo casino, and everyone thought he'd busted the old home and family plate on the tables." "But," Bill had replied, "I thought Gerrard Eltree inherited all his money from his father.” "He did. Really, the old man wasn't a gambler. He chose the casino steps to die on, knowing everyone would leap to the obvious conclusion. But actually his

suicide had nothing to do with gambling. It was because of some woman. And his eyes were exactly like those of Gerrard." 

Bill remembered, too, enquiring if there was suicide in the Eltree blood, and the answer came back to him with startling distinctness: "Something of the kind,

I suppose. The Eltrees are born lovers, only they will go to extremes."

And this was the unimaginable extreme to which the last of the Eltrees was going. "Eltree," Bill said sharply, hoping thereby to obtain mastery of an obviously unhinged mind, "they'll lock you up in a lunatic asylum if you don't stop this nonsense. Unfasten the rope at once!" "And let you have Stella?" The words were flung back like the lash of a whip. He stood with clenched hands raised, eyes blazing with hate.

Then the fury within him abated. He was again leaning with one elbow on the edge of the mantel shelf, outwardly quite normal-so like the Eltree whom everyone had known and been drawn to, that the situation seemed incredible. "I've wanted to talk about this to someone," he said. "Think, all England aching to know what really happened in that flat, and I was the only living soul who knew! Tantalizing when you want to talk in those circumstances, isn't it? But I knew I shouldn't get you if I didn't keep the secret." "There's no harm in telling me now," Bill put in with forced calmness. 

Eltree nodded.

THE MURDERER 

283 "That's very true. You will never live to repeat it.

I have been looking forward to this for days. Now you're the only person on earth to whom I can explain exactly what happened."

Seward moistened his lips. All the time he was testing the tightness and strength of the rope. Apparently his wrists and arms were definitely imprisoned. His legs, too, were bound so securely that only his feet were free. The chair was a heavy one. So far he could think of no effective plan. "I should be very interested," he said, "to know how you got into the flat that evening."

Eltree laughed a harsh sound which struck gratingly on Seward's ears. "It was quite simple. Lady Norencourt, who had the flat before you, was a friend of mine. A year ago -before you had stolen Stella from me-she lent me the flat for three weeks. Ah! You're beginning to understand now. Yes, I lost a key and had another made, never dreaming how useful it might be. Funny how two inches of metal can keep a whole country guessing! It has amused me vastly, Seward." He looked at his victim gloatingly. "Months afterwards," he went on, "I ran across the lost key in a waistcoat pocket, and nearly threw it away, but I got an idea that it might help, so I carried it about with me, loose. Never thought of that possibility, did you? Neither you nor the human blood hounds at Scotland Yard?" "No, I never thought of that," agreed Seward, still fighting for time, and wondering bitterly whether any-

one else would ever learn the truth. "You didn't let 

Peters in though?" "I suppose I did, damn him, but not on purpose.

He's caused me a lot of trouble. I went to infinite pains to finish him off properly." "He attacked you, I suppose?"

Again Eltree laughed harshly. "He never had the chance. I mistook him for you, don't you see? I had been wondering for weeks how to get you. Missed several chances. Then it got to the last night before your marriage. I meant to shoot you some time that night, but on my way to the club I thought I might have an opportunity to do it at your flat. I had just put the key in the door when I heard someone coming up the stairs, so I left the key in the lock-it stuck there somehow-and hurried on to the next landing. "Evidently it was Peters whose steps I had heard," Eltree went on. "Whether he rang the bell or not, I don't know. But there was my key in the door, and he wanted to see this 'Bunty' person, whom he fancied lived there. It makes no difference now, but I should be amused to hear the truth. For once in our lives we can both speak openly, with the certain knowledge that no harm can come of it. Was it really you he wanted to see?" "I'm sorry to disappoint you, Eltree, but I had never even heard of him before." "Oh, well, I have cursed him often enough since then for poking his nose in. When I came down to your landing again a little later I found your door open,

THE MURDERER 

285 just as Peters had left it. And I thought perhaps it was you who had entered. So I went in quietly. I listened, but couldn't hear anything because someone was thumping out a tune on a piano overhead. The door leading into the library was ajar and a man was sitting at the desk with his back to me. He was about your build and, though the light was fading, it never dawned on me that the man there could be anyone except you. "My eye fell on the kris resting on the wall a foot away. Knives are so much quieter than revolvers. It looked like a wonderful opportunity. I wanted to laugh outright. I got my hand on the kris and came nearer over the thick carpet, standing for a second with my arm raised, not caring whether he turned or not, because I knew he was doomed. "Then I plunged the knife up to the hilt into his back. "He made a queer coughing sound, and slid to the ground, lying there face downward, but I saw a moment later that he wasn't dead. He was moving, and groaning. I wasn't sure whether the wound would prove fatal, so I knelt on his back and put my fingers round his throat. My hands are very strong. I can tear a pack of cards in halves. I was very sure of myself that way. I held them tight-tight-exulting in the belief that I was finishing you off. It was a minute of the most intense happiness. "When he was still, I remember being conscious of immense relief. Did you ever wait weeks and weeks for something you were longing for all the time? Ach-

ing for? And then get it with your own unaided effort?

If so, you can imagine how I felt when I knelt there pressing on that throat. "Then I filled my pockets with one or two things lying about, to give the whole affair the appearance of a burglary, and threw them away as I hurried along to the club. "But," his elbow had slipped off the mantel shelf, and he came nearer, menacingly, "the last thing I did before I left your flat was to look at the man's face. 

And then I saw I had killed the wrong man. If I lived to be a hundred I should never forget that disappointment. "I cursed him there as he was lying dead. And all the time someone overhead was thumping out that tune. I didn't seem to notice it so much until that moment."

He pressed his hands to his ears as if to shut out the sound even now. "It was as if the pianist was mocking me. Ever since then I have heard people playing that infernal tune all over London. I took one woman by the throat when she started it on the gramophone. It drove me into a frenzy.

At the time I wanted to kill her, but I didn't. There's no harm in telling you. It was Anito." 

Seward moistened his lips. 

"It was a good idea to arrange things this way,' he said, "so that we could have plenty of time to talk about it."

Eltree's smile was twisted. "Oh, yes, we shall have plenty of time-plenty!" he

THE MURDERER 

287 said with sinister emphasis. "I'm going to kill you-but not yet. There must be at least one more guest at our little party. And then we shall all die." 

Strength oozed from Seward. For the first time he began to have some vague but terrible apprehension that swept infinitely further than any consideration for his own skin. The use of that word "all" had a ghastly significance. Eltree was mad, of course, but it was the insanity of a cold deliberate homicide who had the cunning to plan and to carry out his scheme as flawlessly as the setting and springing of this death trap in a lonely Hampstead house.

Who was the third person whom Eltree meant to die?

Seward's brain reeled. The room began to spin round again. By sheer effort of will he steadied himself, and spoke quietly. "But why need anyone die?" he asked. "You are going to die," Eltree said, "if only to give me the satisfaction of correcting that unfortunate mistake which caused me such bitter disappointment a week ago. Make no error about that. Personally I haven't any particular wish to live, but if I have to go west I prefer to do it my own way. You think nobody has guessed that I murdered Peters, don't you? 

You're wrong. Anito knows, for one. She hasn't said so, but she can't hide it from me. She's afraid." 

Anito! Bill's mind flew back to the half hour he had spent with her yesterday when, as a woman with a breaking heart, she had seemed to allow her subtlety to wither, hungering only for news of Gerrard Eltree,

the man she loved, who had not been near her for days; whereas, all the time, she must have been exercising the last ounce of her wits to pick Bill's brains for Eltree's sake. She had telephoned frantically for him to call, besides sending a note. He remembered now that she had discussed the intricacies of the murder with an intelligence and quickness of perception that had amazed him. "Anito," Eltree went on, "would cast her soul into the pit of hell for me, and when a woman loves that way she can't hide much. I must have given it away. 

When I flew at her throat for playing that damned tune perhaps she realised. I've only seen her once since then. 

Not that I wanted her forgiveness. She means nothing to me. But I did want to see how much she suspected. 

And it was easy. You know how a woman can work round to a question without being obvious? But when you know what is in her mind, she can't fool you in the same way. I tell you, Anito knows. Maybe she thinks it was a fair fight. She has been urging me to run off with her to Spain. There was another scene- 

He paused, staring blankly in front of him. 

"I can't be bothered with women of her sort," he went on. "And the day is coming very soon when our little Anito will turn on the thing she loves. She'll go clean off the deep end when it dawns on her that I meant to kill you and when she realises why. She's built that way. Had I wished to live I could have done so, perhaps, by killing her and Tony Parke." "Tony Parke!" Bill repeated. All this time he had been trying to test the power of his unfettered feet.

THE MURDERER 

289 "And why should you have designs against him?” "Because though he's not sure, he has an idea. He thinks it, but can't bring himself to believe it."

Bill now understood many things for the first time. 

Poor old Tony! He had done his best. How perplexed he must have been when he knew his pal was keeping something up his sleeve, never dreaming it could be a harmless thing like a wedding!

Eltree was still talking. "Anito dropped a hint to me about Parke-only a bare hint skilfully cloaked; but it was enough. After that I was able to see the point of some of the jocular questions he had put to me. He nearly had me cornered one night at the club while everybody was listening, rather amused, without the remotest notion that our battle of words was real. "That's why I shot him on Wednesday night. He was getting too dangerous. Thought I'd got him, too, until I read in the papers that the bullet only grazed his head. I'm sorry about that, because he's been worrying me. Sooner or later he would have got down to the truth." 

Eltree glanced at the clock. "I'm going to leave you now," he said without emotion, fastening a handkerchief over Bill's mouth. "You could shout all the night long if it amused you, because neither the maid nor Binns will be back until tomorrow night, so you have the whole place to yourself and nobody could hear you, especially as this room was fitted with double windows to keep the draughts out; but it is just possible that your voice might

reach the ears of the milkman in the morning or the boy delivering the papers. Such things as these are the minor details which could so easily be overlooked. I thought of that point while you were having your soup to-night. In fact I was thinking out details all the time we were at dinner. You yourself gave me an idea, which accounts for your being left alive until to-morrow. Perhaps, if the hours seem to drag, it may interest you to guess what I mean."

Eltree turned off the electric light, and then Seward was left straining his ears. He heard the switch in the hall clicked off, followed some time later by the distant banging of a door. It was after half past ten at night. He was alone.

The effects of the drug with which Eltree had doped the wine had gone now. His brain was crystal clear, and but for a growing numbness in his arms, due to being pinioned in one position so long, there was nothing wrong with him.

He sat perfectly still for a space, conserving his strength and reviewing the whole diabolical position. 

Sweat began to pour down his face when his thoughts impinged upon the one thing which struck terror into his heart. They were "all" to die, according to Eltree; and the madman's programme was not difficult to visualise. Oh, Seward reflected with intense. bitterness, if only he had not spoken of his plans for to-morrow! 

Now he had the agony of knowing that the final catastrophe would have been averted but for his own crass stupidity. "Plenty of time," Eltree had said. Evidently he did

THE MURDERER 

291 not intend to return until morning. Nine or ten hours hence, at least. 

Seward was in darkness save for the glow of a dying fire in the hearth. By exerting all his strength he was able to tilt the heavy chair back a bare fraction of an inch, and then it sank to all four legs again. 

A dozen times he tried this experiment before admitting to himself that however he varied the movement it could serve no useful purpose. The chair finally remained in exactly the same position; and, even while his strength remained, he could not shift it from the centre of the room. Until now he had been buoyed up by a wild hope of rolling over and letting the fire burn through the rope, or of getting near the wall somehow and using friction to cut through it. To stand any chance of success that way, however, he must have more play about the legs and ankles.

A clock struck eleven as he began a slow, laborious process of attempting to stretch and slacken the rope around his ankles and legs. It seemed that he struggled in between brief rests, for hours, but when the clock next struck it was midnight, and the chafing had caused his legs to swell, for the binding bit closer into his flesh now. There was an intolerable ache, also, in his shoulders, and his hands had become nerveless. The night was slipping past and he had achieved nothing.

Worse, he was becoming exhausted with the tremendous effort.

Deliberately he sat still again for some time, in utter darkness now, for the fire had vanished. Physically he was checked. His brain raced over the things

that might happen in this room with the return of day, and then a desperate frenzy overcame him.

Frantically dragging at his wrists, breathing in jerking gasps between clenched teeth, he fought and wrestled with fifteen yards of stout, relentless manilla rope until his head suddenly fell forward in utter exhaustion and he felt it begin to spin round once more, slowly at first, but gradually gathering impetus. 

For an immeasurable space he was hurtling through inconceivable distance, and he had lost all count of earthly hours when his senses returned. Ten thousand active demons were prodding white hot needles into his shoulders and arms, but he remembered even then that one thing only remained-to try to bite through the handkerchief across his mouth.

He drew his breath in hard, hoping to touch the fabric with his teeth. It was taut. But no other expedient was left. He wet the thing with his tongue, tightened and relaxed the muscles of his face, and continued to draw at the handkerchief desperately with his breath, until the whole world began to sink under him. For a few seconds, with a last effort of will, he writhed in his bondage, and then sagged.

CHAPTER XXIV:  

(Noon, Friday, September 9th.)

INKER BROWN's long and rather dull vigil ended with a jerk. These last few days a good many people had stopped in front of Regent House and gaped at the flats there, with that morbid interest which animates a curiously large section of the public. 

Sometimes they pointed, and Tinker, feeling somewhat fed up, watched them walk away. From the first he had had no faith in this plan of Mr. Parke's; had, indeed, expressed his views clearly to his employer more than once, but had been told to carry on. 

That was how he came to be leaning against the railings in Grasmere Street on this particular Friday morning, masticating a piece of chewing gum that had long lost its flavour and taking periodical glimpses at a thrilling gem of literature whch dealt with cowboys and folded conveniently to slip into the pocket. 

There were not many pedestrians about just now. 

Ten minutes ago a fat old woman had paused in front of the all-important building, sniffed, and passed on. 

The caretaker at Regent House had slipped out for his pint at the corner pub, as was his habit every few hours. The postman was working his way up the 293

street, and Tinker knew it was about time to get his mid-day meal. Strolling along as though he was waiting for someone, there loitered a man with a walrus moustache, who evidently had much patience, for he had been kicking his heels there for over an hour. 

In accordance with precise instructions Tinker had run the rule over him, and dismissed the possibility of the Walrus being the murderer. For one thing he wasn't quite tall enough, and for another he had yawned several times. Incidentally he had looked at Tinker somewhat inquisitively on several occasions. It didn't seem probable to the lad that the murderer would hang around the scene of his crime and yawn. 

Tinker shifted his weight from one leg to the other, and decided to finish that chapter, where a cowboy was hanging over a precipice, before going to his usual coffee shop for dinner. 

A man came walking quickly past Regent House and glanced up at the flat which all England was talking about. He even paused perceptibly opposite the entrance a circumstance which Tinker observed over the top of the page, but dismissed from his mind. 

Then Tinker looked at him again as he came nearer, and recognised the face-a friend of Mr. Parke's. Tinker knew, because two days ago he had taken a note to this Mr. Eltree, at the Magpie Club, with instructions to wait for an answer. And Mr. Eltree had kept the boy waiting ten minutes before he came to the club door and gave him a note to take back.

The boy spat out his chewing gum, weary of it, and walked off to his coffee house, reading, but looked

back mechanically just as Eltree stooped near the pillar box and stared through the railings at the very place where the murderer had thrown that gold cigarette case. 

Tinker's mouth opened, and his fingers wandered to his head where they scratched. Eltree was walking on now, exactly where the murderer had walked. At the corner, just as the murderer had done, he paused and looked over his shoulder, then passed out of sight. 

The boy was conscious of a curious tingling in his live young frame. 

Nobody but Tinker, Mr. Parke, and Detective Inspector Neville knew exactly where that cigarette case was thrown—and Mr. Eltree had deliberately looked in the right place to see if it was still there. 

The boy continued to scratch his head. Not once, but a dozen times, Mr. Parke had impressed one thing upon him in his serious way. "It's a well-known fact, Tinker," Mr. Parke had said, "that murderers are often drawn back to the scene of their crime. There's only one chance in ten thousand that you might spot him, but, if you do, follow him wherever he goes, and then get hold of me like a streak of lightning." 

All thought of dinner was leaving the boy. He had stood there while Mr. Eltree walked away, and maybe he might even have assumed that, being a friend of Mr. Parke's, Mr. Eltree had been let into the secret. 

But Tinker's virile young brain was working keenly. At this moment he could picture that other scene of eight days ago. There was no distinctive peculiarity in El-

tree's walk, yet it was similar to that of the man who threw away the cigarette case.

When Eltree paused and looked back over his shoulder at the corner exactly as the murderer had done, Tinker experienced a thrill of excitement much more vivid than anything to be got from the cowboy book. 

Eltree had done it in exactly the same way as the murderer did, days and days ago, and in one second Tinker's memory had brought back the murderer's movements now so faithfully reproduced. 

Of a sudden he started to run to the corner, the puzzled gaze of the Walrus upon him, but Eltree was not in sight. Tinker stood, a little shaky with emotion, unable to believe that the thing he had been looking for all this time had happened. He didn't see how a friend of Mr. Parke's could have committed the murder. That bothered him. But as he stood there the conviction was growing that he should at least obey orders "like a streak of lightning."

So he ran, and a while later arrived breathless at the door of Tony Parke's flat. 

Answering the bell, Riggs glanced down his nose at the panting boy. "Is the guv'nor in? Tell 'im I want 'im, quick.” "Mr. Parke is hout," came from Riggs with intensified dignity. "Get 'im quick-on the 'phone. Tell 'im—" inspiration came to the boy, "tell 'im I've seen the murderer."

Riggs frowned, then awoke to the fact that there was something afoot which transcended his dignity. "Seen him?" he asked.

"For Gawd's sake, get Mr. Parke quick," commanded the boy, now a living little fury. "Come in and I'll see if he's at the office," said Riggs. “O-00, let me have the telephone," Tinker insisted; and a few moments later somebody at the Monitor office was answering him. "Mr. Parke has gone out, but he may be back soon." "Then find 'im," shouted Tinker. "Get 'old of 'im somehow. Tell 'im this is Tinker Brown that wants 'im. 

Tell 'im," the boy remembered his talisman which had already worked magically upon Riggs, "tell 'im I've seen the murderer." A pause. "Never you mind, see? ... Yes, just tell 'im that . . . I'm at his house waiting for 'im to ring me up, see?"

It was eight minutes afterwards that Tony came back into the office. As he entered the reporters' room somebody held out to him a long thin strip of tape on which a poignant message had been tapped out by the buzzing machine in the corner.

Still staring at the tape which told of the warrant for Bill's arrest, Tony Parke sank into a chair. 

The head messenger was speaking to him. Tony waved the fellow aside, but the messenger was insistent. "He gave the name of Tinker Brown, sir," the voice at Tony's elbow went on. "He's waiting for you to ring him up. Says he's seen the murderer!" "Eh! Eh! What's that?" Tony was electrified. A few seconds later he was sitting with his ear pressed to a telephone receiver, breathing quickly.

He heard Tinker's excited voice, and presently the receiver slid from his fingers and lay unheeded on the table. "Eltree!" he muttered. "But I don't understand!"

Then singling out one memory after another, he knew that he did now come within measurable distance of understanding.

He glanced at his watch. One o'clock. At that moment he was due at a restaurant where he and Stella and Bill Seward were to have had lunch. Heaven only knew where Bill was. Twice already that morning 

Tony had spoken to Stella, and each time she had adhered firmly to the belief that Bill would be there. 

Her faith in him was entirely unshaken. She would be waiting there at the restaurant now, unaware of the fact that a warrant had been issued for Bill's arrest. 

Bill had never failed her in anything, and she would still adhere to her belief that he would turn up, with some entirely satisfactory explanation as to his disappearance over-night. 

Hearing and seeing nothing, Tony stumbled out of the office. Tinker Brown's words were ringing in his ears. Hot, excited words which made the incredible seem almost possible. Tony had never actually suspected Gerrard Eltree, although odd circumstances that seemed to link Eltree with the crime had cropped up, just as other things had seemed to link several people with the murder in flat thirteen.

Even now Tony was bewildered. Could the boy have made some mistake? In his wild enthusiasm over the

wire he had gone so far as to say that he was "sure now it was the same feller."

And yet several of those damnably scattered mosaics, which had kept Tony awake night after night because they did not quite fit anywhere, seemed as if they were falling into place.

Eltree only arrived at the Magpie Club a minute or two before eight on the night of the murder. Since then 

Tony had once asked him half jestingly whether, if faced with the prospect, he could explain his movements after 7.45 p. m., on that particular night; and 

Eltree had momentarily been nonplussed. 

Other little points drifted back to Tony's memory. 

According to Tinker Brown, the man who threw away the cigarette case went to the corner of Grasmere

Street and turned to the left. That was the way Eltree would have to go in making for the Magpie Club. It was a minor point in itself, not worthy of consideration, but taken in conjunction with other things its importance grew. For instance, had the murderer turned to the right at that corner, he could not have been making for the Magpie Club.

Again, when Tony had telephoned to the Club cancelling the ill-fated dinner party, he spoke to Eltree and said clearly, "There's a man dead here at Seward's flat." Eltree had seemed slightly confused, and his answer had been, "What! Seward dead!"

At the time it had seemed merely a misunderstanding, but regarded in this fresh light it was sinister. 

The dead man was of about the same build as Seward.

In the failing light the one might have been taken for the other.

Tony could see now that Lo Chang never had anything to do with the crime. In a groping fashion, he began to fumble with the problem of Anito, and that gramophone record which she broke because she hated it so. With all the passion in her nature, a legacy from her Spanish ancestry, the girl loved Gerrard Eltree, but Tony could not understand her intense hatred of the tune, unless she knew the truth.

He stood on the busy footpath for a few moments, aware that he must go to Detective Inspector Neville.

But on his way he must call at the restaurant, to see 

Stella first. If Seward hadn't arrived she would be distracted. He jumped into a taxi.

Their favourite corner table in the restaurant had been reserved for the three of them. Tony was ten minutes late when he made his way through the crowded room. With a sense of annoyance he noticed that a party of strangers had already begun luncheon there. 

He caught the eye of the French waiter who always served them, and beckoned. "This table is engaged," he said. 

The waiter remembered. "Yes, sare. The lady she wait by herself until three, four minutes ago. Then she leave in a hurry, called away by a gentleman.' 

The waiter was busy and impatient to be about his business. Tony drew money from his pocket. The man knew Bill by sight.

"You remember the friend who dines here with us sometimes? Did he come for the lady?" "No, sare." The waiter was sure of that. "I hear the gentleman tell the lady her friend have an accident at Hampstead. She go leave with him quick."

Again the waiter was itching to be on the move. "Half a moment! Did you notice this gentleman who came? Can you describe him?" 

The waiter spoke a dozen quick words, and then bolted. "Oh, my God!" broke from Tony as he rushed out of the restaurant. The waiter, long trained to be keenly observant, had delivered a lightning sketch of Gerrard Eltree. Eltree, who had murdered one man, and who, it was now obvious, had tried to kill Tony! Hampstead!

Of course Stella would go if she thought Bill had had an accident. The madness that lurked in the Eltrees had come out, and Stella was in the power of a homicidal maniac! The chances were that he had taken her to Marsden Lodge, his home at Hampstead. Was Bill there? What had already happened?

In the street he jumped on to the running board of a taxi, driven by an alert-looking chauffeur. "Double fare and a pound extra if you drive like the devil to Hampstead!" he said. "Make for Jack 

Straw's Castle. Turn off at Bearstead Road. I want 

Marsden Lodge. It's a matter of life and death! I'll be responsible for all fines. Hurry up!"

The door slammed and the cab wound in and out of the traffic at a crazy speed, tearing through short cuts and hurtling along main roads.

Of a sudden Tony realised that in his desperate hurry he had omitted a vital thing. Until now his only thought had been to reach the place where he imagined Stella and Bill might be. He drew a notebook out of his pocket, and as the taxi swayed and jolted he scribbled a message, then thrust his head out with instructions to the driver.

The cab skidded to a standstill near a policeman, and Tony jumped out, thrusting money and the hastily pencilled message into the officer's hands. "Get on to Scotland Yard quick!" he said. "Ask for

Detective Inspector Neville and read this out to him. 

He'll understand. It's urgent-urgent! Here's my card. 

Don't waste a second." "Right, sir," said the policeman, pausing only to take the number of the taxi-cab, and then hurried away. 

Before long the taxi driver got on to less crowded roads and opened the throttle. He narrowly escaped hitting a car at a side turning, and half a mile further on swerved violently to avoid a sedate old gentleman who had incautiously stepped off the footpath. 

Tony kept looking at his watch. He didn't care what happened to the taxi so long as it was not wrecked before he reached his destination. But, half way down 

Bearstead Road, a delivery van came round a corner on the wrong side, and there was a crash. 

Leaping out unhurt, Tony thrust a card into the chauffeur's hand. Then ran. There was a distance of three hundred yards still to cover before he reached the entrance of the drive up to Marsden Lodge; and on turning the bend close to the house itself a sound

struck his ears that made him leap forward faster.

It was the muffled scream of a terrified woman. 

First, without much hope, he hurled himself at the main door, but could not get in there. Rushing round the side of the house he found a low window, and, putting his foot through the glass, unfastened the latch and crawled in.

Again that scream spurred him on. He tore up a flight of stairs, emerging into the hall, where he heard voices in the dining room, the door of which was open. 

Eltree, it was almost certain, would be armed, but 

Tony dashed into the room. 

Bill Seward was huddled in one chair; and Stella, her arms and legs fettered, was in another.

In the middle of the room, brandishing a revolver, stood Eltree, the last vestige of sanity gone. The lust to kill was upon him. This was the grand finale up to which his unhinged brain had been working. 

His finger was already tight on the trigger, the revolver pointing at Seward, as Tony darted in. But the madman, hearing footsteps, spun around, and the revolver cracked.

Tony's impetus was checked. He swayed, and with a loud roar of laughter Eltree fired at him again as he was in the act of falling. "So I've got you after all, my newspaper weasel!" he cried exultingly. "An unexpected pleasure! You came for it in the nick of time." Then, turning from the sprawling figure on the floor, he addressed Seward. "I knew it was ordained that three of us were to die here to-day, but a fourth makes the thing more

complete. We cannot escape our fate, Seward. When 

I knifed the wrong man a week yesterday you little thought-"

His voice was high pitched. His hand holding the revolver was again raised, and in another second Stella would have screamed, but something happened which made her racing heart nearly stop.

Tony Parke was moving. Unnoticed by Eltree, who now fancied he had the field to himself, the wounded man crept forward behind the raving maniac. 

Four feet more to go-three-two-one!

Then, bracing himself firmly for a supreme effort, Tony gripped Eltree by both ankles and, using his head as a battering ram, thrust the madman forward.

Eltree's finger dragged at the trigger as he fell, and the bullet buried itself in the floor. He himself crashed to the ground, striking his head against the edge of the chair, and the revolver slid along the carpet out of reach.

Eltree was only momentarily stunned, but before he recovered Tony had crawled on to the man's back and was holding his arms down. Tony's strength was ebbing, for one bullet had passed through the fleshy part of his arm and the other had hit him in the shoulder, but he turned a triumphant face to the girl. "It's all right now, Stella. I've got him, but-but you'd better see if you can get free somehow." 

The girl was struggling wildly in her bonds. "I can't Tony-I can't. That brute has fastened me up tightly."

Bill Seward, barely conscious now, his limbs swollen

until the manilla ropes cut into his flesh, raised his head feebly. "Fine work, Tony. Stella, didn't I tell you he was a good egg?" "You're not hurt, Bill?" came from Tony. "Hurt! Lord, no!" came from Bill in a faint voice. "I could stick weeks of this now-now that I know 

Stella's all right."

But Eltree was beginning to struggle, and Tony was weakening rapidly. He could feel something warm running down his side. As he exerted his muscles to keep the man's arms down on the floor the pain was excruciating, and for a moment he had a horrible fear that he was going to faint. He gritted his teeth. 

Bill was saying something—the sort of thing one of Bill's breed would say at such a moment—and vaguely he heard Stella's voice also. But Tony was going deaf. The room was swaying. The man underneath him was striving to squirm loose. He seemed to be aware that he could win yet. He was trying to turn over on his side. 

Once he almost succeeded, but fell back as the weight above him gradually became overpowering. He lay still for a full minute, recovering his strength, and then steadily began to draw his knees upward, when a loud banging upon a door reverberated through the house. 

Eltree heard it. His hand was within five feet of the revolver. Tony's grip on his arm was weakening. Eltree gave a jerk and extended one hand. His fingers were nearer the weapon.

The banging on the door had ceased. Eltree was

squirming nearer and nearer the revolver, and Tony knew his own last reserve of strength was nearing an end, when Bill Seward's voice reached his ears. "Good egg, Tony! Stick it!"

The words acted like a whip on Tony's will power. 

He dared not change his grip, but for a few seconds it tightened—and then several men burst into the room. 

Tony rolled over. Like a frenzied animal Eltree leaped to his feet and darted toward the glistening weapon. He snatched it from the floor and, twisting round, pointed the thing at Bill Seward. But as he pressed the trigger his arm was knocked up and the bullet buried itself in the ceiling.

A gondola drifted along the Grand Canal in Venice, rocking slightly under the strong sweep of the oar. 

The night was ablaze with stars. Over the water there drifted music which the gondolier took up in his rich baritone, as is the way of gondoliers. 

Nestling in the cushions, held close to the man she loved, a girl sighed and shuddered. He understood, but did not speak, though his arm only tightened, comfortingly. "Bill," she breathed, "is all this really true? Are we here, alive, loving one another? Is that dreadful nightmare never coming back?” "No, darling," he answered gently. "It's finished with for ever."

The gondola drifted on for a space. Then: "Bill, you're sure Tony's getting better?"

"Yes, he's all right now. The best egg that ever was!" "I kissed him, Bill. You didn't mind, did you?" "You kissed us both, dear, laughing and crying all at the same time," said Bill. "I'm not sure whether you didn't kiss Neville too, only just then I gave out."
